L29: I. Executive Summary
L30: 1. Summary of Regulatory Action
L31: This new drug application (NDA) for atogepant was submitted by AbbVie, Inc. (the Applicant). Atogepant is an oral small molecule calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP) receptor antagonist, developed for the preventive treatment of episodic migraine (EM) in adults.
L32: This NDA received a priority review designation because of the Applicant's use of a Material Threat Countermeasure Priority Review Voucher. The NDA was reviewed by a multidisciplinary review team (Clinical, Clinical Infectious Disease, Clinical Hepatology, Clinical Pharmacology, Clinical Outcomes Assessment Team, Pharmacology/Toxicology, Chemistry Manufacturing and Controls, and Statistics) that did not identify any issues that preclude approval. I, the signatory authority, agree that the benefit-risk assessment supports approval.
L33: Migraine is a primary headache disorder characterized by recurrent headaches that are moderate to severe, accompanied by various associated symptoms. Chronic migraine (CM) is defined as 15 or more headache days per month, with features of migraine headache on \geq 8 days per month. Patients with 14 or fewer migraine headache days per month are defined as having EM.
L34: The Applicant submitted the results of two adequate and well-controlled trials (Study CGP-MD-01 and Study 3101-301-002) to support the efficacy of atogepant for the preventive treatment of EM in adults. Both trials used a well-validated and clinically meaningful primary efficacy endpoint: the change from baseline in the mean number of monthly migraine days. Study CGP-MD-01 evaluated daily doses of 10 mg, 30 mg, and 60 mg, and twice-daily doses of 30 mg and 60 mg. The primary endpoint was met in all dosage groups. Given the relatively flat dose-response relationship observed in this trial, only the daily doses of 10 mg, 30 mg, and 60 mg were evaluated in Study 3101-301-002. In that study, all atogepant dosage groups had significantly greater reductions in the mean number of monthly migraine days than the placebo group (0.7 to 1.7 fewer monthly migraine days). The treatment effect size on that endpoint is similar to that of other products approved for the preventive treatment of migraine (EM and/or CM). In Study 3101-301-002, significant results were also observed for several secondary endpoints, including reduction in monthly medication use, and patient-reported clinical outcome assessment tools. The results of the secondary efficacy endpoints in Study CGP-MD-01 all numerically favored atogepant.
L35: Atogepant has a favorable safety profile. In the controlled trials, there were no deaths and no serious adverse events with a clear association with atogepant. Common adverse events that occurred at an incidence of at least 2% of patients on atogepant and greater than placebo were nausea, constipation, fatigue, and decreased appetite. Safety signals of interest that were evaluated during the review were vascular ischemic abnormalities, hypertension, and serious gastrointestinal events, based on known pharmacodynamic effects of CGRP and subsequent theoretical risks with CGRP antagonism. No serious vascular events or serious gastrointestinal effects related to the use of atogepant were identified. Hepatotoxicity was also considered a safety signal of interest as the development programs for two older-generation oral CGRP antagonists were discontinued because of cases of serious liver injury. The rate of transaminase
L36: elevations over 3 times the upper limit of normal was similar between atogepant and placebo (1% for atogepant vs. 1.8% for placebo). However, there were a few cases with elevations over three times the upper limit of normal assessed as having a probable or possible relationship with atogepant treatment. All cases resolved after discontinuation of atogepant. Routine monitoring of liver enzymes is not recommended. The risks of atogepant can be mitigated through labeling and pharmacovigilance.
L37: This application triggers the Pediatric Research Equity Act; therefore, pediatric postmarketing requirements to study safety and efficacy of this product for the preventive treatment of EM in patients age 6 to less than 18 years of age will be issued. In addition, postmarketing requirements for a pregnancy registry study, a pregnancy outcomes study, a nonclinical dose-range finding study, and a 2-year nonclinical carcinogenicity study will be issued.
L38: Based on the review of all available efficacy and safety data for atogepant, the benefits outweigh the risks for the preventive treatment of EM in adults. This product provides for a second oral CGRP receptor antagonist for this indication.
L39: For detailed information supporting the basis for the benefit-risk assessment, please refer to the details in this integrated assessment document.
L40: 2. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L41: 2.1. Benefit-Risk Framework
L42: Table 2. Benefit-Risk Framework
L43: DimensionEvidence and UncertaintiesConclusions and Reasons



Analysis of condition

Migraine is a primary headache disorder characterized by recurrent headaches that are moderate to severe, accompanied by associated symptoms of nausea, vomiting, and light and/or sound sensitivity. The typical headache of migraine is throbbing, unilateral, and aggravated by motion, but bilateral and nonthrobbing headaches are also commonly reported. Migraine attacks typically last 4 to 72 hours when untreated. About one third of people with migraine experience transient neurological symptoms before and/or during an attack, referred to as migraine aura. Migraine is more prevalent in females than males and peaks in the fourth decade of life.
Migraine patients can be categorized as having either episodic or chronic migraine. Episodic migraine refers to patients with a diagnosis of migraine with or without aura as per the International Classification of Headache Disorders (ICHD)-3 criteria and who have 14 or fewer headache days per month. The ICHD-3 defines chronic migraine as patients with migraine that for at least 3 consecutive months experience 15 or greater headache days a month, in which at least 8 of those monthly headaches have the features of migraine.


Episodic migraine is a common and debilitating disease that can significantly impact the quality of life.
L44: DimensionEvidence and UncertaintiesConclusions and Reasons



Current treatment options

There are currently many approved therapies for the preventive treatment of migraine, as well as many others that are used off-label.
Recently, several therapies that act on the calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP) pathway were approved for the preventive treatment of migraine (indicated for both patients with episodic migraine and chronic migraine). These include the injectable CGRP antagonist monoclonal antibodies, including erenumab, fremanezumab, galcanezumab, and eptinezumab. Erenumab and galcanezumab are administered as subcutaneous injections monthly; fremanezumab can be administered either monthly or quarterly; eptinezumab is administered intravenously quarterly. Rimegepant, a small molecule CGRP receptor antagonist, was also approved recently for the preventive treatment of episodic migraine (this medication is also indicated for the acute treatment of migraine).
Other treatments approved for the preventive treatment of migraine include onabotulinumtoxinA (approved only for the preventive treatment of chronic migraine), and several oral drugs: propranolol, timolol, divalproex/sodium valproate, and topiramate. Therapies for the preventive treatment of migraine that were approved prior to 2010 have an indication that does not distinguish between episodic or chronic migraine, as the classification into episodic or chronic migraine only appeared a few years before. Most of the drugs currently marketed for the preventive treatment of migraine demonstrate a reduction of monthly migraine days of 0.7 to 2.0 days.


Other drugs within the CGRP antagonist class have been approved for the preventive treatment of migraine. Most are injectable (erenumab, fremanezumab, galcanezumab and eptinezumab). Another oral product, rimegepant, was initially approved for the acute treatment of migraine, and recently approved for the preventive treatment of episodic migraine.
Atogepant will be the second oral CGRP receptor antagonist marketed for the preventive treatment of episodic migraine. Rimegepant is dosed every other day based on how the drug was studied in clinical trials and has a postmarketing commitment to study daily dosing, as such a regimen would be consistent with the drugs' pharmacokinetic properties.


Benefit

The efficacy of atogepant for the preventive treatment of episodic migraine was demonstrated in two adequate and well-controlled trials, Study CGP-MD-01 and Study 3101-301-002. Both studies used a well-validated and clinically meaningful endpoint to establish efficacy, a reduction in the number of monthly migraine days (MMD). Study CGP-MD-01 assessed five dosing regimens, daily doses of 10 mg, 30 mg, and 60 mg and twice daily doses of 30 mg and 60 mg, as compared to placebo. All doses had statistically significantly greater reductions from baseline in MMD compared to placebo: 1.2, 1.0, 0.7, 1.4, and 1.3 for atogepant groups of 10 mg daily (QD), 30 mg QD, 60 mg QD, 30 mg twice daily (BID), and 60 mg BID groups, respectively. Numerically, the twice-daily dosing regimens performed slightly better than the daily dosing; however, only daily doses were carried forward into the subsequent trial based on overall considerations, including the practicability of twice-daily dosing in the setting of limited additional benefit. Study 3101-301-002 studied three dosing regimens, daily doses of 10 mg, 30 mg, and 60 mg, as compared to placebo. All doses demonstrated efficacy on the primary endpoint with a placebo corrected reduction from baseline in MMD of 1.2, 1.4, and 1.7 for the


Atogepant at daily doses of 10 mg, 30 mg and 60 mg demonstrated efficacy for the preventive treatment of episodic migraine in adults.
Other marketed drugs for this indication also have generally modest effect sizes, typically reducing the number of monthly migraine/headache days by 1 to 2 days; like the other marketed products, atogepant is not likely to render patients migraine-free.
L45: DimensionEvidence and UncertaintiesConclusions and Reasons

In Studies CGP-MD-01 and 3101-201-0002, efficacy was supported by the demonstration of treatment-related reductions in mean monthly headache days. In Study 3101-301-002, efficacy was also supported by the demonstration of treatment-related benefits in the reduction in mean monthly acute medication use, increase in the 50% responder rate, and effects on patient-reported outcome measures evaluating the impact of migraine on daily functioning: the Migraine-Specific Quality of Life Questionnaire (MSQ) Role Restrictive Function and the the Activity Impairment in Migraine Diary (AIM-D) Physical Function domain score and AIM-D Physical Impairment domain score.



Risk and risk management

No serious safety signals were identified in this review.
There were no deaths in the controlled clinical trials.
There were two deaths in the long-term safety study, related to a group A streptococcus infection and homicide, respectively. Neither was assessed as being causally related to atogepant.
There were no serious adverse events that were clearly associated with the use of atogepant. The most common adverse reactions in the controlled clinical efficacy trials in atogepant treated patients, occurring at greater than 2%, were constipation (6%), nausea (5 to 9%), fatigue/somnolence (4 to 6%), and decreased appetite (1 to 2%).
Hepatic risk was assessed as a safety signal of special interest due to previous experience for earlier oral CGRP receptor antagonists. Overall, the rate of transaminase elevations over 3X ULN were similar between atogepant treatment arms and placebo (1.0% versus 1.8%). There were cases of serum transaminase elevations (alanine aminotransferase 7 to 10X upper limit of normal [ULN] and aspartate aminotransferase 3 to 7X ULN) that were associated with atogepant use in the controlled clinical trials; however, all cases were asymptomatic and resolved after discontinuation of atogepant. Routine monitoring of liver enzymes is not recommended.
Atogepant-treated patients experienced weight loss at a greater frequency than patients treated with placebo. The proportion of patients with a weight decrease greater than or equal to 7% at any point was 2.8% for placebo, 3.8% for atogepant 10 mg, 3.2% for atogepant 30 mg, and 4.9% for atogepant 60 mg QD.
As CGRP is thought to enhance compensatory vasodilatation in the setting of ischemia; there is a theoretical risk that CGRP inhibition may lead to increased


There were no significant safety findings that would preclude the approval of atogepant. Adequate labeling and enhanced pharmacovigilance are sufficient to address the identified safety issues.
No serious cases of hepatic injury are associated with atogepant; however, a possible association of atopant treatment with a liver enzyme elevation will be described in labeling.
The data submitted with this application do not support the need to include CV restrictions in labeling; however, these data are also insufficient to definitively establish the CV safety of atogepant.
Because the risk of adverse outcomes in pregnancy has not been characterized, and because atogepant will be used in women of childbearing potential, a pregnancy registry and a pregnancy outcome study will

Other uncertainties

• The risk of adverse outcomes in pregnancy has not been characterized.
• Safety and efficacy in pediatric migraine patients has not been established.
• The carcinogenicity of this product in nonclinical studies was not fully characterized.

Since safety and efficacy of atogepant in pediatric migraine patients has not been established, studies to evaluate atogepant for the preventive treatment of episodic migraine in patients 6 to less than 18 years of age will be required under the Pediatric Research Equity Act.
Since the carcinogenicity of this product in nonclinical studies was not fully characterized, a dose-range finding nonclinical study and a 2-year nonclinical carcinogenicity study will be issued as PMRs.
Enhanced pharmacovigilance with periodic evaluation of hepatotoxicity, cardiovascular events, hypertensive events, cerebrovascular events, and serious gastrointestinal events will be requested.

L48: 2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
L49: Migraine is a condition that may result in significant morbidity and greatly impacts daily function. Although there are a number of drugs approved for the preventive treatment of episodic migraine in adults, additional treatment options remain important. Atogepant is only the second oral CGRP antagonist to seek an indication for the preventive treatment of episodic migraine in adults.
L50: The Applicant has clearly demonstrated the efficacy of atogepant for the proposed indication in two 12-week placebo-controlled, double-blind, parallel-arm clinical trials that enrolled adult migraine patients with 14 or fewer headache days per month. Daily atogepant doses of 10 mg, 30 mg, and 60 mg, resulted in statistically significant reductions in the number of mean monthly migraine days from baseline, relative to placebo (range 0.7 to 1.7 days). The treatment effect size is similar to that of other drugs approved for
L51: the preventive treatment of migraine. Efficacy is further supported by significant treatment-related reductions, compared to placebo, in the number of mean monthly headache days from baseline, reductions in acute medication use, increases in the number of patients having 50% or greater reductions in their baseline migraine frequency, and by the results of patient-reported outcome measures assessing the impact of migraine on daily functioning.
L52: The safety database for atogepant was adequate for the proposed dosing regimens and the intended patient population. Overall, atogepant has a favorable safety profile, and the safety findings can be adequately addressed in labeling and by routine and enhanced pharmacovigilance. Common adverse reactions associated with treatment include constipation, nausea, fatigue, and decreased appetite. Enhanced pharmacovigilance will be requested for serious gastrointestinal toxicity, based on a signal for such events in the CGRP antagonist class, along with a theoretical risk based on the presence of CGRP receptors in the gastrointestinal system. There was no evidence to suggest the presence of an ischemic risk with atogepant, also a theoretical concern with CGRP antagonists; however, enhanced pharmacovigilance for such events will be requested, consistent with the approach taken to all approved drugs of this class. Although there was no signal of serious hepatotoxicity, increases in liver function tests associated with atogepant treatment have been observed and will be described in labeling. Enhanced pharmacovigilance will also be requested for liver injury events.
L53: Based upon review of all available efficacy and safety data, the benefits of atogepant clearly outweigh the risks for the preventive treatment of episodic migraine in adults.
L54: II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
L55: 3. Introduction
L56: Atogepant is an orally-administered calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP) receptor antagonist. CGRP is widely distributed in the central and peripheral nervous systems. Evidence suggests that CGRP plays an important role in migraine attacks, as CGRP levels have been demonstrated to increase during an acute migraine attack, and infusions of CGRP have been demonstrated to induce migraine-like headaches in patients with a diagnosis of migraine (Goadsby and Edvinsson 1993; Lassen et al. 2002).
L57: Several agents that inhibit the CGRP pathway have been approved for the treatment of migraine in adults. Four monoclonal antibodies (erenumab, galcanezumab, fremanezumab, and eptinezumab) that target CGRP (the peptide), or its receptor, have been approved for the preventive treatment of migraine in adults (comprising both chronic migraine [CM] and episodic migraine [EM]). These drugs are administered either subcutaneously or intravenously. Two small molecules that are orally administered have also been approved for the acute treatment of migraine in adults (ubrogepant and rimegepant) and, most recently, rimegepant was also approved for the preventive treatment of EM in adults.
L58: The approach to the evaluation of efficacy in development programs for the preventive treatment of migraine is well established and includes a comparison between treatment and placebo in the reduction in the mean monthly migraine days and mean monthly headache days in a double-blind treatment period from baseline. Several additional efficacy endpoints are also generally evaluated (e.g., the number of patients with at least a 50% reduction in mean monthly migraine days and the use of monthly acute migraine treatments). Development programs for the preventive treatment of migraine have also sought to evaluate the impact of migraine on patients' daily functioning through the use of existing and novel patient-reported outcome measures.
L59: CGRP has been described in the literature as a microvascular vasodilator that has a role in compensatory vasodilatation in the setting of ischemia. (b) (5)
L60: Development programs for several small molecule CGRP receptor antagonists were previously discontinued because of an association with hepatotoxicity; however, the two recently approved such products, ubrogepant and rimegepant, did not have any indication of hepatotoxicity in their respective development programs. Despite the apparent safety of these more contemporary
L61: agents in this regard, hepatotoxicity remains a safety signal of interest in the small molecule CGRP receptor antagonist development programs.
L62: The review team identified six key review issues listed below; three that have a significant impact on the overall determination of benefit of atogepant and three that contribute to the understanding of safety to inform the benefit-risk profile and labeling.
L63: 3.1. Review Issue List
L64: 3.1.1. Key Review Issues Relevant to Evaluation of Benefit
L65: 3.1.1.1. The Adequacy of Study CGP-MD-01 to Support an Efficacy Claim
L66: Assess if Study CGP-MD-01, a Phase 2/3 dose-finding trial, can serve as one of the two adequate and well-controlled efficacy studies needed to support approval.
L67: 3.1.1.2. Should Atogepant 10 mg Daily Dose Be Recommended for the General Population?
L68: Assess if the 10 mg daily dose, in addition to the 30 mg and 60 mg daily doses, should be recommended for the general population (and not just specific populations as the Applicant proposes).
L69: 3.1.1.3. Are the AIM-D and MSQ v2.1 RFR Appropriate for Inclusion in Labeling?
L70: Evaluate if the Activity Impairment in Migraine Diary (AIM-D) Performance of Daily Activities (PDA) domain score, the AIM-D Physical Impairment (PI) domain score, and the Migraine-Specific Quality of Life Questionnaire version 2.1 (MSQ v2.1) Role Function-Restrictive (RFR) domain score results should be described in labeling.
L71: 3.1.2. Key Review Issues Relevant to Evaluation of Risk
L72: 3.1.2.1. Elevation of LFTs and CPK With Atogepant
L73: Evaluate cases of elevated liver function tests (LFTs) and creatine phosphokinase (CPK), specifically in the setting of a serious adverse event (SAE) in which elevated LFTs and CPK were associated with a fatality.
L74: 3.1.2.2. Increased Ischemic Vascular Risk
L75: Evaluate if there is an increased risk of vascular ischemia, specifically ischemic cardiovascular events, hypertensive events, ischemic cerebrovascular events, or thrombotic events with the use of atogepant.
L76: 3.1.2.3. Decreased Body Weight
L77: Evaluate if decreased body weight is appropriate for inclusion in labeling.
L78: 3.2. Approach to the Review

Efficacy data supporting this application will be discussed first, followed by an evaluation of the three Key Benefit Review Issues (Section 6.3). The safety data supporting this application will then be discussed, followed by an evaluation of the three Key Risk Review Issues (Section 7.7).

L98: CharacteristicDrug Information



Mass balance resultsFollowing administration of 50 mg 14C-atogepant, 89% of the administered dose was recovered in the excreta; 81% of the dose was recovered in feces (42% as unchanged) and 8% was recovered in urine (5% as unchanged). The major circulating forms in plasma were atogepant (75%) and the major metabolite M23 (dioxygenated methylated glucuronide of atogepant, 15% of total exposure in plasma).

ClearanceBased on the population PK analyses, the mean estimate of apparent clearance (CL/F) was 19 L/h.

Half-lifeMean terminal half-life was approximately 11 hours.

Metabolic pathway(s)Atogepant is metabolized primarily via CYP3A4.

Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations

Body weightPopulation PK analyses indicated that body weight has no clinically relevant impact on atogepant exposures. Dose adjustment is not needed based on body weight.

AgePopulation PK analyses indicated that age has no clinically relevant impact on atogepant exposures. Dose adjustment is not needed based on age.

Renal impairmentNo dose adjustment is needed in patients with mild or moderate renal impairment. Dose adjustment is needed in patient with severe renal impairment and ESRD patients (Section 8.1).

Hepatic impairmentNo dosage adjustment is required in patients with mild or moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Class A or B). In patients with severe hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh Class C), use of atogepant should be avoided (Section 8.1).

Drug Interaction Liability (Drug as Perpetrator)

Inhibition/induction of metabolismBased on in vitro studies, atogepant is not an inhibitor of CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, or CYP3A4, MAO-A, or UGT1A1. Atogepant does not induce CYP1A2, CYP2B6, or CYP3A4 at clinically relevant concentrations.

Inhibition/induction of transporter systemsAtogepant does not inhibit P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3, OCT2, BSEP, MRP3, NTCP, MRP4, OCT1, MATE1, or MATE2-K at clinically relevant concentrations.

Drug Interaction Liability (Drug as Victim)

Inhibition/induction of metabolismAtogepant is a substrate of CYP3A4. Dose adjustment is needed when co-administered with strong CYP3A4 inhibitors, and strong and moderate CYP3A4 inducers. No dose adjustment is needed when co-administered with moderate and weak CYP3A4 inhibitors, and weak CYP3A4 inducers (Section 8.2).

Inhibition/induction of transporter systemsAtogepant is a substrate of P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3 and OAT1. Dose adjustment is needed when co-administered with OATP inhibitors (Section 8.2).

L101: 5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential Effectiveness
L102: CGRP is thought to be involved in migraine based on studies reporting that administration of CGRP can induce migraine-like headaches, that CGRP levels increase in the cranial circulation during a migraine attack, and that CGRP can induce vasodilation (a proposed mechanism for the underlying cause of migraine). In addition, CGRP antagonists have been shown to alleviate migraine symptoms. Atogepant is a CGRP receptor antagonist.
L103: In in vitro binding and functional assays, atogepant demonstrated low picomolar affinity and antagonist activity against human and monkey CGRP receptors, and low nanomolar antagonist activity against mouse, rat, rabbit, and dog CGRP receptors. In off-target binding assays (&gt;120 receptors, enzymes, and ion channels), atogepant showed no affinity at up to micromolar concentrations.
L104: In isolated human coronary, meningeal, and cerebral artery preparations, atogepant (up to 1 \mu\text{M}), antagonized CGRP-induced relaxation. However, atogepant alone (up to 100 \mu\text{M}) did not exhibit any vasoactivity. Atogepant inhibited capsaicin-induced dermal vasodilation at low nanomolar concentrations in monkeys and low micromolar concentrations in rats and rabbits. In monkeys, atogepant antagonized CGRP-induced increases in blood pressure and heart rate at low nanomolar plasma concentrations.
L105: 6. Assessment of Effectiveness
L106: 6.1. Dose and Dose Responsiveness
L107: The Applicant conducted one Phase 2/3 12-week dose-finding study, Study CGP-MD-01, to determine the range of doses to move forward into a Phase 3 confirmatory efficacy study of atogepant for the preventive treatment of migraine. Study CGP-MD-01 was designed to assess both once daily (QD) (10 mg, 30 mg, and 60 mg) and twice daily (BID) dosing (30 mg and 60 mg [total daily doses of 60 mg and 120 mg, respectively]). The results of this study (discussed in detail in Section 6.2.1.4) demonstrated preliminary efficacy of all doses with no clear evidence of a dose-response relationship.
L108: During the November 19, 2018, End of Phase 2 meeting, the Division stated that on face, Study CGP-MD-01 had the characteristics of an adequate and well-controlled efficacy trial, but that a final determination would be made during the review of the application. The doses to be studied in the confirmatory efficacy trial (Study 3101-301-002) were discussed during a Type C meeting in June 2017. At that time, the Division acknowledged that QD dosing may be more feasible for patients than BID dosing, although BID dosing performed slightly better numerically with respect to efficacy in Study CGP-MD-01. The Division recommended that the Applicant also study the 10 mg QD dose in Study 3101-301-002, since efficacy of the 10 mg dose appeared similar to that of the 30 mg and 60 mg QD dose.
L109: A detailed discussion of the evaluation of any dose-response relationship based on the data from these two efficacy studies is provided in Sections 6.3.1 and 6.3.2 of this review.
L110: 6.2. Clinical Trials Intended to Demonstrate Efficacy
L111: 6.2.1. Study CGP-MD-01
L112: 6.2.1.1. Design
L113: Study CGP-MD-01 was a Phase 2/3 multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel-group study that enrolled adult patients with a history of EM (4 to 14 migraine days per month) from 78 sites in the United States. Patients were randomized to one of six different arms in a 2:1:2:1:2:1 randomization sequence of placebo, atogepant 10 mg QD, atogepant 30 mg QD, atogepant 30 mg BID, atogepant 60 mg QD, or atogepant 60 mg BID, respectively. The study included a 4-week screening and baseline period, a 12-week double-blind treatment period, and a 4-week follow-up visit. The primary efficacy endpoint was the change from baseline in mean monthly migraine days during the 12-week double-blind treatment period.
L114: 6.2.1.2. Eligibility Criteria
L115: Key inclusion criteria:
L116: • Male and female patients, ages 18 to 75, inclusive• 1-year history of migraine with or without aura, consistent with the International Classification of Headache Disorders• Age at onset of migraine prior to 50 years• History of 4 to 14 migraine/probable migraine days per month in prior 3 months and baseline period
L117: Key exclusion criteria:
L118: • History of chronic migraine, new persistent daily headache, trigeminal autonomic cephalgia• \geq 15 headache days per month in prior 3 months or during baseline period• History of inadequate response to 3 or more medications used for preventive treatment of migraine• Use of prohibited medications, use of opioids or barbiturates &gt; 2 days/month, triptans or ergots \geq 10 days/month or simple analgesics (aspirin, nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs, acetaminophen) \geq 15 days/month• Abnormal baseline laboratory or electrocardiogram (ECG) results• History of acute hepatitis or abnormal results on hepatitis screen, history of clinically significant cardiovascular or cerebrovascular disease, baseline hypertension (&gt; 160 mm mercury (Hg) for systolic blood pressure or diastolic blood pressure &gt; 100 mm Hg)• History of prior or current use of CGRP pathway drugs in the last 3 months• History of significant risk of self-harm based on responses on the Columbia-Suicide Severity Rating Scale (C-SSRS)
L119: • Baseline alanine aminotransferase (ALT) or aspartate aminotransferase (AST) &gt;1.5X the upper limit normal (ULN), total bilirubin greater than &gt;1X ULN, or serum albumin &lt;2.8 gram/dL• Use of strong or moderate cytochrome P450 (CYP)3A4 inhibitors and inducers and medications with demonstrated efficacy for the prevention of migraine (e.g., topiramate and propranolol) in the 30 days prior to Visit 1 and throughout the study; botulinum toxin injections were prohibited within the 6 months prior to Visit 1
L120: 6.2.1.3. Statistical Analysis Plan
L121: Efficacy Endpoints
L122: The primary efficacy endpoint was the change from baseline in mean monthly migraine days across the 12-week treatment period. Migraine days were defined as the combination of migraine headache days and probable migraine headache days.
L123: Secondary efficacy endpoints included (in hierarchical testing order):
L124: • Change from baseline in mean monthly headache days across the 12-week treatment period.• Proportion of patients with at least a 50% reduction in mean monthly migraine days across the 12-week treatment period.• Change from baseline in mean monthly acute medication use days across the 12-week treatment period.
L125: Efficacy Analysis Population
L126: The efficacy analyses were based on the modified intent-to-treat population, which consisted of all randomized participants who received at least one dose of study treatment, had an evaluable baseline period of diary data, and had at least one evaluable postbaseline 4-week period (weeks 1 to 4, 5 to 8, and 9 to 12) of diary data. For baseline, evaluable is defined as having at least 20 days of diary data during the 4-week baseline period. For each postbaseline 4-week treatment period, evaluable is defined as having at least 12 days of diary data for that particular period.
L127: Analysis Methods
L128: The primary endpoint was analyzed using mixed-effects model for repeated measures (MMRM). The response variable was the change from baseline to each postbaseline month in monthly migraine days. The model included baseline monthly migraine days as a covariate, treatment group and visit (month) as fixed factors, and treatment group-by-visit and baseline-by-visit as interaction terms. The restricted maximum likelihood method was used. The within-patient correlation was modeled using the unstructured covariance matrix (compound symmetry covariance matrix if convergence failed). The Kenward-Roger approximation was used to estimate denominator degrees of freedom. Contrasts were constructed to obtain the average treatment effects across the 12-week (Months 1 to 3) treatment period to compare each atogepant treatment group versus the placebo group.
L129: Two sensitivity analyses were planned on the primary analysis. The first used a pattern-mixture model approach based on the reference-based copy reference method for missing value imputation. This analysis used a missing-not-at-random mechanism for missing data to assess the impact of potential deviation from a missing at random assumption in the primary analysis. The second sensitivity analysis used multiple imputations in conjunction with robust regression to assess the robustness of the primary MMRM analysis with respect to the possibility of a violation of the normality assumption. The normality assumption of MMRM analysis is usually not a concern for trials of this size; therefore, the statistical review mainly focused on the sensitivity analysis for missing data.
L130: For secondary endpoints, pairwise comparisons were analyzed using MMRM for continuous variables, and generalized linear mixed model for repeated measures of the binary response of at least 50% reduction.
L131: Multiplicity Adjustment
L132: The overall type I error rate was controlled at \alpha=0.05 for the set of primary and secondary endpoint comparisons between each dose level of atogepant versus placebo using a graphical approach with weighted-Bonferroni test procedure (Figure 1). The weighting strategy of the multiple comparisons was designed to allocate initial alpha equally to the QD and BID dose regimens. Within each dosing regimen, individual atogepant doses were tested in a hierarchical order from high to low dose. In addition, for a given dose comparison versus placebo, the primary endpoint and secondary endpoints were tested in a prespecified order. If the null hypotheses for both the primary and secondary endpoints are rejected for one of the doses, the associated alpha is passed to the other doses to increase the chances of success for the other doses. For example, if the null hypotheses were rejected for all endpoints for the BID doses, the 60 mg QD dose would have 1/2 \times 2/3 = 1/3 of the alpha from the testing of BID doses in addition to the original half of the alpha. Hence the primary endpoint for 60 mg QD could be tested at a significance level of 0.05 \times (1/2 + 1/3) = 0.0417, and the adjusted p-value would be nominal p-value \times 0.05/0.0417.

L145: As indicated in the preceding tables, most enrolled patients were female, white, and had a mean age of 39 to 42 years. Demographic data were generally balanced across groups. Disposition and reasons for discontinuation did not indicate any clear trends, though the percentage of patients who discontinued due to an adverse event (AE) was highest in the atogepant 60 mg BID study arm by a small margin.

L147: As indicated in Table 11, all 5 atogepant doses demonstrated statistically significant treatment differences compared with placebo in the reduction of mean monthly migraine days during the treatment period compared to baseline after multiplicity adjustment. The results of sensitivity analyses (Section 16) were consistent with the results of the primary efficacy analysis.

L163: Medical Officer's Assessment: All doses demonstrated statistically significant reductions on the primary endpoint and first secondary endpoint. The reduction in mean monthly migraine days of 0.7 to 1.15 days (difference from placebo) for the daily dosing regimens is in line with other recently approved therapies that act on the CGRP pathway (range 0.7 to 2.0 reduction in mean monthly migraine days). This degree of change was deemed to represent a clinically meaningful change for the other approved therapies for the preventive treatment of migraine and this reviewer concludes that the degree of change detected in this study also represents a clinically meaningful change. No clear dose-response relationship was observed in this study. Refer to details of this issue later in this review (Section 6.3.1).
L164: 6.2.2. Study 3101-301-002
L165: 6.2.2.1. Design
L166: Study 3101-301-002 was a Phase 3 multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled, parallel-group study which enrolled adult patients with a history of EM (4 to 14 migraine days per month) from 128 sites in the United States. The primary efficacy endpoint was the change from baseline in mean monthly migraine days during the 12-week double-blind treatment period. Patients were randomized to one of four different arms in a 1:1:1:1 randomization sequence of placebo, atogepant 10 mg QD, atogepant 30 mg QD, or atogepant 60 mg QD. Approximately 70% of randomized participants had to have taken at least one prior medication for the preventive treatment of migraine, and randomization was stratified based on this prior exposure (yes/no). The study included a 4-week screening and baseline period, a 12-week double-blind treatment period, and a 4-week follow-up visit.
L167: Medical Officer's Assessment: A notable difference between this trial and Study CGP-MD-01 was the requirement that 70% of patients had to have taken at least one prior medication for the preventive treatment of migraine. A discussion of the potential impact of this difference is discussed in Section 6.3.1.
L168: 6.2.2.2. Eligibility Criteria
L169: Key inclusion criteria were the same as Study CGP-MD-01.
L170: Key exclusion criteria were the same as Study CGP-MD-01 (Section 6.2.1.2 above), with the following exceptions:
L171: • Patients with baseline ALT or AST &gt;1X ULN, total bilirubin &gt;1X ULN, serum albumin &lt;2.8 gram/dL, or estimated glomerular filtration rate &lt;30 mL/min/1.73 m2 were also excluded.• Patients on prior injectable monoclonal antibodies blocking the CGRP pathway within the last 6 months and/or patients with a history of using more than three doses of ubrogepant or rimegepant were excluded.
L172: Medical Officer's Assessment: The criteria for abnormal LFTs at baseline in this study are stricter than the criteria listed for Study CGP-MD-01. Additionally, prior use of drugs or biologics that act on the CGRP pathway required a longer time or more specific criteria since last use compared to that of Study CGP-MD-01. Although these are relatively minor differences, they may affect conclusions regarding hepatic safety or the general safety of atogepant when used either as a single CGRP antagonist, or in combination with another product that acts on the CGRP pathway.
L173: 6.2.2.3. Statistical Analysis Plan
L174: Efficacy Endpoints
L175: The primary efficacy endpoint was the change from baseline in mean monthly migraine days across the 12-week treatment period.
L176: Secondary efficacy endpoints included (in hierarchical testing order):
L177: • Change from baseline in mean monthly headache days across the 12-week treatment period• Change from baseline in mean monthly acute medication use days across the 12-week treatment period• At least a 50% reduction in 3-month average of monthly migraine days• Change from baseline in MSQ v2.1 RFR domain score at week 12• Change from baseline in mean monthly PDA domain score of the AIM-D across the 12-week treatment period• Change from baseline in mean monthly PI domain score of the AIM-D across the 12-week treatment period
L178: Efficacy Analysis Population
L179: The efficacy analyses were conducted on the modified intent-to-treat population, which consisted of all randomized participants who received at least one dose of study intervention, had an evaluable baseline period of eDiary data, and at least one evaluable postbaseline 4-week period (Weeks 1 to 4, 5 to 8, and 9 to 12) of eDiary data during the double-blind treatment period. For baseline, evaluable is defined as having at least 20 days of diary data during the 4-week baseline
L180: period. For each postbaseline 4-week treatment period, evaluable is defined as having at least 14 days of diary data for that particular period.
L181: Analysis Methods
L182: The primary endpoint was analyzed using MMRM. The statistical model included treatment group, visit, prior exposure (yes/no) to a migraine preventive medication, and treatment group-by-visit interaction as categorical fixed effects. It also included the baseline score and baseline-by-visit interaction as covariates. An unstructured covariance matrix was used to model the covariance of within-participant repeated measurements. The Kenward-Roger approximation was used to estimate the denominator degrees of freedom.
L183: Sensitivity analyses for missing data handling were planned, including: (1) analysis of covariance model based on 3-month average of the monthly migraine days, (2) MMRM based on primary measures collected during the double-blind and follow-up periods, (3) analysis of covariance with multiple imputations within group, and (4) a pattern-mixture model approach based on the reference-based copy reference method. Sensitivity analysis using multiple imputations in conjunction with robust regression were conducted to assess the robustness of the primary MMRM analysis to the possible violation of the normality assumption.
L184: The 50% or greater responder endpoint, where responders were defined as a participant with at least 50% reduction in 3-month average of monthly migraine days, was analyzed using a logistic regression model. The other secondary efficacy endpoints were analyzed in the same manner as that was used to analyze the primary endpoint.
L185: Multiplicity Adjustment
L186: The overall type I error rate was controlled at \alpha=0.05 for the set of primary and secondary endpoint comparisons between each dose level of atogepant versus placebo using a graphical approach with weighted-Bonferroni test procedure (Figure 3). The initial allocation of the overall significance level to three primary hypotheses was 1/3 of the overall significance level for each dose. Within each individual dose, testing started from the primary endpoint, and then the secondary endpoints in a prespecified order. If the null hypotheses for both the primary and the first three secondary endpoints were rejected for one of the doses, 1/3 of the associated alpha was passed to the other doses (1/6 fraction for each dose) to increase the chances of success for the other doses in testing endpoints in the primary positions of the hierarchy, and the remaining 2/3 of the associated alpha was reserved for testing null hypothesis endpoints within the same dose. If hypotheses for three null hypothesis endpoints were rejected within a dose based on the remaining alpha, the alpha for this dose was to be propagated to the other two doses to make full use of the alpha.
L187: Figure 3. Study 3101-301-002: Multiple Comparisons Procedure
L188: The diagram illustrates the Multiple Comparisons Procedure (MCP) for Study 3101-301-002. It features four vertical columns of nodes representing different treatment groups: Placebo (10mg/P1 to 10mg/P6), 10mg QD (10mg/S1 to 10mg/S6), 30mg QD (30mg/S1 to 30mg/S6), and 60mg QD (60mg/S1 to 60mg/S6). Each node contains a label (e.g., 10mg/P1), a fraction (e.g., 1/2), and a '0'. Arrows connect nodes between groups, with weights indicating the comparison type. For example, arrows from 10mg/P1 to 10mg/S1, 30mg/S1, and 60mg/S1 are labeled 1/2. Arrows from 10mg/S1 to 10mg/S2, 30mg/S1 to 30mg/S2, and 60mg/S1 to 60mg/S2 are labeled 1/3. Arrows from 10mg/S2 to 10mg/S3, 30mg/S2 to 30mg/S3, and 60mg/S2 to 60mg/S3 are labeled 1/6. Arrows from 10mg/S3 to 10mg/S4, 30mg/S3 to 30mg/S4, and 60mg/S3 to 60mg/S4 are labeled 1/2. Arrows from 10mg/S4 to 10mg/S5, 30mg/S4 to 30mg/S5, and 60mg/S4 to 60mg/S5 are labeled 1/2. Arrows from 10mg/S5 to 10mg/S6, 30mg/S5 to 30mg/S6, and 60mg/S5 to 60mg/S6 are labeled 1/2. Large arcs on the left and right sides of the diagram are labeled 1/2.A complex diagram illustrating the Multiple Comparisons Procedure for Study 3101-301-002. It shows four groups of nodes: Placebo (10mg/P1 to 10mg/P6), 10mg QD (10mg/S1 to 10mg/S6), 30mg QD (30mg/S1 to 30mg/S6), and 60mg QD (60mg/S1 to 60mg/S6). Nodes are connected by arrows with associated weights (e.g., 1/2, 1/3, 1/6, 1/2, 1/2, 1/2).

L206: As indicated in Table 13, most enrolled patients were female, white, and had a mean age of 40 to 42 years. A recent literature review noted that of 36 recent (since 2011) clinical trials studying migraine, 84.2% of participants were women and 82.9% were white (Robbins and Bernat 2017). The percentage of females (~90%) is slightly higher than is typically seen in migraine clinical
L207: trials yet sensitivity analyses (Section 16) did not suggest differences based on gender, race, or age. Reasons for discontinuation were generally balanced across groups.
L208: As indicated in Table 16, each of the three atogepant doses demonstrated statistically significant treatment differences compared with placebo in the reduction of mean monthly migraine days across the 12-week treatment period compared to baseline after multiplicity adjustment (adjusted p &lt; 0.0001 for all comparisons). The results of sensitivity analyses (Section 16) are consistent with the primary analysis.

L227: Medical Officer's Assessment: Study 3101-301-002 demonstrated statistically significant treatment effects favoring atogepant on all primary and secondary endpoints for all doses, with the exception of the 10 mg dose of atogepant on the clinical outcome assessments, the AIM-D PDA domain score and the AIM-D PI domain score (although there was a nonsignificant numerical benefit at this dose). The effect sizes for the primary efficacy endpoint of the change from baseline in mean monthly migraine days were generally consistent with those observed in Study CGP-MD-01; the clinical meaningfulness of which has been discussed previously in this review.
L228: Unlike Study CGP-MD-01, there was a slight numerical dose-response relationship for all efficacy endpoints, with higher doses demonstrating greater benefit. The 1:1:1:1 allocation of patients to treatments in Study 3101-301-002 may have allowed for an increased ability to identify the apparent dose-response trend. Another factor that may have led to the more predictable estimates for the treatment effect, and thus to seeing a numerical dose response for the doses tested, was the requirement for this study that 70% of patients had to have taken a prior preventive medication, which may have led to a slight reduction in the placebo response rate in this study (-2.48) compared to Study CGP-MD-01 (-2.85). It is important to note that this study was not enriched for prior treatment failures in a manner that could support any related labeling claims. As per the recommendations for study designs described in the Guidance for Industry: Enrichment Strategies for Clinical Trials to Support Approval of Human Drugs and Biological Products,1 a trial which uses an enrichment strategy of including patients who were treatment failures, nonresponders to a different drug should be randomized to the new drug or to the drug they did not respond to. This Applicant did not employ this strategy. Historical account
L229: 1 We update guidances periodically. For the most recent version of a guidance, check the FDA guidance web page at https://www.fda.gov/regulatory-information/search-fda-guidance-documents.
L230: of taking a prior drug for preventive treatment of migraine does not necessarily mean nonresponse or response to that drug.
L231: 6.3. Key Review Issues Relevant to Evaluation of Benefit
L232: 6.3.1. The Adequacy of Study CGP-MD-01 to Support an Efficacy Claim
L233: Issue
L234: Assess if Study CGP-MD-01, a Phase 2/3 dose-finding trial, can serve as one of the two adequate and well-controlled efficacy studies needed to support approval.
L235: Background
L236: A number of drugs have been approved for the preventive treatment of migraine in adults in recent years, including five drugs that act on the CGRP pathway. Following review of Study CGP-MD-01, the Applicant concluded that, although initially designed as a Phase 2/3 dose-finding study, this trial could be considered an adequate and well-controlled study that could be submitted in the current application as one of the two pivotal efficacy studies required to support the approval of atogepant for the preventive treatment of EM. This was discussed with the Division at the November 19, 2018 End of Phase 2 meeting, and the Division concurred that on face, this study appeared to have the characteristics of such a study, but that the final determination would be a matter of review.
L237: During the new drug application (NDA) review, the Division evaluated the following specific issues with respect to the design and strength of the findings from Study CGP-MD-01:
L238: • The trial was originally designed as a Phase 2/3 dose-finding study and not as a study intended to support marketing approval, so the clinical review team evaluated whether the trial had the characteristics needed to consider this an adequate and well-controlled trial.• Though the trial demonstrated a statistically significant effect on the prespecified primary endpoint, the treatment effect size (placebo corrected reduction of 0.7 to 1.1 monthly migraine days) was on the lower end of what was seen with other products marketed for this indication (placebo corrected reduction of 0.7 to 2.0 monthly migraine days).• There was an inverse numerical dose-response relationship, with the highest numerical treatment effect in the QD dose arms occurring in the lowest dose of 10 mg QD and progressively lower numerical effects in the 30 mg and 60 mg QD dose arms. The BID dosing arms had a greater numerical benefit than the QD dosing arms, but, again, the 60 mg QD arm had a smaller numerical effect size as compared to the 30 mg BID arm.• Although all numerically favoring atogepant, the secondary endpoints (beyond the first secondary endpoint of change from baseline in mean monthly headache days) did not reach statistical significance in any arm of patients treated with daily atogepant compared to placebo. (Section 6.2.1.4)
L239: Assessment
L240: In order to determine whether Study CGP-MD-01 could serve as one of the two adequate and well-controlled efficacy trials to support the current application, this reviewer conducted an assessment to evaluate why the findings in this study appeared to be less robust to those of Study 3101-301-002. This assessment is described below.
L241: The study design and many characteristics of Study CGP-MD-01 were identical to that of Study 3101-301-002, except that in Study 3101-301-002, at least 70% of patients were required to have taken at least one prior preventive treatment for migraine. Study 3101-301-002 was stratified for this factor and Study CGP-MD-01 was not. In Study CGP-MD-01, only 30% percent of patients had tried a prior preventive treatment for migraine. As stated previously, it is possible that the enrollment of a greater percentage of patients who were naïve to preventive treatments for migraine in Study CGP-MD-01 accounts for the somewhat higher placebo response in this study, thereby decreasing the ability to demonstrate statistical significance on the analyses of several secondary efficacy measures.
L242: The Applicant conducted a post hoc analysis of Study CGP-MD-01 comparing the reduction from baseline in the number of monthly migraines over the 12-week treatment period in the subgroup of patients who had taken prior preventive treatment to those who had not (treatment-naïve). This analysis demonstrated that in patients who had previously tried a prior therapy for the preventive treatment of migraine, the effect size was greater (-1.38, -1.51, -2.17 for 10 mg, 30 mg, and 60 mg atogepant, respectively) between atogepant and placebo arms compared to treatment-naïve patients (-0.81, -1.05, -0.66 for 10 mg, 30 mg, and 60 mg atogepant, respectively). The Applicant concluded that the slightly higher placebo response rate seen in Study CGP-MD-01 (-2.85 days) as compared to that of Study 3101-301-002 (-2.48 days) may have contributed to this difference. Please see Section 16 (sensitivity analyses) for results of these analyses. In addition, Study CGP-MD-01 utilized a 2:1:2:1:2:1 randomization ratio unlike the 1:1:1:1 randomization ratio used in Study 3101-301-002, and this unequal allocation may have biased patients leading to a heightened expectation of therapeutic benefit in Study CGP-MD-01 (Hey and Kimmelman 2014).
L243: Conclusion
L244: The review team, including the medical officer, clinical pharmacology, and statistical reviewers, has assessed the design, results, and analysis of Study CGP-MD-01 to evaluate whether this study can be considered an adequate and well-conducted trial to support approval of this application. The team determined that although there are some limitations identified in the trial results, the data provided still support efficacy of atogepant for the preventive treatment of EM in adults. Overall, despite the presence of an inverse numerical dose response, the primary endpoint was positive for all doses, and the secondary endpoints that did not reach statistical significance demonstrated a trend in the correct direction. There was a numerically higher treatment effect in the BID dosing groups as compared to the QD dosing groups, again suggesting the presence of a higher treatment effect and biological activity at these higher exposures. In addition, the unequal allocation of treatment arms in this study and the higher percentage of treatment naïve patients, may have increased the placebo response rate, making it more difficult to detect a treatment effect. Ultimately, the data were consistent with atogepant's efficacy. Therefore, the team concludes that this study can serve as an adequate and well-controlled trial in support of the efficacy of atogepant.
L245: 6.3.2. Should the Atogepant 10 mg Daily Dose Be Recommended for the General Population?
L246: Issue
L247: Assess if the 10 mg daily dose, in addition to the 30 mg and 60 mg daily doses, should be recommended for the general population (and not just specific populations as the Applicant proposes).
L248: Background
L249: The Applicant studied several doses of atogepant, including daily doses of 10 mg, 30 mg, 60 mg and twice daily doses of 30 mg and 60 mg, in Study CGP-MD-01. The Applicant only studied the three daily doses in Study 3101-301-002. The Applicant initially proposed the 10 mg QD dose be marketed only for specific populations, namely patients with kidney disease (see Pharmacology discussion, Section 8.1 for their final recommendation) and patients taking concomitant CYP3A4 inhibitors; however, it may be advantageous to include the option of a lower effective dose to the general population.
L250: Assessment
L251: Atogepant was effective at all doses studied in Studies CGP-MD-01 and 3101-301-002 (Section 6.2, Table 11, Table 12, Table 16, and Table 17) The primary efficacy endpoint, the change from baseline in mean monthly migraine days over the double-blind treatment period, demonstrated positive results in both trials. In Study CGP-MD-01, the 10 mg daily dose had the numerically largest treatment effect compared to other groups, with an inverse numerical dose-response relationship observed when considering all daily doses studied, although the twice daily dosing regimens did perform numerically better than the daily dosing regimens. In Study 3101-301-002, there was a slight numerical dose- response relationship for the three daily doses studied (10 mg, 30 mg, and 60 mg).
L252: Studies CGP-MD-01 and 3101-301-002 demonstrated statistically significant results for all doses on the key secondary endpoint, change from baseline in mean monthly headache days. Study 3101-301-002 also demonstrated statistically significant results for all doses on secondary endpoints of change from baseline in acute medication use, \geq 50\% reduction in mean monthly migraine days, and change from baseline in the MSQ v2.1 RFR domain score. The 30 mg and 60 mg dose arm also demonstrated statistically significant results on the analysis of the change from baseline for the AIM-D PDA domain score and the AIM-D PI domain score, while the 10 mg arm did not (see Section 6.3.1).
L253: Given the consistency of results at the 10 mg daily dose with that of the 30 mg and 60 mg daily doses, and the generally flat-dose response relationship across the range of doses investigated, there is support for approving the 10 mg dose in addition to the other doses proposed by the Applicant (30 mg and 60 mg). As discussed in Section 7.6 of this review, the safety profile for atogepant demonstrates a dose-related increase for some of the adverse reactions, such as nausea, decreased appetite, somnolence, and decreased weight.
L254: Conclusion
L255: The 10 mg daily dose demonstrated consistent efficacy across a range of clinically meaningful endpoints in Studies CGP-MD-01 and 3101-301-002. The lack of a statistically significant treatment effect of this dose on the AIM-D scales in Study 3101-301-002, especially in the setting of a numerical benefit, should not preclude the approval of this dose for the general population.
L256: In considering whether to approve this lower dose, the safety profile of atogepant should also be considered. As discussed in detail in the safety sections below, there did appear to be a dose-related increase in several commonly reported adverse reactions, including nausea, decreased appetite, fatigue, and decreased weight. This supports the approval of the lowest effective dose.
L257: The recommended doses of atogepant are 10 mg, 30 mg, and 60 mg QD for the general population.
L258: 6.3.3. Are the AIM-D and MSQ v2.1 RFR Appropriate for Inclusion in Labeling?
L259: Issue
L260: Evaluate if the results of the analyses of the AIM-D PDA domain score, AIM-D PI domain score, and MSQ v2.1 RFR domain score should be described in labeling.
L261: Background
L262: The Applicant proposes including in labeling the results of the analyses of three clinical outcome assessment (COA) measures that were included as hierarchically-controlled secondary endpoints in Study 3101-301-002. To support these claims, the Applicant submitted a COA evidence dossier for the MSQ v2.1 RFR domain and the AIM-D.
L263: Assessment
L264: The following COAs were prespecified as hierarchically-controlled secondary endpoints in the Study 3101-301-002.
L265: • MSQ v2.1 RFR domain:— Evaluates the frequency of the impact of migraine on daily social and work activities— Has a 0 to 100 scale, with higher scores indicating better daily functioning (i.e., lesser impact of migraine)• The AIM-D PDA domain— Assesses the difficulty in performance of daily activities due to migraine— Has a 0 to 100 scale, with higher scores indicating worse performance of daily activities• AIM-D PI domain:— Assesses physical impairment due to migraine
L266: Has a 0 to 100 scale, with higher scores indicating worse physical impairment. The conceptual frameworks of these COAs are presented in the figures below (Figure 5 and Figure 6).
L267: Figure 5. Conceptual Framework of MSQ v2.1 Role Function-Restrictive Domain
L268: The diagram illustrates the conceptual framework of the MSQ v2.1 Role Function-Restrictive (RFR) Domain. It consists of a vertical stack of seven rectangular boxes, each containing a question. These boxes are connected by a single curved line to a large circle on the right labeled "RFR Domain".1. In the past 4 weeks, how often have migraines interfered with how well you dealt with family, friends and others who are close to you?2. In the past 4 weeks, how often have migraines interfered with your leisure time activities, such as reading or exercising?3. In the past 4 weeks, how often have you had difficulty in performing work or daily activities because of migraine symptoms?4. In the past 4 weeks, how often did migraines keep you from getting as much done at work or at home?5. In the past 4 weeks, how often did migraines limit your ability to concentrate on work or daily activities?6. In the past 4 weeks, how often have migraines left you too tired to do work or daily activities?7. In the past 4 weeks, how often have migraines limited the number of days you have felt energetic?Conceptual framework diagram showing 7 questions grouped into the RFR Domain.

L271: Figure 6. Conceptual Framework of AIM-D
L272: The diagram illustrates the Conceptual Framework of AIM-D. It is organized into three columns: ITEMS, DOMAIN, and GENERAL CONCEPT. The ITEMS column lists 12 specific difficulties, which are grouped into two DOMAINS. The first domain, 'Performance of Daily Activities', includes difficulties with household chores, errands, leisure activities at home, leisure or social activities outside the home, strenuous physical activities, concentrating, and thinking clearly. The second domain, 'Physical impairment', includes difficulties with walking, moving the body, bending forward, and moving the head. L275: Key results for COA-related endpoints from Study 3101-301-002 are summarized below:
L276: • MSQ v2.1 RFR domain score (4th secondary endpoint)– A greater improvement from baseline at Week 12 was observed for all atogepant treatment groups compared with the placebo treatment group after multiplicity adjustment (p &lt; 0.0001), although the endpoint was insensitive to the apparent dose response reflected in the primary and other key secondary endpoints.• Mean monthly PDA domain score of the AIM-D (5th secondary endpoint)– A greater improvement from baseline across the 12-week treatment period was observed for the 30 mg and 60 mg atogepant treatment groups compared with the placebo treatment group after multiplicity adjustment (p \leq 0.0005).– The numerical improvements observed with the 10 mg dose were not statistically significant compared with placebo.• Mean monthly PI domain score of the AIM-D (6th secondary endpoint)– A greater improvement from baseline across the 12-week treatment period was observed for the 30 mg and 60 mg atogepant treatment groups compared with the placebo treatment group after multiplicity adjustment (p \leq 0.0021).– The numerical improvements observed with the 10 mg dose were not statistically significant compared with placebo.

L281: Anchor-based analyses were conducted using data from Study 3101-301-002 to derive thresholds of clinically meaningful within-patient change in COA scores. The anchor-based methods, analyses, and anchors, patient global impression of severity (PGI-S) scale and patient global impression of change (PGI-C) scale, are considered appropriate. Estimated clinically meaningful change thresholds listed below were based on one-point improvement in PGI-S scale, which have clear separations from “no change” category in PGI-S scale. Additionally, the one-point improvement thresholds are consistent with the thresholds presented in the literature, which relied on receiver operating characteristic methods to incorporate various anchors into the psychometric analysis (Speck et al. 2019). However, a one-point improvement as specified a priori by the Applicant is not always sufficient, and the interpretation of within patient change employing a two-point improvement was also considered in our interpretation of clinically meaningful improvement on the clinical efficacy endpoints.
L282: Derived thresholds using one-point improvement in PGI-S scale:
L283: • Improvement from baseline of at least 25.7 points in the MSQ v.2.1 RFR domain
• Improvement from baseline of at least 9.0 points in the AIM-D PDA domain
• Improvement from baseline of at least 6 points in the AIM-D PI domain
L284: Derived thresholds using two-point improvement in PGI-S scale:
L285: • Improvement from baseline of at least 40.0 points in the MSQ v2.1 RFR domain
• Improvement from baseline of at least 9.5 points in the AIM-D PDA domain
• Improvement from baseline of at least 6.2 points in the AIM-D PI domain
L286: Derived thresholds using “much better” response category in PGI-C scale:
L287: • Improvement from baseline of at least 22.9 points in the MSQ v2.1 RFR domain
• Improvement from baseline of at least 8.4 points in the AIM-D PDA domain
• Improvement from baseline of at least 5.5 points in the AIM-D PI domain
L288: Figure 7 through Figure 12 show the empirical cumulative distribution function (eCDF) curves that the Applicant provided based on patient global scales (PGI-S and PGI-C) to derive these thresholds of clinically meaningful within-patient changes.

L341: Conclusion
L342: The MSQ v2.1 RFR domain, AIM-D PDA domain, and AIM-D PI domain measures appear fit-for-purpose in this context of use, and the corresponding clinical efficacy endpoints are defined appropriately and positioned as alpha-controlled endpoints in the endpoint model. Therefore, these measures can be described in labeling. For future drug development, we recommend that Applicants focus on measuring the most relevant and important concepts to patients rather than a broad, disease-specific, health-related quality of life measurement. With
L343: that respect, the AIM-D domains are more consistent, interpretable, and sensitive to the apparent dose-response relationship reflected in the primary and other key secondary endpoints (see Table 17).
L344: The results of the COA measures based on the above three clinical efficacy endpoints have been confirmed by the FDA Statistical Reviewer. The anchor-based analysis results of the COA endpoints showed the following:
L345: • There is a clear separation between all three treatment arms and placebo arm in MSQ v2.1 RFR domain across the range of clinically meaningful thresholds, suggesting that all three treatment arms had clinically meaningful within-patient improvements in the impact of migraine on daily social and work activities, as assessed by the MSQ v2.1 RFR domain score.• There is a more complex pattern of results in the anchor-based analysis of the AIM-D endpoints.— There is a modest separation between the 30 mg and 60 mg treatment arms and the placebo arm in both AIM-D PDA and PI domains across the range of clinically meaningful thresholds, suggesting that both 30 mg and 60 mg treatment arms had clinically meaningful within-patient improvement in performance of daily activities due to migraine and reduction in physical impairment due to migraine.— There is a small separation between the 10 mg treatment arm and the placebo arm in both AIM-D PAD and PI domains within the range of clinically meaningful thresholds, suggesting that the 10 mg treatment arm improves performance of daily activities due to migraine, as assessed by the AIM-D PDA, and reduces physical impairment due to migraine, as assessed by the PI domain scores, but minimally.— Overall, there is a clear dose-response relationship observable in the change from baseline results for the AIM-D domains, which is consistent with other clinical efficacy endpoints (e.g., headache days and acute medication use).• Given the conceptual overlap between the MSQ v2.1 and the AIM-D, consideration should be given in the label to avoid overweighing the apparent treatment benefit. For example, listing overlapping items twice in the label to document the two COA measures or presenting multiple p-values that document a common inference may be misleading.
L346: 7. Risk and Risk Management
L347: 7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Nonclinical Data
L348: A complete nonclinical package was submitted to the NDA, which included general toxicology studies of up to 6 and 9 months' duration in rat and monkey, respectively; a standard battery of genetic toxicology assays (Ames, in vitro clastogenicity in CHO cells, and in vivo rat micronucleus); 2-year carcinogenicity studies in mouse and rat; and reproductive and development studies (fertility and early embryonic, embryofetal development, and pre- and postnatal development studies in rat; embryofetal development study in rabbit).
L349: Dosing in the pivotal general toxicology studies was based on decreases in body weight gain in rats and emesis in monkeys in the nonpivotal studies. No target organ toxicity was identified in the general toxicology studies.
L350: Atogepant was negative in Organisation for Economic Cooperation and Development-compliant Ames, in vitro chromosomal aberration, and rat bone marrow micronucleus assays. Atogepant was not carcinogenic in 2-year mouse and rat studies.
L351: The following safety concerns were identified:
L352: (1) In the 2-year carcinogenicity study conducted in male and female mice, females were not adequately evaluated because none of the criteria for an adequate high dose (HD) was met.(2) In animal studies, oral administration of atogepant during organogenesis resulted in embryo lethality, adverse effects on embryofetal development (decreased fetal body weight in rats, increased incidences of fetal variations in rats and rabbits) at maternal exposures greater than those used clinically.
L353: The nonclinical data support approval of atogepant. However, to address the concerns regarding the assessment of carcinogenic potential, the sponsor will need to conduct a dose range-finding study to determine if the HD in the 2-year carcinogenicity study in female mice was adequate (e.g., a maximum tolerated dose or maximum feasible dose). If the HD is determined not to be adequate, the carcinogenicity study will need to be repeated in female mice with an adequate HD. These may be conducted as postmarketing requirements.
L354: 7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
L355: The following potential risks based on drug class or other drug-specific factors have been identified for evaluation in this review:
L356: • Several oral CGRP receptor antagonists that are no longer in development caused serious cases of drug-induced liver injury (DILI). Modifications were made to drugs developed in this class, resulting in the development of products, such as atogepant, that are not expected to be associated with this safety finding (such findings have not been associated with either ubrogepant or rimegepant, to date). However, given the history of DILI in previously developed oral CGRP antagonists, liver injury was analyzed as a safety signal of interest in this review (see Section 7.7.1).• Since CGRP is thought to play a role in compensatory vasodilation in the setting of ischemia, there is a theoretical concern that CGRP inhibition may increase the risk of cardiovascular and cerebrovascular events. Although an increased risk of such events has not been identified as a safety signal in the other marketed products in this class, this risk is evaluated as a safety signal of interest in this review (see Section 7.7.2).• Due to the diffuse presence of CGRP receptors in the gastrointestinal system, and the labeled Warning for constipation with erenumab, the effects on this system, including the risk of constipation, were assessed as a safety area of interest in this review (see Section 7.7.3).
L357: 7.3. Potential Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L358: Not applicable.
L359: 7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
L360: The safety review focuses on the safety of atogepant for the preventive treatment of EM in adults. The data sources include the results from Studies CGP-MD-01, 3101-301-002, 3101-302-002, and 3101-309-002 (refer to Table 3). The safety population is defined as any patient who received one or more doses of atogepant in any of these studies. The cut-off date for the original submission was May 25, 2020. The Applicant provides safety data in the Integrated Summary of Safety (ISS) based on the following groups:
L361: • Group 1: Controlled clinical efficacy studies (CGP-MD-01 and 3101-301-002) involving several doses of atogepant (10 mg, 30 mg, 60 mg, 30 mg BID, and 60 mg BID) or placebo (Section 6.2)• Group 2: Open-label long-term safety studies (3101-302-002 and 3101-309-002) with atogepant 60 mg or standard of care (SoC) (Section 7.6)• Group 3: Group 1 and Group 2 pooled
L362: In this review, summaries of the Applicant’s findings with confirmatory analyses conducted by the Clinical Data Scientist, Dr. Anne Bunner, will be presented. AE tables in this review refer to treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) by “actual dose” treatment group (the dose the patient received). Tables are either reproduced from the Applicant’s submission (where noted) or created by Dr. Bunner.
L363: All placebo-controlled data will be included in tables and figures, and data from the long-term safety studies will be included, only when indicated. All doses (including the 30 mg BID and 60 mg BID doses) will be examined to provide the most complete information regarding evidence of dose-related AEs. When discussing ISS Group 2, only the 60 mg dose was evaluated, since this is the highest dose proposed for marketing and the only dose studied in the long-term safety studies.
L364: A 120-Day Safety Update Report was submitted on May 19, 2021 (which included a cut-off date of November 13, 2020). The report includes an additional 30 patients who were exposed to atogepant 60 mg daily and additional long-term exposure data for patients enrolled in extension Study 3101-309-002.
L365: The 120-Day Safety Update also included safety information from four additional ongoing studies regarding patients with elevated LFTs (per the Division’s request for an analysis of postbaseline aminotransferase elevations in ongoing studies at the September 22, 2020 preNDA meeting). The ongoing studies include Study 3101-303-002 (a double-blind, randomized, placebo-controlled trial of atogepant for the preventive treatment of CM), Study 3101-306-002 (the long-term extension study for 3101-303-002), and two clinical pharmacology studies. These studies are not reviewed here; however, the pertinent hepatic safety data from these studies is presented as part of Section 7.7.1.
L366: 7.5. Adequacy of Clinical Safety Database
L367: Data sources to support the proposed indication are summarized in Table 3. Of note, the doses in Table 22 below, which summarizes the duration of exposure in all patients (ISS Group 3), represent the first assigned dose (during the placebo-controlled trial) and the second assigned dose (during the open-label study, for those patients who rolled over from the pivotal trials). Patients with only the 60 mg QD dose listed were de novo patients enrolled in the long-term safety study (i.e., had not previously received atogepant). Importantly, there could be a gap of 6 months up to 2 years from the time of the completion of the placebo-controlled study to enrollment in the long-term safety study (Study 3101-302-002), due to a delay in its initiation.

L379: At the time of the cutoff date for the original application, Studies CGP-MD-01, 3101-301-002 and 3101-302-002 were complete. Only Study 3101-309-002 was still ongoing, and the Applicant submitted a 120-Day Safety Update on May 19, 2021, to provide updated safety analyses. Per this safety update, as of November 13, 2020, 2,479 unique participants were exposed to at least one dose of atogepant during the clinical development program; 997 of these participants received atogepant for ≥6 months (715 at the 60 mg once-daily dose) and 670 received atogepant for ≥12 months (469 at the 60 mg once-daily dose). The updated overall mean treatment duration in the long-term safety studies was 278.9 days in the standard of care group and 253.9 days in the atogepant 60 mg once daily group.

L388: The safety database is adequate to allow for a comprehensive safety assessment of atogepant for the proposed indication, patient population, dosage regimen, and duration of therapy. The overall exposure to atogepant exceeds the minimum number of patients recommended by the International Council for Harmonisation E1 Guideline for chronically administered medication.

L402: 7.6.2. Deaths
L403: There were no deaths in the placebo-controlled studies, and two deaths in the open-label long-term safety studies. One death in Study 3101-302-002 was in a 50-year-old female who was the victim of homicide, and this case is not summarized in this review as it was assessed as not related to atogepant by FDA reviewers. The second death (due to beta hemolytic streptococcal infection) is reviewed in detail in Section 7.7.1 and was also assessed as not related to atogepant use by FDA reviewers.
L404: 7.6.3. Serious Adverse Events
L405: There were eight SAEs in the placebo-controlled safety population in the atogepant arms and five SAEs in the placebo arms. These are reviewed below.
L406: There were no trends in SAEs by organ class or by preferred terms.
