L413: The narratives of the SAEs in the safety population in the placebo-controlled studies were reviewed, and a brief summary of select SAEs, related to safety signals of interest, follows by study:
L414: Study CGP-MD-01:
L415: (1) Patient (b) (6) was a 41-year-old white female with a history of migraine who was randomized to atogepant 30 mg QD on (b) (6), and developed right-sided weakness, right hand tingling, nausea, eye twitching, and right eye blurry vision on (b) (6). She went on to develop her typical migraine and took paracetamol and study drug with no change. She developed right jaw pain, slurred speech, and her blood pressure was 138/82 with a normal pulse and ECG. Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) of the brain was conducted in the emergency room and was negative, but computerized tomography showed sinusitis. She was treated with analgesics and fluids and discharged on (b) (6). Atogepant was discontinued on (b) (6) due to the SAE. There are multiple confounders, including possible sinusitis, the history of migraine and the possibility that this may have represented a complicated migraine; however, given the theoretical association of CGRP antagonists and increased risk of cerebrovascular disease, and given that transient ischemic attack is a diagnostic consideration in this case, a relationship of this event and atogepant cannot definitively be ruled out.
(2) Patient (b) (6) was a 26-year-old white female with a history of migraine, nephrolithiasis, status-post-lithotripsy (b) (6) who was randomized to atogepant 30 mg QD (b) (6). The patient stopped study treatment on the last planned day of dosing (Day 84). On Day 96, the patient developed an SAE of ureteritis. She presented with right kidney pain and fever and was admitted with an infected right ureteral calculus. The patient underwent stent placement in the hospital and had an outpatient lithotripsy. These events were not considered to be related to study treatment given the patient's medical history of a previous similar event prior to her participation in this trial. This case is described because it represents an infection. Due to the death reported in this application from sepsis, additional attention was given to cases of severe infection in this application.
L416: (3) Patient (b) (6) was a 55-year-old white male with a history of migraine, gastrointestinal reflux disease, seasonal allergy, and chronic urticaria who was randomized to atogepant 10 mg QD who experienced acute right upper quadrant abdominal pain and cholelithiasis of severe intensity on Day 34 that resolved the same day. On Day 38, the patient presented to the emergency room with recurrent symptoms of right upper quadrant abdominal pain. Work-up revealed a distended gallbladder, with cholelithiasis, potential acute cholecystitis, and no gross stone in the common bile duct. The patient was treated in the emergency room with resolution of the pain. The next morning (Day 39) the patient had recurrent pain and was admitted with a diagnosis of cholecystitis (SAE). The patient underwent a cholecystectomy successfully and was discharged on Day 40. The patient completed study treatment with no further events; therefore, a relationship of this event to atogepant use is unlikely.(4) Patient (b) (6) was a 28-year-old white female with a history of migraine who presented with an overdose attempt. This case is described in further detail in Section 7.6.10.4 Suicidality.
L417: Medical Officer's Assessment: Although an association with treatment cannot be definitively ruled out in the case of the 41-year-old female (Patient (b) (6) described above, the first three SAEs listed do not appear to be related to study drug. The overdose SAE is discussed separately in Section 7.6.10.4 Suicidality.
L418: Study 3101-301-002
L419: There were four total treatment-emergent SAEs (two in the 10 mg arm [described below] and two in the placebo arm).
L420: Patient (b) (6) was a 23-year-old female randomized to atogepant 10 mg who experienced an event of optic neuritis on Day 23 of the study. Her ophthalmological exam was inconsistent and variable, MRI brain and orbits, and visual-evoked potentials were reported as normal. She was treated with steroids and improved on Day 108. Atogepant was discontinued after diagnosis. The patient has a positive family history of optic neuritis and multiple sclerosis. The Applicant assessed the event as not likely to represent optic neuritis and not related to study intervention.
L421: Medical Officer's Assessment: This reviewer disagrees with the Applicant and believes this event could represent optic neuritis. Additionally, a role of atogepant in this event cannot be entirely ruled out since the event did occur close to initiation of this drug; however, a relationship to atogepant remains unlikely given the patient's age, family history, and the increased risk of clinically isolated events in her demographic group. The patient's risk factors confound assessing the relationship between atogepant initiation and the optic neuritis event. In addition, there were no other similar cases observed in the development program, even though patients in this database are in the demographic group that would have a high risk for developing multiple sclerosis or optic neuritis.
L422: Patient (b) (6) was a 56-year-old black female with an extensive past medical history (including asthma, tobacco use, obesity, hypertension, drug abuse, and sleep apnea) who experienced an SAE of an asthma attack on Day 1 of receiving study medication (atogepant 10 mg daily). The day of treatment (Day 1), she started feeling an "itchy/scratchy" throat with shortness of breath and wheezing. Atogepant was
L423: temporarily interrupted on Day 2. On Day 4, she went to the emergency room and was treated for an asthma attack. She was started on steroids and nebulizer treatments. She also experienced myalgia of moderate severity and musculoskeletal pain with coughing. On Day 6, she reported nausea and was discharged from the hospital. Atogepant was resumed on Day 9. On Day 11, she experienced pelvic pain and hematuria. These events were not considered related to treatment. She was withdrawn from the study due to the pelvic pain and hematuria and did not complete the safety follow-up visit.
L424: Medical Officer's Assessment: The Applicant assessed these events as not related to treatment. It is difficult to assess causality due to multiple confounders; however, it is possible that atogepant use led to the initial asthma attack, although the mechanism for such an association is unclear. The patient was on concomitant medications for asthma and was an active smoker. The events of myalgia and musculoskeletal pain may be associated with atogepant (this is reviewed separately as part of Section 7.7.1), but these events are also confounded in this patient due to multiple factors.
L425: Group 2 SAEs
L426: In the long-term safety study, Study 3101-302-002, there were 24 SAEs in the atogepant 60 mg once daily arm and 7 SAEs in the SoC arm. There were no trends in SAEs by organ class or by preferred terms, and individual SAEs pertaining to specific organ systems are evaluated separately (e.g., suicidality, beta hemolytic streptococcal infection).
L427: The following is a notable case of a generalized seizure that occurred in the long-term safety study.
L428: • Patient (b) (6) was a 31-year-old female receiving atogepant 60 mg in the long-term safety study who discontinued due to a seizure on Day 33 of treatment with atogepant. This patient did not report that she had a history of epilepsy, for which she was taking topiramate, at screening. The patient had a history of complex partial seizures, was status-post-amygdalohippocampectomy in (b) (6), and experienced an SAE of generalized seizure (after a 10-year history of not having generalized seizures and only having continued complex partial seizures). She was discontinued from the study. It is unlikely the seizure was related to atogepant, given the patient's epilepsy diagnosis and the much greater likelihood of the occurrence representing a breakthrough seizure.
L429: 7.6.4. Dropouts and/or Discontinuations Due to Adverse Events
L430: In Study CGP-MD-01, three patients discontinued due to an SAE (two patients in an atogepant arm and one patient in the placebo arm). Five patients discontinued due to elevated LFTs: three patients discontinued due to elevated ALT or AST, one in the atogepant 10 mg QD arm and two in the 60 mg atogepant QD arm. These patients are further described in Section 7.7.1.
L431: In Study 3101-301-002, three patients discontinued due to an SAE (two in the atogepant 10 mg arm and one in the placebo arm). There were no discontinuations due to cardiovascular or LFT abnormalities. One patient in the placebo arm discontinued because of suicidal ideation.

L438: In the placebo-controlled trials (ISS Group 1), 50 (3.9%) patients in the atogepant arms and 11 (2.7%) patients in the placebo arms discontinued due to a TEAE. Common AEs leading to discontinuation in the atogepant arms included constipation (7 patients), nausea (6 patients), fatigue (5 patients, with 3 patients in 60 mg BID arm), and dizziness (4 patients).
L439: Though constipation and nausea were the most common reasons for discontinuation in the placebo-controlled trials, the absolute number of patients who discontinued due to these TEAEs was small. There were no dose-related trends for discontinuation. TEAEs that led to discontinuation in less than 2 atogepant-treated patients are not included to simplify the table above, and because these are less likely to be related to atogepant in the absence of any trends. The full table is included in Section 17 (Table 127).
L440: In the long-term safety studies (ISS Group 2), the most common reasons for discontinuation in the atogepant-treated arms were nausea or vomiting in seven patients (0.6%), fatigue or somnolence in five patients (0.4%), and abdominal pain in four patients (0.3%). Please see Table 128 in Section 17 for the full table in ISS Group 2.

L495: NDA 215206Qulipta (atogepant)
L496: An increase in sodium to 145 to 149 mEq/L was observed in 10.8% of atogepant-treated patients compared to 7.8% of placebo-treated patients at any time in the study. Sodium levels did not trend higher than 155 for any patient, and only three patients (0.23%) in the atogepant arms had a level 2 sodium increase (&gt;150). There were no reported AEs associated with these laboratory abnormalities. These findings likely reflect natural variations over time as there is no clear mechanism for this finding based on what is currently known about the pharmacodynamics of atogepant.
L497: An increase in potassium of 5.6 to 5.9 mEq/L at any time was observed in 70 (5.4%) patients in the atogepant arms compared to 15 (3.8%) patients in the placebo arm, with a risk difference of 1.64 (-0.6 to 3.87). There were no reported AE terms associated with these findings. These findings likely reflect natural variation over time as there is no clear mechanism for this finding either, based on what is currently known about pharmacodynamics of atogepant.
L498: Lastly, the bicarbonate levels in 227 (17.5%) of atogepant-treated patients were lower than 20 mEq/L at some point during the study compared to 63 (15.8%) of patients in the placebo arm. The risk difference is 1.71 (-2.42 to 5.84). This may be due to some degree of hyperventilation or metabolic acidosis, but the mechanism of this is unclear, and the changes are similar in the placebo arm (with a smaller population of patients) with a less than 2% risk difference.
L499: Kidney function
L500: Laboratory values related to kidney function were evaluated over the course of the placebo-controlled trials (ISS Group 1). There were no patients in either atogepant or placebo groups with elevated creatinine of at least 1.5X baseline values during the study.

L551: 7.6.8. QT Interval Evaluation
L552: This application was reviewed by the Interdisciplinary Review Team for cardiac safety studies. The Interdisciplinary Review Team concludes that there was no significant corrected QT interval prolongation effect of atogepant detected.
L553: The Applicant conducted a thorough study (CGP-PK-04) and evaluated the highest dose of 300 mg atogepant. The results of an analysis of the data from this study did not suggest that atogepant is associated with a significant corrected QT interval prolonging effect (Table 41). Nonclinical data support this finding.

L562: 7.6.9. 120-Day Safety Update
L563: As of November 13, 2020, 2,479 unique participants were exposed to at least one dose of atogepant during the clinical development program for episodic migraine; 997 of these participants received atogepant for ≥6 months (715 at 60 mg QD) and 670 received atogepant for ≥12 months (469 at 60 mg QD).
L564: There were no deaths since the May 25, 2020 cut-off date for the initial NDA.
L565: There were six additional treatment-emergent SAEs (gastritis, Clostridium difficile colitis, road traffic accident and concussion, basal cell carcinoma, prostate cancer, and ureterolithiasis), all of which are considered unrelated to study drug.
L566: There were no reports of suicidal ideation with intent to act or of suicidal behavior in Study 3101-309-002.
L567: One additional participant in Study 3101-309-002 (atogepant 60 mg once daily) had a treatment-emergent AST value \geq 3\times ULN since the safety reporting cut-off for the initial NDA; the Clinical Adjudication Committee adjudicated the elevation as unlikely related to atogepant.
L568: As of January 31, 2021, a total of five participants had ALT and/or AST value \geq 3\times ULN reported during participation in ongoing studies (one study in CM and one pharmacokinetics [PK] study). Please see review in Section 7.7.1 by Dr. Paul Hayashi for details of these cases and assessment.
L569: 7.6.10. Analysis of Submission-Specific Safety Issues
L570: 7.6.10.1. Cardiovascular (Including Hypertension), Cerebrovascular, or Peripheral Vascular Disease
L571: Please refer to Section 7.7.2 for a detailed description of this safety issue and an analysis of findings in this application.
L572: 7.6.10.2. Hepatotoxicity
L573: Please refer to Section 7.7.1 for a detailed description of this safety issue and an analysis of findings in this application.
L574: 7.6.10.3. Gastrointestinal Disorders
L575: Gastrointestinal disorders have been associated with CGRP antagonism, and the erenumab label has a Warning for constipation, including the potential for serious cases. A review of gastrointestinal AEs was conducted to assess if there is an increased risk of these events associated with atogepant use.
L576: Evaluation of TEAEs demonstrated that constipation and nausea were among the most commonly reported in atogepant-treatment patients at 6% (compared to 1% in placebo) and 7% (compared to 3% in placebo), respectively. In addition, constipation and nausea were the most common reasons for discontinuation in the placebo-controlled trials, although the absolute number of patients who discontinued due to these events was small. In the long-term safety studies, constipation was cited as a reason for discontinuation in one patient (0.08%), though other terms in the gastrointestinal disorders category (including nausea, abdominal pain, vomiting, constipation, and dyspepsia) were cited as AEs leading to discontinuation in a total of 13 patients (1.1%).
L577: There is no apparent association with atogepant treatment and serious gastrointestinal events; however, the risk of such events should continue to be evaluated in the postmarketing setting through enhanced pharmacovigilance.
L578: 7.6.10.4. Suicidality
L579: Suicidal ideation (SI) and suicidal behavior evaluations were conducted across all clinical trials of atogepant (based on the recommendations to evaluate such risk in the development programs
L580: for all drugs with central nervous system activity as described in the Guidance for Industry: Suicidal Ideation and Behavior: Prospective Assessment of Occurrence in Clinical Trials2). The clinical trials excluded patients who had significant risk of self-harm based on clinical interview or responses on the C-SSRS (type 4 or 5 on the C-SSRS) in the past 6 months or suicidal behavior in the past 6 months. The C-SSRS has categories with binary responses (yes/no) with Category 1 (“wish to be dead”) to Category 5 (“active suicidal ideation with specific plan and intent”) and further categorizations with aborted, interrupted, actual and completed suicide (Category 10).
L581: Group 1 - Placebo-Controlled Studies
L582: In Study CGP-MD-01, there was one patient with SI in the 30 mg BID arm (“wish to be dead” with no specific plan or intent). No patients reported suicidal behavior.
L583: In Study 3101-301-002, one patient in atogepant 10 mg arm and two patients in placebo arm reported SI during the double-blind treatment period. No patients reported SI with intent to act. One patient in the placebo arm discontinued the study due to SI with aborted attempt and this patient is not reviewed here.
L584: In total 2 out of 1,142 (0.2%) patients in atogepant-treated arms reported SI compared to 3 out of 408 (0.7%) in the placebo arms.
L585: Group 2 - Long-Term Safety Studies
L586: In the long-term safety study, nine patients (0.8%) in the atogepant 60 mg arm reported SI on the C-SSRS assessment (three with “wish to be dead,” three with nonspecific suicidal thoughts, one with active suicidal ideation with some intent to act without a specific plan, and one with a plan and intent), and five patients (2.6%) in the SoC arm reported SI. In the safety follow-up period, an additional three patients (0.3%) reported suicidal ideation in the atogepant 60 mg QD arm.
L587: Suicidal behavior was reported on the C-SSRS assessment by two (0.2%) of atogepant-treated patients during the open-label treatment (both were discontinued from the study) due to actual attempts and one patient (0.5%) reported suicidal behavior in the follow-up period. Two of these cases are described below:
L588: Review of Cases With Suicidal Behavior
L589: (1) Patient (b) (6) was a 28-year-old white female with a history of migraine, obesity, anxiety, major depression, and post-traumatic stress disorder who was enrolled in Study CGP-MD-01 (60 mg QD) and then rolled over to the long-term safety study. On Day 34 of treatment, she took 28 tablets of acetaminophen for abdominal pain and migraine, but she denied suicidal ideation (though a friend thought it was an attempt to self-harm). She was admitted for accidental overdose with acetaminophen. Study treatment was temporarily discontinued, and she was treated for the acetaminophen overdose. On Day 37 that patient self-admitted for post traumatic reaction. The patient then entered the long-term safety study and was placed on atogepant 60 mg daily. On Day 60 of the long-term safety study, she was started on escitalopram 5 mg daily for worsening depression. She was reported to have sinus node dysfunction (tachy-brady syndrome) on Day 71 with
L590: 2 We update guidances periodically. For the most recent version of a guidance, check the FDA guidance web page at https://www.fda.gov/regulatory-information/search-fda-guidance-documents.
L591: low blood pressure and was started on fludrocortisone. On Day 155, the patient had an overdose of escitalopram (exact dose unclear), denied being suicidal and was not admitted. On Day 158, she had suicidal ideation (type 4 C-SSRS) of moderate severity. Study treatment was discontinued on Day 167. This reviewer concludes that an association between her behaviors and atogepant cannot be excluded; however, these events are confounded by her significant psychiatric history.
L592: (2) Patient (b) (6) reported nonsuicidal self-injurious behavior, “active suicidal ideation with specific plan and intent” and an actual attempt (SAE) and was discontinued from the study. This patient was a 36-year-old black woman with a history of migraine, two prior suicide attempts, depression, prior hospitalization for ecstasy use, epilepsy, and a family history of schizophrenia and bipolar disorder per the SAE report. The patient had a history of being abused, had five children, and reported that she was unemployed. On Day 63 of treatment in the long-term safety study with atogepant 60 mg QD, she experienced an SAE of suicide attempt (chronic major depressive disorder with suicidal attempt and psychosis), contusion, and nausea. She reported taking 5 to 10 pills, but the investigator noted that 16 pills were unaccounted for. She was admitted and atogepant was discontinued. She was discharged from the hospital on Day 71. This is a highly confounded case due to prior suicide attempts and psychiatric history, therefore, a relationship of this event to atogepant use is unlikely.
L593: Assessment of SI/Suicidal Behavior
L594: In the placebo-controlled pivotal studies, there were infrequent reports of SI. There were several reports of both SI and suicidal behavior in the long-term safety studies, including one active SI with specific plan and intent, and three overdose attempts that reportedly did not have associated SI, but were clearly overdose situations. All SI in atogepant-treated patients were in patients with a medical history of psychiatric illness except for one patient who reported a nonserious TEAE of SI that resolved. In the placebo-controlled trials, the rate of SI was higher in the placebo arm than in the atogepant treatment arm, and all cases described are confounded, making it unlikely that atogepant contributed to these events. Comorbid depression is common in migraineurs and the risk of suicide or SI in patients with depression is higher than in the general population. This reviewer recommends routine pharmacovigilance in the postmarketing setting to evaluate for suicidality.
L595: 7.6.10.5. Pregnancy
L596: Three patients in the safety population in Study CGP-MD-01 became pregnant during the study. Two of the three patients delivered babies that were reportedly healthy (one patient received 27 days of treatment with atogepant 30 mg QD during the first trimester and a second patient ended treatment with atogepant at Day 87 and became pregnant approximately 13 days after discontinuing atogepant 30 mg QD), and the third patient elected to terminate the pregnancy. The patient who terminated the pregnancy was a 19-year-old woman who received atogepant 60 mg daily for 82 days with first trimester exposure. She elected to terminate the pregnancy on Day 96 with an SAE of abortion.
L597: Two patients in Study 3101-301-002 became pregnant during the study. Both patients were randomized to 10 mg daily, treated for 8 and 21 days, respectively, and reportedly gave birth to
L598: full-term healthy infants. One patient who was a 29-year-old white female on atogepant 10 mg daily experienced a ruptured ectopic pregnancy.
L599: Medical Officer's Assessment: No conclusions can be drawn from the limited information in this application about atogepant and pregnancy outcomes. This reviewer recommends the standard pregnancy registry and postmarketing pregnancy outcomes study to further assess the effect of atogepant on pregnancy and the unborn fetus.
L600: 7.7. Key Review Issues Relevant to Evaluation of Risk
L601: 7.7.1. Elevation of LFTs and CPK With Atogepant
L602: Issue
L603: Evaluate cases of elevated LFTs and CPK, specifically in the setting of an SAE in which elevated LFTs and CPK were associated with a fatality.
L604: Background
L605: Two oral CGRP receptor antagonists discontinued development (i.e., telcagepant and MK-3207) due to an association with DILI. In the Phase 3 studies with telcagepant, liver injury signals occurred with a relatively short latency of seven days (Holland and Goadsby 2018). The mechanism of liver injury for these agents was postulated to be predominantly the result of a combination of bile acid accumulation (transport inhibition) and oxidative stress (reactive metabolite formation), and less so from mitochondrial toxicity (Smith et al. 2020). In vitro and in silico testing have led to the development of newer CGRP receptor antagonists with the expectation that these agents would not be associated with hepatotoxicity.
L606: At the time of this review, neither of the two recently approved small molecule drugs that act on the CGRP pathway, ubrogepant and rimegepant, have included warnings of DILI based on either the premarketing or postmarketing data.
L607: Of specific interest related to these concerns was one fatal SAE in the development program for atogepant in which a 26-year-old woman experienced elevated LFTs and CPK and an influenza-like illness within 4 weeks of starting atogepant de novo in Study 3101-302-002 (long-term safety).
L608: The DILI team of the Division of Hepatology and Nutrition was asked to review this fatal SAE, as well as the entirety of the application, to evaluate whether there may be a signal for hepatotoxicity with atogepant use (Dr. Paul Hayashi was the reviewer). The Division of Anti-Infectives was also consulted.
L609: Lastly, given the association of elevated CPK with the elevated LFTs, an analysis of any effect of atogepant on CPK, and an evaluation of AE terms related to muscle injury, were also conducted.
L610: Assessment
L611: SAE of Specific Interest
L612: A 26-year-old black female (Patient (b) (6)) died 43 days after initiating treatment with atogepant 60 mg daily de novo in the long-term safety study. On Day 2, she was started on sertraline 50 mg daily. On Day 4, she reported a “mild influenza like illness” that left her bedridden for several days with fever and myalgias that she treated with over-the-counter drugs, including acetaminophen. She did not seek medical attention for this illness. This event reportedly resolved on Day 8. On Day 29, she experienced elevated LFTs (up to 20X ULN for AST) and CPK (200X ULN) without a reported increase in total bilirubin or changes in coagulation tests. She had no associated signs or symptoms (specifically, no fever, myalgias, abdominal pain, weakness, rash, or fatigue, and a normal physical and neurological exam). Atogepant was discontinued, however her LFTs continued to trend upward (with ALT 205, AST 677, and CPK 38,607). She had been working with a personal trainer and was told to stop exercising. On Day 42, the patient reported “feeling fine” per a MedWatch report. Her ALT and AST had fallen by 50% and her CPK was in the 400s. On Day 43, the patient experienced chest pain and became unresponsive. Emergency services was called by her family. She required cardiopulmonary resuscitation on the way to the hospital, and was successfully resuscitated, but subsequently went into cardiac arrest again and died that day. Autopsy report findings determined that she died from Group A beta hemolytic streptococcal infection (toxic shock syndrome) with the same organism growing from her spleen, lung, and blood. She was noted to have elevated creatinine and CPK at death. The Applicant’s assessment was that this event was not related to study treatment.
L613: The DILI team considers it unlikely that this case of elevated transaminases is associated with atogepant use and considers the presence of myositis or myopathy to be better explanations for the elevated AST and ALT, given the extremely high CPK levels. They suggest that the myopathy may have been related to sertraline or excess exercise. The patient’s CPK and liver enzymes had fallen by greater than 50% before the onset of shock; therefore, the Division of Anti-Infectives consultant considers it unlikely that the elevated LFTs had a direct relationship to the development of this patient’s overwhelming group A streptococcal (GAS) infection and death. Therefore, no clear relationship of atogepant to the elevated transaminases or the infection was identified by these consultants.
L614: The Division of Anti-Infectives Clinical Reviewer’s Assessment of this fatal case is extracted from her review, as described below:
L615: Despite this being an atypical case, this patient’s clinical history does appear to be consistent with streptococcal toxic shock syndrome related to invasive group A streptococcal infection.
L616: Per the autopsy report, postmortem cultures grew GAS from spleen, lung, and blood. There was no mention of an overt skin or soft tissue infection or other potential nidus. Therefore, the fact that lung cultures were positive supports GAS pneumonia as the most likely source. Although GAS is an uncommon causative pathogen, most cases of GAS pneumonia are community-acquired and occur in immune competent hosts. GAS pneumonia also tends to be aggressive, with approximately 80% of cases leading to bacteremia and a case fatality rate of 38% (Muller et al. 2003).
L617: Of significance, this patient developed a flu-like illness on study Day 4 and was reportedly “bedridden for several days with fever and myalgias.” However, she did not seek medical care and presumably was not tested for influenza or other respiratory viruses. Patients with a recent history of respiratory viral infection are at increased risk for bacterial superinfections, including GAS (Muller et al. 2003). Although the five-week delay from the inciting viral illness in this case may have been somewhat prolonged, she may still have been at increased risk for a secondary bacterial pneumonia.
L618: Approximately one third of invasive GAS infections lead to development of streptococcal toxic shock syndrome (Svensson et al. 2000; Ekelund et al. 2005). Toxic shock syndrome is defined by rapid onset (within hours) of shock/hypotension and multiorgan failure. The mortality rate for toxic shock syndrome ranges from 30 to 70% despite aggressive treatment (Nelson et al. 2016). This patient reportedly “felt fine” a day prior to onset of symptoms, but subsequently developed chest pain and shortness of breath the next morning, with rapid clinical deterioration and cardiac arrest within hours of symptom onset. Although this is an example of a severe case, it could certainly be consistent with the typical clinical course of invasive GAS infection/toxic shock syndrome.
L619: Based on the patient’s clinical course, including improvement in liver tests prior to onset of infection, lack of evidence of synthetic dysfunction, and potential pulmonary risk factors, it is unlikely that the suspected drug-induced liver injury significantly contributed to her overwhelming GAS infection and subsequent death.
L620: Evaluation of LFTs
L621: A review of LFTs (transaminases) in the atogepant safety database was conducted to evaluate whether there may be a signal for hepatotoxicity with atogepant use.
L622: Overall, there were no clear imbalances in elevation of ALT or AST between the placebo (1.8%) and atogepant treatment arms (1.0%) in the placebo-controlled trials. There were also no cases meeting Hy’s Law criteria. In addition, no clear differences in LFTs were observed in the long-term safety studies between the atogepant and SoC arms.
L623: Despite the lack of imbalances in the elevations of LFTs between treatment arms in the controlled trials, there were a total of 10 cases of liver injury identified by Dr. Hayashi in the atogepant database (including the original submission and the 120-Day Safety Update) that were designated as having evidence of a probable or possible relationship to atogepant use. Of the six cases of at least possible DILI that were identified in the original application, he considered four cases as possible, and two cases as probable. The two cases of probable DILI and one representative case of possible DILI due to atogepant are summarized below.
L624: In addition, the 120-Day Safety Update Report included five new cases of transaminase elevations that Dr. Hayashi assessed as follows: one unlikely, one possible, and three probable DILI due to atogepant. Of the four at least possible cases, one case occurred in a healthy volunteer PK study (CGP-PK-14), two cases in a preventive treatment of CM open-label safety study (Study 3101-306-002), and one case in Study 3101-302-002 (a long-term extension study of a preventive treatment of EM study). These were reviewed in detail by Dr. Hayashi in the addendum to his review. No cases met Hy’s Law criteria.
L625: Review of the 120-Day Safety Update Report also introduced a concern that the risk of developing transaminase elevations was higher for patients of Asian descent, since Study
L626: 3101-306-002, which was an open-label study of 113 Japanese patients, demonstrated a higher rate of transaminase elevation than the overall rate observed in this application. Ultimately, the DILI team concluded that the data do not warrant labeling of a difference in liver injury risk for Asians. Although the ongoing open-label safety study of 113 Japanese patients (Study 3101-306-002) indicated a higher rate of transaminase elevation than the overall rate in the NDA's ISS, no such increase was seen for Asians in the ISS nor the randomized controlled trial from which patient's entered Study 3101-306-002. Therefore, the number of Asians in the ISS is too low to draw conclusions. In addition, the randomized controlled trial had 248 Asians randomized across three arms (placebo, atogepant 30 mg, and atogepant 60 mg), and in that study only 1 out of 248 Asians had elevations in transaminases, which is unlikely to be attributable to study drug. Given that the open-label safety study had no comparator, the DILI team concludes that they cannot definitively assign an increased risk of DILI among Asians based on the current data.
L627: A summary of representative cases of DILI are as follows:
L628: Probable DILI due to atogepant:
L629: • Patient (b) (6) in Study 3101-301-002 was a 39-year-old white female who had elevated LFTs without jaundice 3 weeks after starting atogepant 30 mg QD. At baseline, the patient was on acetaminophen but had normal liver function and CPK tests. She developed elevated LFTs on a routine follow-up visit at 3 weeks but was asymptomatic. Her ALT was 248, AST 107, AP 169, and bilirubin 0.2. Atogepant was held and her LFTs normalized within 9 days. She was restarted on atogepant and her ALT and AST rose to 96 and 55, respectively. She remained asymptomatic. Atogepant was stopped again and follow-up labs 15 days later were normal. She was lost to follow-up thereafter. Given that this patient had a positive rechallenger, a relationship of this event to study drug seems probable.
L630: Probable DILI due to atogepant:
L631: • Patient (b) (6) in Study CGP-MD-01 was a 39-year-old white female who developed elevated transaminases without jaundice 12 days after starting atogepant 60 mg QD. The patient had no history of pre-existing liver disease and drank one alcoholic beverage per week. The patient had normal LFTs at baseline, with a slight increase at screening (only slightly higher than 1X ULN). On Day 13, she had elevated ALT (10X ULN) and AST was 7X ULN (with normal bilirubin and AP). The study drug was stopped 5 days after onset of elevated LFTs. Viral hepatitis serology was negative, autoimmune hepatitis markers were negative, and ultrasound was unrevealing. The patient was taking Excedrin Migraine frequently (every other day) but precise dosing was not provided, and this drug was continued through the washout after stopping study drug.— Per Dr. Hayashi, this case is a weak probable DILI due to atogepant. The acetaminophen is a confounder, but its use continued throughout the washout period. The Applicant suggested this case represented nonalcoholic fatty liver disease, but that disease is not typically associated with such an acute rise and fall of ALT. The Applicant also noted that on the day of DILI onset (ALT reported as &gt;300), the patient took a double dose of atogepant. While a relationship of this event is probable, one fact suggesting that this elevation may have been due to another underlying etiology is the high/normal LFTs reported in this patient at baseline; however,
L632: considering the rapid drop in the transaminase levels when the drug was stopped (dechallenge) a relationship of this event to study drug is probable.
L633: Possible DILI due to atogepant:
L634: • Patient (b) (6) in Study 3101-302-002 was a 56-year-old white female who developed elevated transaminases without jaundice 24 weeks after initiating treatment with atogepant 60 mg QD. She was taking acetaminophen at baseline and AP was mildly elevated (slightly greater than 1X ULN) at baseline, but her LFTs and CPK were normal. She developed asymptomatic elevation in transaminase levels at 24 weeks, which peaked to ALT 177 and AST 83 one week later. She did have a positive cytomegalovirus immunoglobulin M and a flu-like illness that came on after the transaminitis was noted. She continued study drug and her liver enzymes decline by 50% from the peak level, but her LFTs stayed elevated over baseline for weeks 22 to 27 on study drug. The LFTs returned to normal 9 weeks after stopping atogepant.— Per Dr. Hayashi, this may be a case of chronic DILI while on atogepant with partial or slow adaptation; however, the 24-week onset (compared to the other probable DILI case) is a long latency. Autoimmune hepatitis may have played a role but the fact that the injury did resolve without treatment and after the drug was discontinued makes it less consistent with a diagnosis of autoimmune hepatitis.
L635: Evaluation of CPK
L636: Given that there were several cases of elevated CPK in association with elevated LFTs, an analysis of CPK-related findings (as a TEAE and as a laboratory abnormality) was conducted.
L637: There have been no definite associations of elevated CPK values with the approved drugs/biologics that act on the CGRP pathway to date.
L638: As noted previously during the review of laboratory findings (Section 7.6.6), CPK results fluctuated across all treatment arms, including placebo, with approximately 4% of both the atogepant- and placebo-treated patients experiencing an increase in CPK of &gt;3X ULN and 1% with a CPK &gt;10X ULN. There was no dose-related increase in CPK elevations.
L639: Discontinuations due to elevated CPK or muscle symptoms were also assessed. In Study CGP-MD-01, one patient (Patient (b) (6)) discontinued due to elevated CPK on Day 43, and two patients discontinued due to myalgia, one reported on study Day 2 (Patient (b) (6)) and one on study Day 21 (Patient (b) (6)). Patient (b) (6), a 21-year-old black female who was randomized to atogepant 10 mg daily, was found to have a mild severity elevation in CPK on Day 43 and was discontinued from the study. Patient (b) (6) was assigned to placebo and reported myalgia on Day 2 and was discontinued from the study. Patient (b) (6) a 62-year-old white female, reported myalgia and palpitations on Day 21 (and heartburn on Day 28). In Study 3101-301-002, one patient (Patient (b) (6)) discontinued due to leg cramps that began within a month of starting treatment.
L640: TEAEs related to elevated CPK were also analyzed. As noted previously, the preferred term “blood creatine phosphokinase increased” was assessed. This AE term was reported in 2.4% of patients across all atogepant-treated groups compared to 1.2% of patients in the placebo arms for a risk difference of 1.2 (CI: -0.2, 2.5); however, there did not appear to be any dose-related increase in this finding (Table 42).
L641: Additionally, the following grouped queries from the placebo-controlled trials were conducted to assess muscle injury and musculoskeletal pain.

L648: This analysis did not indicate an increase in muscle injury or musculoskeletal AEs in the atogepant-treated groups compared to placebo. The analysis of individual TEAE terms (related to muscle injury or CPK elevations) and laboratory analyses of CPK elevations also did not indicate an increase in these events in the atogepant-treated groups compared to placebo.
L649: Conclusion
L650: The analysis for liver injury demonstrates that there were several cases of transaminase elevations in patients taking atogepant in clinical trials where the relationship to atogepant was considered either probable or possible. No Hy’s law cases were identified, and there was no imbalance of LFT abnormalities overall between atogepant and placebo-treated patients.
L651: The Division of Hepatology and Nutrition DILI team concludes that there was no significant liver toxicity that would threaten approval of atogepant. The fatal case did not involve DILI. The team also notes that there were 10 DILI cases described in the atogepant safety database that were assessed as having either a probable or possible relationship to atogepant use. All cases were asymptomatic and resolved with discontinuation of atogepant. The DILI team recommends language for labeling and also concluded the following:
L652: (1) Routine blood tests for liver enzyme monitoring is not necessary.
(2) The use of atogepant should be avoided in patients with severe hepatic impairment.
L653: Following labeling negotiations, the following language for Section 6 of the Prescribing Information was agreed-upon related to this issue:
L654: In Study 1 and Study 2, the rate of transaminase elevations over 3 times the upper limit of normal was similar between patients treated with QULIPTA (1.0%) and those treated with placebo (1.8%). However, there were cases with transaminase elevations over 3 times the upper limit of normal that were temporally associated with QULIPTA treatment; these were asymptomatic, and resolved within 8 weeks of discontinuation. There were no cases of severe liver injury or jaundice.
L655: Regarding the evaluation of whether there is a signal in the current database for muscle injury, the analyses conducted of CPK values, AE terms related to muscle injury, and the review of specific cases, did not demonstrate a clear increased risk for muscle injury in patients taking atogepant.
L656: 7.7.2. Increased Ischemic Vascular Risk
L657: Issue
L658: Evaluate if there is an increased risk of vascular ischemia, specifically ischemic cardiovascular events, hypertensive events, ischemic cerebrovascular events, or thrombotic events with the use of atogepant.
L659: Background
L660: Theoretical safety concerns associated with CGRP inhibition include cardiovascular, cerebrovascular, and peripheral vascular events. Specifically, CGRP is thought to play a role in compensatory vasodilatation in the setting of tissue ischemia; therefore, there is a concern that CGRP antagonism may increase the risk of ischemic events (Aradi et al. 2019; Mulder et al. 2020).
L661: The prescribing information for erenumab (Aimovig), another drug that acts on the CGRP pathway, was amended after initial approval to include hypertension in the Warnings and Precautions section after postmarketing surveillance revealed an association between elevated blood pressure and erenumab (Saely et al. 2021).
L662: Assessment
L663: Ischemic vascular events discussed in this section focus on ischemic cardiovascular disease, hypertension, ischemic cerebrovascular disease, and thrombotic events. The general approach provides an analysis of SAEs, AEs, discontinuations, and relevant narratives by safety signal, followed by an overall assessment of the safety signal as it relates to atogepant use.
L664: Ischemic Cardiovascular Disease
L665: An analysis of AEs, SAEs, ECG changes related to ischemia, and discontinuations related to cardiovascular risk was conducted to assess if there is an increased risk of cardiovascular ischemic events with atogepant use compared to placebo in the pivotal trials (ISS Group 1) and long-term safety studies (ISS Group 2).
L666: Patients with cardiovascular risk factors at baseline were enrolled in the placebo-controlled trials. A baseline history of hypertension was reported by approximately 10 to 11% of patients in each arm across the placebo-controlled studies.
L667: A search of the preferred term “ischemic heart disease” yielded no cases in any group for the placebo-controlled trials (ISS Group 1) and long-term safety studies (ISS Group 2). There were no discontinuations related to ischemic events.
L668: No ECG changes seen in this application appeared to be related to cardiovascular ischemic events. ECG changes not related to ischemic changes are discussed in Section 7.6.7 of this review.
L669: Medical Officer’s Assessment: Few cardiovascular events were reported across groups (presented previously) and no dose-dependent relationship in the risk of these events was evident. There were no ischemic heart disease events in the atogepant arms of the placebo-controlled or long-term safety studies. The data do not indicate an increased risk of ischemic cardiovascular events with use of atogepant; however, enhanced pharmacovigilance in the postmarketing setting is recommended. This approach is consistent with that taken for all of approved drugs of this class, to date.
L670: Cerebrovascular Ischemic Events
L671: Cerebrovascular ischemic events were evaluated. There were few cerebrovascular events reported across the placebo-controlled trials (ISS Group 1) (Table 43).

L677: The following are narratives of patients who experienced possible cerebrovascular events in a placebo-controlled trial (first patient) and long-term safety study (second patient).
L678: (1) Patient (b) (6) in Study 3101-301-002 was a 44-year-old white female randomized to atogepant 60 mg QD who experienced hemiparesthesia on Day 19 of the study that resolved on Day 36. The patient continued on atogepant treatment She also had reported anxiety on Day 26. Treatment was discontinued on Day 39 due to lack of efficacy. Although this patient discontinued treatment early, the rationale provided was that it was related to lack of efficacy, not this event.
(2) Patient (b) (6) was a 41-year-old white female with a history of migraine, hyperlipidemia, obesity, and was on oral contraceptives (estrogen containing) who experienced transient blindness (of left eye reported as moderate severity) on Day 22 of treatment with atogepant 60 mg QD. This event resolved on the same day and was not considered related to treatment. On Day 32, she reported intractable headache (status migrainosus) and study drug was discontinued. On Day 36, she had an MRI of the brain performed that showed no evidence of acute infarct and normal circle of Willis, but did demonstrate tiny nonspecific foci of T2/flair hyperintensity in cerebral white matter. She was admitted and treated for status migrainosus and discharged on Day 44 after resolution.
L679: Medical Officer’s Assessment: The first patient’s event of hemiparesthesia may have been related to a transient ischemic attack, but this may have also been related to anxiety or a complicated migraine. Another patient in this database, discussed previously, experienced an SAE that may also have represented a transient ischemic attack. Both of these cases were confounded by other concomitant diagnoses, but a relationship between atogepant use and these possible cerebrovascular symptoms cannot definitively be ruled out.
L680: The second patient’s transient vision loss may have been the result of ischemia, but it may also have resulted from an aura occurring with the migraine. Given that a workup followed to evaluate this patient for ischemia, it seems that transient ischemic attack was in the differential. Since an MRI (~14 days later) did not show evidence of acute/subacute infarct, no conclusive diagnosis can be made.
L681: Overall, a relationship of these events to atogepant cannot be ruled out. Given the continued uncertainty of the relationship of atogepant to possible cerebrovascular ischemic events, this reviewer recommends enhanced pharmacovigilance in the postmarketing setting to evaluate for this possible safety signal. This approach is consistent with that taken for all of approved drugs of this class, to date.
L682: Hypertensive Events
L683: Events related to hypertension were evaluated. As mentioned above, a history of hypertension was reported by 10 to 11% of patients across all treatment groups in the placebo-controlled studies. Hypertension events were reported in 0.2% of the placebo group, compared to 0.6%, 1.0%, and 0.2% in the atogepant 10 mg, 30 mg, and 60 mg arms, respectively. The narratives are reviewed below. New antihypertensive medication use was examined by the Applicant and occurred in 0.5%, 0.6%, 1.0% and 0% of patients in the placebo, 10 mg, 30 mg, and 60 mg arms, respectively.

L689: Cases of new-onset or worsening hypertension were also reviewed. In the placebo-controlled trials (ISS Group 1), hypertension events occurred in 0.7% (9) of patients in the atogepant groups
L690: compared to 0.2% (1) in the placebo group. All narratives were reviewed; however, selected narratives are summarized below.
L691: • Patient (b) (6) was a 33-year-old black female, randomized to atogepant 30 mg BID, who had worsening hypertension and was discontinued from treatment on Day 54. The hypertension was mild in severity. Her predose sitting blood pressure was 136/96 mm Hg. On Day 54, her sitting blood pressure was 150/108 mm Hg. Atogepant was discontinued due to worsening hypertension and noted as not related to study treatment by the Applicant.• Patient (b) (6) was a 48-year-old white female with a history of hypertension on medications, who received atogepant 30 mg QD with predose blood pressure of 142/91 mm Hg. The patient had worsening hypertension (no reading recorded) and urinary tract infection on Day 63. She had valsartan increased to 80 mg twice daily. Her blood pressure medications were changed during the study with repeat measurements as high as 148/92 mm Hg. She completed the study as this was not considered related to study treatment by the Applicant.
L692: The risk of hypertensive events was examined in the long-term safety studies. Interpretation of these data are limited by the open-label nature of these studies.
L693: A baseline history of hypertension was reported by 9.2% of patients in the SoC group and 12.7% of patients in the atogepant 60 mg QD group. Changes to existing medications to treat hypertension occurred in 3.0% of the atogepant 60 mg daily group. Analysis of blood pressure changes over the course of the long-term safety studies did not reveal any changes or safety concerns. Evaluation of patients based on predefined criteria for potentially clinically significant elevations demonstrated similar changes across groups for both the SoC and atogepant-treated arms in the long-term safety studies.
L694: In ISS Group 2, Patient (b) (6) discontinued study drug due to worsening of hypertension. This event was assessed as mild in severity.
L695: An analysis of systolic blood pressure data in the placebo-controlled trials (Figure 16) did not reveal any imbalances, dose-dependent changes, or clear safety concerns linking elevated blood pressure with atogepant use as compared to placebo.

L699: Gray line represents no increase.
L700: Abbreviations: BID, twice daily; ISS, Integrated Summary of Safety; QD, once daily
L701: Medical Officer’s Assessment: It is difficult to assess a causal association with study drug in any of the cases with hypertensive events, since all the patients had a baseline or predose (i.e., prior to the first dose of atogepant) elevated blood pressure reading. Several patients had been on antihypertensive agents or were started on treatment for high blood pressure during the trial. Some patients had blood pressure readings normalize during continued treatment with atogepant and others had continued slight elevations in blood pressure readings. There was no clear dose-related increase in high blood pressure as slight elevations occurred with most doses (ranging from 10 mg QD to 30 mg BID to 60 mg QD, but none in the 60 mg BID arm).
L702: Only one patient had study drug discontinued and she was not rechallenged. Given that all patients had an elevated blood pressure reading at follow-up in the cases reviewed, and that the vital sign outlier analyses of both systolic and diastolic blood pressure did not demonstrate an imbalance in the atogepant-treated arms as compared to placebo arms, it is unclear at this time if atogepant causes worsening hypertension in patients with baseline high blood pressure.
L703: Therefore, the review of all the hypertensive events associated with atogepant use does not indicate a clear safety signal; however, this reviewer recommends enhanced pharmacovigilance in the postmarketing setting based on mechanistic plausibility and the occurrence of similar events noted with at least one other drug that acts on the CGRP pathway. This approach is consistent with that taken for other approved drugs of this class.
L704: Thrombotic events
L705: There were two patients in the placebo-controlled trials (ISS Group 1) who experienced thrombotic events. The narratives are provided below:
L706: • Patient (b) (6) was a 35-year-old white female with a BMI of 45.4 kg/m2 in Study CGP-MD-01 who experienced a deep vein thrombosis on Day 6 of the study that was assessed by the Applicant as not related to atogepant 60 mg BID. The drug was continued, and the event resolved on Day 109.• Patient (b) (6) was a 50-year-old white female with a BMI 39.1 kg/m2 in Study 3101-301-002 who, experienced a postoperative thrombosis on Day 17 of the study (following an unspecified preplanned procedure) and study drug (atogepant 60 mg QD) was continued.
L707: There was one patient who experienced a thrombotic event in the long-term safety study (ISS Group 2). The narrative is provided below:
L708: • Patient (b) (6) was a 57-year-old white female with a history of migraine, peripheral artery occlusion and peripheral artery stent who completed the pivotal study on atogepant 10 mg QD and rolled over into the long-term safety study on 60 mg QD. She experienced an event of worsening peripheral arterial occlusive disease (unclear start date but was started on cilostazol 100 mg daily to treat this on Day 41). Atogepant was discontinued on Day 56.
L709: Medical Officer's Assessment: The cases described in the placebo- controlled trials patients have an unclear association with atogepant although the postoperative case was most likely procedure related. The event that occurred in the open-label safety study of worsening peripheral vascular disease is confounded since the patient had underlying disease with a history of a stent. It is unclear if atogepant contributed to this event since the patient had been on a 10 mg dose for 3 months without a similar event and then transitioned to a higher dose when the event occurred.
L710: Overall, the data do not indicate an increase in the risk of thrombotic events in the atogepant safety database.
L711: Conclusion
L712: Vascular risk related to atogepant was assessed by analyzing cardiovascular (ischemic) events, hypertensive events, cerebrovascular ischemic events, and thrombotic events. The analysis of SAEs, TEAEs, selected patient narratives, vital signs, and ECG data did not reveal an increase in cardiovascular or cerebrovascular ischemic events related to atogepant use in this application. There were several patients with baseline/predose high blood pressure readings who had continued elevated blood pressure readings after treatment with atogepant. The safety data provided in this application related to vascular risk do not support inclusion of language regarding vascular risk in the label of atogepant. Enhanced pharmacovigilance in the postmarketing setting for cardiovascular events, cerebrovascular events, and hypertension is recommended by this reviewer.
L713: 7.7.3. Decreased Body Weight
L714: Issue
L715: Evaluate if decreased body weight is appropriate for inclusion in labeling.
L716: Background
L717: The Applicant asserts that data from the atogepant development program demonstrate an increased risk of weight loss with treatment that should be described in labeling; however, the potential also exists that such a statement could also be viewed as promotional. Therefore, an assessment of this risk was conducted along with a consideration of any appropriate related description in labeling.
L718: Assessment
L719: As displayed in Figure 17, Dr. Bunner analyzed the mean change and percent change from baseline for weight (kg) and BMI (kg/m2).

L723: Abbreviations: BID, twice daily; CI, confidence interval; ISS, Integrated Summary of Safety; QD, once daily
L724: The mean change in weight across arms appeared to be greatest at the end of treatment visit (Week 12) and demonstrated a dose-response, with doses up to 60 mg BID having the greatest decrease in weight. With respect to the three daily doses, the 10 mg dose appeared to be associated with an increase in weight of 0.09 kg compared to a decrease in the 30 mg QD (weight loss of 0.22 kg) and 60 mg daily arms (weight loss of 0.94 kg) at the end of treatment (12 weeks of placebo-controlled treatment).
L725: To evaluate for a change that may be considered clinically meaningful, Dr. Bunner also conducted an analysis to evaluate how many patients had experienced a \geq 5\% weight loss and \geq 7\% weight loss at any time during the study (Table 45). The \geq 5\% cutoff was chosen based on the clinically meaningful threshold used in Prescribing Information (PI) for orlistat (Zenical) (Cheplapharm) and phentermine (Qsymia) (Vivus), two drug products indicated for weight loss. Furthermore, the \geq 7\% cutoff was chosen based on the PI for sertraline (Zoloft) (Pfizer) where weight loss is described as a safety finding.

L731: Weight Changes
L732: Table 45 demonstrates that ≥5% and ≥7% weight loss occurred at any time during the study at similar frequencies in the atogepant 10 mg and 30 mg arms and placebo arms; however, the 60 mg QD arm had a 13.4% incidence of ≥5% weight loss and a 4.7% incidence of ≥7% weight loss, which is a 6.5% and 2% risk difference, respectively, from placebo. Across all arms, the treatment difference for ≥5% weight loss was ~3% between all atogepant-treated and placebo arms. Approximately 2/3 of patients who experienced ≥5% or ≥7% weight loss at any time, had this weight loss demonstrated at the end of treatment (Week 12) visit. Treatments were discontinued at this visit, so the end of study visit would represent 4 weeks off treatment. Almost half of patients had the weight loss ≥5% sustained at the end of study visit.
L733: Of the 157 experiencing ≥5% weight loss:
L734: • 47 patients were normal weight (BMI ≤25)
• 54 patients were overweight (BMI 25 to ≤30)
• 56 patients were obese (BMI ≥30 [range 30 to 53.8])
L735: An outlier analysis of weight gain was also conducted. The data (not shown) suggest that more patients in the placebo arms gained weight compared to those on higher doses (≥30 mg daily) of atogepant.
L736: In the long-term safety study, weight loss ≥5% had an incidence of 30.9% in the atogepant 60 mg QD arm and 24.5% in the SoC arm. Of note, ~36% of patients in the SoC arm were taking topiramate, which has an established relationship with weight loss. The maximum weight loss of -1.7 kg (3.7 pounds) occurred at Months 6 to 9. The weight loss appeared sustained out to 12 months in the long-term safety study, though there was a two-thirds attrition of patients by the end of the long-term safety study, limiting interpretability of these results.
L737: There are no clear patterns to weight change, in terms of which patients were disproportionately affected (higher or lower weight, gender, race, or age).
L738: Associated symptoms and weight changes:
L739: Given that nausea and decreased appetite were examined in association with weight changes and that there was a relatively low frequency of any these preferred terms (e.g., nausea, decreased
L740: appetite and reported weight loss), no associations could be drawn from these subgroup analyses (not shown).
L741: Conclusion
L742: A mild degree of weight loss (mean weight loss of 0.43 kg [0.95 pounds] to 0.94 kg [2.1 pounds]) appears to be associated with use of atogepant 30 mg and 60 mg daily, respectively. Across all arms of the placebo-controlled studies, 9.8% of atogepant-treated patients, compared to 6.9% of placebo-treated patients experienced \geq 5\% weight loss. In addition, the proportion of patients with a weight decrease \geq 7\% at any point was 2.8% for placebo, 3.8% for atogepant 10 mg, 3.2% for atogepant 30 mg, and 4.9% for atogepant 60 mg. Weight gain does not appear to be associated with atogepant use.
L743: Based on the review of weight changes in the placebo-controlled trials, this reviewer recommends including the following statement in labeling:
L744: The proportion of patients with a weight decrease (b) \geq 7\% at any point was 2.8% for placebo, 3.8% for atogepant 10 mg, 3.2% for atogepant 30 mg, and 4.9% for (b) (4) 60 mg.
L745: 8. Therapeutic Individualization
L746: 8.1. Intrinsic Factors
L747: Hepatic Impairment
L748: In a dedicated hepatic impairment (HI) clinical study (CGP-PK-01) in subjects with mild (Child-Pugh A), moderate (Child-Pugh B) and severe (Child-Pugh C) HI, the total atogepant area under the plasma concentration-time curve (AUC) increased by 24%, 15%, and 38%, respectively relative to healthy controls. Based on the magnitude of impact of HI on the total atogepant exposures, the Applicant proposed (b) (4)
L749: The protein binding data collected at predose and 2 hours postdose from the HI study indicated that the subjects with normal hepatic function, mild, moderate, or severe HI showed varied free fraction (% unbound) of atogepant at 1.8%, 2.6%, 2.9%, and 4.7%, in human plasma, respectively. Based on protein binding data collected from all the subjects, the unbound atogepant AUC increased to 1.84, 1.91, and 3.66-fold in subjects with mild, moderate, or severe HI relative to healthy controls, respectively. Based on the absorption, distribution, metabolism, and excretion properties, atogepant is likely to be considered as a low hepatic extraction drug and the clinical significance of changes in unbound concentrations of atogepant is not clear. In addition, the analysis on unbound atogepant PK has few limitations, e.g., plasma protein binding data were collected at limited time-points to derive unbound exposures, and thus the characterization of unbound atogepant PK should be interpreted as an exploratory assessment (refer to Section 14.2, Study CGP-PK-01 for additional details).
L750: Based on the magnitude of impact of mild and moderate HI on total and unbound atogepant exposures (i.e., less than 2-fold increase in AUC based on unbound concentrations noted above as a worst-case scenario), the review team agrees with the Applicant's proposal to not adjust
L751: doses in subjects with mild or moderate HI. This is because a clear dose-/exposure-efficacy relationships in the dose range of 10 mg QD to 60 mg BID was not observed in the Phase 2 and Phase 3 studies. In addition, there is adequate benefit/risk for the 10 mg to 60 mg QD dose (refer to Section 7.6). Although the 60 mg BID is not proposed for general dosing, the review team believes that the clinical experience with this dosing regimen provides appropriate safety coverage if potential increases in unbound atogepant concentrations in hepatic impairment have any clinical relevance. In patients with severe HI, dose adjustment to 10 mg QD may be a reasonable approach from a PK perspective, based on less than 4-fold increase in AUC on unbound concentrations noted above as a worst-case scenario, and lack of a clear dose-exposure-efficacy relationship in more than 6-fold range in dosing regimen noted above. However, a DILI assessment of atogepant suggests a potential for liver toxicities with atogepant (refer to Section 7.7.1). Therefore, the review team agrees with the recommendation from DILI team to avoid the use of atogepant in patients with severe HI.
L752: In conclusion, the review team recommends that no dose adjustment is needed in patients with mild or moderate HI and avoid use in patients with severe HI.
L753: Renal Impairment
L754: Renal elimination is a minor excretion pathway for atogepant (5% excreted in urine as unchanged) and the Applicant did not conduct a dedicated renal impairment (RI) study. The population PK analysis using pooled PK data from clinical studies CGP MD-01 and 3101-301-002 did not reveal a significant difference in the PK of atogepant in patients with mild (creatinine clearance [CLCr] 60 to 89 mL/min, N=161) or moderate (CLCr 30 to 59 mL/min, N=13) RI, relative to those with normal renal function (CLCr &gt;90 mL/min, N=1,182). Therefore, the Applicant proposed that no dose adjustment is needed in subjects with mild or moderate RI. Based on a lack of a clinically relevant impact of mild and moderate RI on atogepant exposures, the review team agrees with the Applicant's labeling proposal to not adjust doses in subjects with mild or moderate RI.
L755: There were no subjects with CLCr less than 30 mL/min in the population PK analysis dataset. The Applicant used a physiologically based pharmacokinetics (PBPK) model to predict atogepant exposure in subjects with severe RI and proposed atogepant (b) (4)
L756: However, owing to concerns related to the reliability of the predictive performance of PBPK modeling in the RI population, the review team does not agree with relying PBPK predictions to guide dose adjustment in patients with severe RI and ESRD at this time. The review team recommends dose adjustment in patient with severe RI (CLCr 15 to 29 mL/min) and ESRD (CLCr &lt;15 mL/min) as 10 mg QD. The key considerations to support this recommendation include: (1) renal excretion is a minor route for atogepant elimination, as noted above; (2) RI of any degree is not expected to significantly affect exposures and the population PK analysis confirmed the minimal impact on atogepant exposure in patients with mild or moderate RI; (3) Even if there is some increase in exposures in subjects with CLCr less than 30 mL/min, there is over 6-fold margin (as noted above) with the lowest dose recommended (i.e., 10 mg); (4) there is no clear dose-/exposure-response relationship for efficacy in the atogepant dose range of 10 mg QD to 60 mg BID; and (5) if RI affects hepatic function, the observed increase in total and unbound AUC in severe HI is 1.38 and 3.66-fold, respectively. Even under such a worst-case scenario using unbound exposures, the 10 mg dose can be adequately justified. For ESRD patients with
L757: intermittent dialysis, we recommend dose adjustment to 10 mg QD. Because atogepant is a highly protein bound drug (&gt;95% protein binding), dialysis may not have a significant impact on atogepant exposures. As a cautionary measure, the review team suggests taking atogepant after dialysis session in such patients.
L758: In conclusion, the review team agrees with the Applicant's labeling proposal to have no dose adjustment for patients with mild and moderate RI, and recommends a lower dose of 10 mg QD in patients with CLcr less than 30 mL/min. For patients with ESRD undergoing intermittent dialysis, the review team recommends taking atogepant 10 mg QD after dialysis.
L759: Other Intrinsic Factors
L760: The population PK analysis indicated that sex, race, age, and body weight do not have a clinically relevant effect on atogepant PK. Therefore, no dose adjustments are warranted based on these factors (Section 14.3).
L761: 8.2. Extrinsic Factors
L762: 8.2.1. Food Effect
L763: Study 3101-105-002 evaluated the impact of a high-fat meal (total calories of approximately 800 to 1,000, derived approximately 150 calories from protein, 250 calories from carbohydrates, and 500 to 600 calories from fat) on atogepant PK. The geometric mean values of AUC and Cmax were reduced by 18% and 22%, respectively, while the median time to maximum concentration was unaffected. Atogepant was administered without regard to food in Phase 2 and 3 studies (CGP MD-01 and 3101-301-002). Since administration of atogepant with a high-fat meal did not reveal clinically relevant effects on atogepant PK, the review team agrees with the Applicant's proposed labeling that atogepant can be taken without regard to food-intake (see Section 14.2).
L764: 8.3. Drug Interactions
L765: Key labeling recommendations based on drug interactions are summarized below.
L766: Table 46. Key Labeling Recommendations
L767: Concomitant DrugsRecommended Dose(s)Strong CYP3A4 inhibitors10 mgWeak/moderate CYP3A4 inhibitorsNo dose adjustments necessary. Follow general dosing scheme.Strong CYP3A4 inducers30 mg or 60 mgModerate CYP3A4 inducers30 mg or 60 mgWeak CYP3A4 inducersNo dose adjustments necessary. Follow general dosing scheme.OATP inhibitors10 mg or 30 mg
L768: Source: Reviewer analysis
L769: Abbreviations: OATP, organic anion transporting polypeptide
L770: 8.3.1. Effects of Other Drugs on Atogepant
L771: Atogepant is predominately metabolized by CYP3A4 through oxidation. Atogepant is a substrate of P-glycoprotein (P-gp), breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP), organic anion transporting polypeptide (OATP) 1B1, OATP1B3, and OAT1 transporters.
L772: The Applicant investigated the effects of a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor, a strong CYP3A4 inducer, an OATP inhibitor, and a P-gp inhibitor on atogepant PK in clinical drug-drug interaction (DDI) studies. The Applicant also evaluated the effects of a moderate or weak CYP3A4 inhibitor, and a BCRP inhibitor on atogepant PK via PBPK modeling and simulation.
L773: CYP3A4 Inhibitors
L774: In a clinical DDI study (CGP-PK-02), coadministration of atogepant with itraconazole, a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor, resulted in a 5.5-fold increase in atogepant AUC_{0-inf} and a 2.2-fold increase in C_{max}. Atogepant dose should be reduced to 10 mg when administered concomitantly with strong CYP3A4 inhibitors (see Section 14.2).
L775: The Applicant used a PBPK model to predict the effects of moderate and weak CYP3A4 inhibitors on atogepant PK. The PBPK model predicted that concomitant administration of 60 mg atogepant with fluconazole, a moderate CYP3A4 inhibitor, would result in increased AUC and C_{max} by 68% and 21%, respectively. Concomitant administration with cimetidine, a weak CYP3A4 inhibitor, is predicted to increase AUC and C_{max} by 8% and 5%, respectively. The review team agrees with the Applicant's PBPK prediction for effects of concomitant administration of fluconazole and cimetidine on atogepant PK and these changes are not considered to be clinically relevant. Therefore, no dose adjustment is needed with concomitant use of moderate or weak CYP3A4 inhibitors (see Section 14.4).
L776: CYP3A4 Inducers
L777: In a clinical DDI study (CGP-PK-12), coadministration of atogepant after multiple doses of rifampin resulted in a 60% reduction in atogepant AUC_{0-inf} and a 30% reduction in C_{max}. Rifampin, in addition to its effects in inducing CYP3A4, also affects other transporter pathways such as OATPs, P-gp etc. As noted above, atogepant is a substrate of CYP3A4, OATP1B1/1B3 and P-gp. Therefore, the impact of concomitant administration of atogepant after multiple doses of rifampin is likely a composite interaction mediated by induction of CYP3A4, inhibition of OATP1B1/1B3, induction of OATPs, and induction of P-gp. Although the induction potential of rifampin on OATP1B1/1B3 cannot be ruled out, there is uncertainty on the magnitude of the impact of OATP1B1/1B3 induction on atogepant exposures. The review team conducted PBPK simulations for multiple scenarios to evaluate the impact of different combinations of DDI mechanisms on atogepant PK after multiple doses of rifampin. These simulations indicated that the net effect of rifampin (at steady state) on OATP1B1/1B3 is not expected to be significant. PBPK simulations on rifampin's impact on CYP3A4 induction alone indicated that atogepant AUC ratio (relative to controls) was 0.63 (refer to Section 14.4). Given the lack of clear dose-exposure-response relationship for efficacy in the dose range of 10 mg QD to 60 mg BID, the review team recommends adjusting atogepant doses to 30 mg or 60 mg with concomitant use of strong CYP3A4 inducers. (see Section 14.4).
L778: Coadministration of atogepant with moderate CYP3A4 inducers was not evaluated. The magnitude of interaction with concomitant administration of moderate CYP3A4 inducers on atogepant is expected be lower than that noted with strong CYP3A4 inducers. Therefore, the review team recommends the same dose adjustment strategy when administering atogepant with moderate CYP3A4 inducers as with strong CYP3A4 inducers.
L779: Coadministration of atogepant with weak CYP3A4 inducers was not evaluated. According to the FDA Guidance for Clinical Drug Interaction Studies,3 a weak inducer is expected to decrease the AUC of a sensitive index CYP substrate by \geq 20\% to &lt; 50\%. These changes are not considered to have a clinically relevant impact because of a lack of a clear dose-/exposure-response relationship for efficacy in the range of 10 mg QD to 60 mg BID. Therefore, no dose adjustment is needed when coadministering weak CYP3A4 inducers.
L780: OATP, P-gp, and BCRP inhibitors
L781: In a clinical DDI study (CGP-PK-12), coadministration of atogepant with a single dose of rifampin resulted in a 2.9-fold increase in atogepant AUC_{0-inf} and a 2.2-fold increase in C_{max}. When rifampin was administered at single dose, rifampin's effect on atogepant PK is likely to be predominately mediated by inhibition on OATP (refer to Section 14.4). Therefore, the review team recommends adjusting atogepant doses to 10 mg or 30 mg with concomitant use of OATP inhibitors (see Section 14.2).
L782: In a clinical DDI study (3101-103-002), coadministration of atogepant with quinidine, a P-gp inhibitor, resulted in a 1.3-fold increase in atogepant AUC_{0-inf} and a 1.04-fold increase in C_{max}. Results of PBPK simulations showed that coadministration of atogepant with a BCRP inhibitor would result in a 1.2-fold increase of atogepant AUC_{0-inf} and a 1.3-fold increase in C_{max}. Therefore, no dose adjustment is needed when coadministering with P-gp and BCRP inhibitors.
L783: Other Drugs for Migraine Treatment
L784: In clinical DDI studies conducted with commonly coadministered drugs for migraine treatment, e.g., sumatriptan (Study CGP-PK-13), naproxen (Study CGP-PK-06), and acetaminophen (Study CGP-PK-06), no clinically relevant interactions were observed. Therefore, no dose adjustment is needed when coadministering with sumatriptan, naproxen, and acetaminophen.
L785: Drugs that Increase Gastric pH
L786: In clinical DDI studies with esomeprazole (Study 3101-102-002), a proton pump inhibitor, and famotidine (Study MK-8031 P008), a H2 receptor blocker, no clinically relevant interactions were observed. Therefore, no dose adjustment is needed when coadministering with pH-altering agents.
L787: 8.4. Effects of Atogepant on Other Drugs
L788: 8.4.1. Oral Contraceptives
L789: In a clinical DDI study (Study MK-8031-P005), coadministration of multiple doses of atogepant with a combined oral contraceptive containing ethinyl estradiol (EE) and levonorgestrel (LNG) did not result in clinically relevant changes in EE or LNG exposures (C_{max} and AUC). Therefore, no dose adjustments in combined oral contraceptives are needed when coadministered with atogepant.
L790: 3 We update guidances periodically. For the most recent version of a guidance, check the FDA guidance web page at https://www.fda.gov/regulatory-information/search-fda-guidance-documents.
L791: 8.5. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
L792: The initial approval of atogepant for the preventive treatment of episodic migraine will be limited to adults. This development program triggers the Pediatric Research and Equity Act, therefore pediatric postmarketing studies to evaluate safety and efficacy of atogepant in pediatric migraine patients will be required. Please refer to Section 22 of this review for details.
L793: 8.6. Pregnancy and Lactation
L794: Due to the lack of information on pregnancy and lactation outcomes for atogepant-treated patients, postmarketing studies will be required for a pregnancy registry study and a pregnancy outcomes study. Please see Section 22 of this review for details.
L795: Oral administration of atogepant (0, 5, 20, or 125 mg/kg/day) to male and female rats prior to and during mating and continuing in females to Gestation Day 7 resulted in no adverse effects on fertility or reproductive performance. Plasma exposures (AUC) at the highest dose tested (125 mg/kg/day) were approximately 15 times that in humans at the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) of 60 mg/day.
L796: Oral administration of atogepant (0, 5, 15, 125, or 750 mg/kg/day) to pregnant rats during the period of organogenesis resulted in decreases in fetal body weight and skeletal ossification at the two highest doses. At the no-effect dose (15 mg/kg/day) for adverse effects on embryofetal development in rats, plasma exposure (AUC) was approximately 4 times that in humans at the MRHD.
L797: Oral administration of atogepant (0, 30, 90, or 130 mg/kg/day) to pregnant rabbits during the period of organogenesis resulted in an increase in fetal visceral and skeletal variations at the highest dose tested, which was associated with minimal maternal toxicity. At the no-effect dose (90 mg/kg/day) for adverse effects on embryofetal development in rabbits, plasma exposure (AUC) was approximately 3 times that in humans at the MRHD.
L798: Oral administration of atogepant (0, 15, 45, or 125 mg/kg/day) to rats throughout gestation and lactation resulted in decreased pup body weight at the highest dose, which persisted into adulthood. At the no-effect dose (45 mg/kg/day) for adverse effects on pre- and postnatal development, plasma exposure (AUC) was approximately 5 times that in humans at the MRHD.
L799: 9. Product Quality
L800: The Office of Pharmaceutical Quality Review team has assessed NDA 215206 with respect to Chemistry, Manufacturing, and Controls (CMC) and has determined that it meets all applicable standards to support the identity, strength, quality, and purity that it purports. As such OPQ recommends approval of this NDA from a quality perspective.

L802: Not applicable.
L803: 10. Human Subjects Protections/Clinical Site and Other Good Clinical Practice Inspections/Financial Disclosure
