Review of the financial disclosures did not raise any concerns about the validity or reliability of the data. Please see Section 20 for a summary of inspection findings and Section 23 for financial disclosures.
L805: 11. Advisory Committee Summary
L806: This application was not referred to an FDA advisory committee because the clinical trial design was acceptable, the efficacy findings were clear, and the safety profile was acceptable for use in the intended population.
L807: III. Appendices
L808: 12. Summary of Regulatory History
L809: On June 5, 2012, Merck Sharp and Dohme Corp., a subsidiary of Merck and Co., Inc., submitted investigational new drug (IND) application 114780 to study MK-8031 (atogepant) for the acute treatment of migraine. Initial Agency reviews described MK-8031 as a selective antagonist of the neuropeptide transmitter, calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP).
L810: The Division of Neurology Products concluded that the Sponsor may proceed with its proposed clinical investigation for the treatment of migraine on July 5, 2012. The Division determined that safety and tolerability, as the primary endpoint, should be assessed based on clinical evaluation with monitoring of elevation of hepatic transaminases, mainly serum alanine aminotransferase as increases in alanine aminotransferase have been observed with a number of CGRP receptor antagonists.
L811: In an email dated February 11, 2013, the Sponsor notified the Division of its intent to develop MK-8031 for the treatment of migraine prophylaxis, rather than for the acute treatment of migraine.
L812: On August 24, 2015, the application underwent a change of sponsorship from Merck Sharp and Dohme Corp. to Aptalis Pharma Ltd. On July 21, 2016, the Division was notified of a change in Sponsor from Aptalis to Allergan Pharmaceuticals International Limited, with Allergan Sales, LLC. as the Sponsor's United States Agent contact.
L813: The Sponsor submitted Special Protocol Assessment (SPA) requests for its proposed rat (CGP-TX-06, SPA-2) and mouse (CGP-TX-05, SPA-3) carcinogenicity studies on March 10, 2017. An agreement letter was issued for the SPA-2 and a no agreement letter for SPA-3 on April 14, 2017. The Executive Carcinogenicity Assessment Committee was unable to concur on doses for females because the 3-month study did not identify a maximum tolerated dose or a dose that produced plasma exposures (area under the plasma concentration-time curve, AUC) 25-fold above that at the maximum human dose.
L814: A Type C guidance (teleconference) meeting between the Division and Allergan took place on June 27, 2017, to discuss nonclinical and clinical development plans. The Sponsor's intent for this meeting was to obtain feedback from the Division regarding the adequacy and potential for its planned study, Study CGP-MD-01, titled "A Phase 2/3, Multicenter, Randomized, Double-Blind, Placebo-Controlled, Parallel-Group Study to Evaluate the Efficacy, Safety, and Tolerability of Multiple Dosing Regimens of Oral AGN-241689 in Episodic Migraine Prevention," to be used as a pivotal trial. The Division stated that Study CGP-MD-01 was inadequate to be a pivotal trial. At the time of this meeting, the proposed trial's statistical testing procedure allows for the possibility of statistical significance to occur at a low dose when a high dose may not demonstrate statistical significance. The Division stated that the results of the study could be uninterpretable if the high dose does not meet statistical significance when the low dose does. Additionally, the Division recommended that the primary endpoint be analyzed based on a comparison of the change from baseline in the number of migraine headache days over the entire double-blind treatment period rather than only the last four weeks of the treatment period, as proposed. The Division advised Allergan to provide justification for excluding patients
L815: with hemiplegic migraine, or to remove the exclusion criteria if no data exist to support increased risk to patients with this diagnosis.
L816: The Division concluded that a drug discrimination study with atogepant was not necessary; however, it requested that the Sponsor conduct a self-administration study in rats.
L817: Following the June 2017 meeting, Allergan submitted a revised protocol to Study CGP-MD-01, and a revised Statistical Analysis Plan in response to the Division's feedback. The subsequent changes made to both the protocol and Statistical Analysis Plan adequately addressed the Division's concerns regarding the proposed analysis of the primary endpoint, the addition of a secondary endpoint to assess the use of rescue medication, the Sponsor's overall study design, and the plan for testing the proposed doses.
L818: In a submission dated June 29, 2018, the Sponsor requested a meeting to discuss the suitability of patient-reported outcome instruments for atogepant clinical studies. In the FDA preliminary meeting comments, the Division expressed concerns regarding the lack of definition for "headache," and the lack of clear instructions for patients in the instructions for the Sponsor's novel patient-reported outcome, the Activity Impairment in Migraine Diary (AIM-D). The Division stated that it was unclear how the Sponsor planned to analyze AIM-D scores during headache and nonheadache days. The Division provided additional recommendations concerning the firm's proposed psychometric study protocol synopsis and statistical plan. The Sponsor canceled the meeting, indicating that the preliminary comments sent by the Division were sufficiently clear and complete. The Division issued Final Written Responses to Allergan on September 11, 2018.
L819: An End of Phase 2 meeting was held on November 19, 2018, to discuss the Sponsor's Phase 3 clinical development plan for the (b) (4). In the November 14, 2018, meeting preliminary comments, the Division advised the Sponsor to consider including the 10 mg dose in the Phase 3 study, as this dose may provide a better safety profile compared to the higher doses selected. Additionally, the Division recommended that the primary efficacy analysis be calculated based on the 3-month average of the mean monthly migraine days rather than using the mixed effects model for repeated measures. The Division stated that the rationale for the (b) (4)
L820: (b) (4) The Division recommended the inclusion of multiple anchor scales to provide several assessments to assist in determining what may be a clinically meaningful within-patient change in the AIM-D scores from the patient perspective. Specifically, the Division recommended the addition of a patient global impression of severity scale in addition to a 5-point patient global impression of change scale.
L821: In addition, the Division acknowledged and accepted the Sponsor's preference to use the 7-point patient global impression of change scale over a 5-point scale, based on patients' preferences, and to keep the patient global impression of change scale consistent with the version used in their Phase 2 trial. The Division acknowledged Allergan's concerns about twice daily (BID) dosing, but suggested that if higher efficacy for a lower dose given BID versus once daily (QD) was demonstrated, the benefit of the improved efficacy may balance out the inconvenience of BID dosing, and that it may be worthwhile to continue to explore BID dosing, specifically 10 mg BID
L822: NDA 215206
L823: Qulipta (atogepant)
L824: or 30 mg BID. The Division issued the End of Phase 2 meeting minutes to Allergan on December 18, 2018.
L825: An End of Phase 2 chemistry, manufacturing, and controls meeting was requested on February 19, 2019, granted on March 8, 2019, and preliminary comments issued on April 11, 2019. The Sponsor found these responses to be acceptable and the meeting scheduled for April 15, 2019, was cancelled.
L826: An Initial Agreement letter was issued on August 15, 2019, for the Sponsor's proposed pediatric study plan (b) (4)
L827: On October 23, 2019, the Sponsor requested a meeting to discuss considerations for their planned new drug application (NDA). A Type C meeting was granted on November 13, 2019. Discussion at this meeting focused on questions regarding the appropriateness of the pivotal studies, and the AIM-D, and the Migraine-Specific Quality of Life Questionnaire Version 2.1. FDA Written Responses were issued to the Sponsor on January 6, 2020.
L828: The Sponsor requested a chemistry, manufacturing, and controls-focused meeting on December 19, 2019, to obtain FDA agreement regarding its proposed control and manufacture strategy of the atogepant drug substance and adequacy the drug product's dissolution method in the NDA. A Type C Guidance meeting was granted on January 10, 2020, and written responses issued on February 25, 2020.
L829: A Type B preNDA meeting to discuss the Sponsor's incoming NDA was held on September 22, 2020. During this meeting, the Sponsor was advised to include exposure of atogepant of at least 300 patients at 6 months and 100 patients at 1 year, with a substantial number of patients exposed at the highest planned marketed dose at 1 year. The Division determined that the receptor binding studies, nonclinical data, and abuse-related adverse event profile assessment did not suggest an abuse potential signal associated with atogepant. The Division stated that a human abuse potential study and clinical physical dependence study were not required for the proposed NDA. Allergan agreed to include tables for each Phase 2 and Phase 3 study which specify the number of unique patients for each dose level with duration of exposure, and to include safety analyses to evaluate whether there was hepatotoxicity associated with atogepant.
L830: Allergan and its subsidiaries were acquired by AbbVie Inc. in May 2020. AbbVie sent a Notification of Intent to submit an NDA with a Material Threat Medical Countermeasure Priority Review Voucher on October 30, 2020.
L831: 13. Pharmacology Toxicology: Additional Information and Assessment
L832: 13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted Under the Investigational New Drug Application
L833: 13.1.1. Pharmacology
L834: Atogepant is a calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP) receptor antagonist, with activity at human and monkey CGRP receptors at low picomolar concentrations, and mouse, rat, rabbit, and dog CGRP receptors at low nanomolar concentrations. In vivo, atogepant inhibited capsaicin-induced dermal vasodilation up to 90% at nanomolar concentrations in monkeys and approximately 45% at micromolar concentrations in rat and rabbit.
L835: In secondary pharmacology assays, atogepant demonstrated low micromolar binding affinity at CGRP-related receptors (AMY1 and AMY3), but only weak binding affinity at 2 of 116 receptors, enzymes, and ion channel assays (half inhibitory concentration &gt;20\muM).
L836: 13.1.2. Safety Pharmacology
L837: A complete battery of safety pharmacology (CNS, cardiovascular, and respiratory) endpoints was adequately assessed and showed no safety concerns.
L838: 13.1.3. PK/ADME
L839: In rats and monkeys following IV or PO administration, the half-life was between 1 and 4 hours, oral bioavailability was 27%, and plasma protein binding was 92%. 14C-atogepant (total radioactivity) was eliminated in the urine (~9%), bile (~55%), and feces (~18%). Peak tissue concentrations in rat were as follows (highest to lowest concentration): GI tract &gt; liver &gt; bile duct &gt; salivary gland (submaxillary). Oxidative metabolism primarily occurred by CYP3A4. Atogepant was a substrate for membrane-bound efflux transporters P-gp and BCRP, hepatic uptake transporters OATP1B1 and OATP1B3, and the renal uptake transporter, OAT1. There were no major or unique circulating metabolites in humans.
L840: 13.1.4. General Toxicology
L841: The toxicologic and toxicokinetic profile of orally administered atogepant was assessed in a series of single and repeat dose studies in rats and monkeys up to 6- and 9-months' duration, respectively. At the high dose (HD) in the 14-day (250 mg/kg) and 3-month (200 mg/kg) toxicology studies in rat, there were body weight decreases of 23 and 16%, respectively, and vacuolation in the small intestine. In the 3-month rat (10/sex/group) study (0, 5, 20, or 200 mg/kg), the no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) was the mid dose (MD) based on a 16% decrease in body weight gain at the HD. In the pivotal 6-month study in rat (15/sex/group; 0, 10, 30, or 100 mg/kg), there were non-adverse findings consisting of decreases in body weight of up to 10% at the HD, which correlated with a decrease in food consumption. These findings partially resolved during the recovery period. There was also minimum to marked vacuolation in
L842: the villous epithelium of the small intestine in MD and HD animals, which resolved during the recovery period. The HD was justified based on decreases in body weight gain at \geq 200 mg/kg in the 14-day and 3-month studies. The safety margin based on plasma AUC at the NOAEL of 100 mg/kg was 27 times relative to that at the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) of 60 mg. Plasma AUC0-t generally increased dose proportionally.
L843: In the single dose (up to 1000 mg/kg) studies in monkey, vomiting was observed at 150 and 300 mg/kg and liquid feces was observed at 300 mg/kg. In the pivotal 9-month oral toxicology study in monkey (4/sex/group, 0, 15, 40, or 300 mg/kg) there were no drug-related findings. Plasma Cmax and AUC0-24 h increased dose proportionally from the low dose (LD) to MD and less than dose proportionally from the MD to HD. The NOAEL was the HD. The study was considered adequate based on the vomiting observed at the 150 and 300 mg/kg doses in the single dose studies. At the HD (300 mg/kg) in the 9-month study, the safety margin was 10-fold compared to that at the MRHD based on plasma AUC at the NOAEL.
L844: 13.1.5. Genetic Toxicology
L845: Atogepant was negative in Organisation for Economic Cooperation and Development-compliant Ames, in vitro chromosomal aberration, and in vivo rat bone marrow micronucleus assays.
L846: 13.1.6. Carcinogenicity
L847: Based on the FDA statistical analysis of the 2-year mouse and rat carcinogenicity studies, atogepant was not carcinogenic. The study in female mice was not adequate because the HD was not adequately justified; it was not demonstrated to be a maximum tolerated dose or maximum feasible dose and did not achieve plasma exposures at least 25-fold that at in humans at the MRHD.

L852: 13.1.7. Reproductive and Developmental Toxicology
L853: A fertility study in male and female rats (20/sex/group) administered oral doses of 0, 5, 20, or 125 mg/kg (QD) for 15 days prior to cohabitation, showed no effects on the testes, sperm motility or number, pregnancy status, corpora lutea, uterine implants, live or dead fetuses, or resorptions. The HD was justified based on decreases in body weight gain at \geq 200 mg/kg in the 14-day and 3-month studies. No toxicokinetic data were collected. (The exposure data from the rat embryo-fetal development study was used to calculate margins.) At the high dose, plasma exposure (AUC) was approximately 15 times that in humans at the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) of 60 mg/day.
L854: In a preliminary oral embryo-fetal development study in rat (12/group), animals were orally administered doses of 0, 5, 20, 250, or 750 mg/kg (QD) on gestation days (GDs) 6 through 20. Findings consisted of decreased fetal body weight (11-14%) at 250 and 750 mg/kg. There were
L855: no effects on fetal viability, weight, sex ratio, and external or visceral morphology. In the pivotal study, doses of 0, 5, 15, 125, or 750 mg/kg (PO) was administered QD (20/group) on GDs 6 through 20. There were no adverse findings in the dam. The fetal NOAEL was 15 mg/kg based on increases in total resorptions and post-implantation loss (125 and 750 mg/kg) and incomplete ossification (125 and 750 mg/kg). Exposure increased greater than dose proportionally between 5 and 125 mg/kg and decreased between 125 and 750 mg/kg. At the no-effect dose (15 mg/kg), plasma exposure (AUC) was approximately 4 times that at the MRHD.
L856: In the preliminary embryofetal development study in rabbits (12/group), animals were orally administered doses of 0, 15, 45, 100, or 325 mg/kg (QD) on GDs 7 through 20. There was a 6% decrease in maternal body weight gain at the high dose. At necropsy (GD 28) corpora lutea, gross morphology, fetal viability and weight, sex, and fetal external and visceral morphology were assessed. At the HD, there were decreases in male and female fetal weight of 10 and 14%, respectively, compared to control. In the pivotal study, oral doses of 0, 30, 90, or 130 mg/kg (23/group) were administered daily on GDs 7 through 20 and the necropsy was conducted on GD 28. Maternal body weight loss (18%) was observed at the HD. The fetal NOAEL was the MD based on an increase in skeletal and visceral variations at the HD. Plasma exposure (AUC) at the NOAEL for adverse development effects was approximately 3 times that in humans at the MRHD.
L857: In the pre and postnatal development study, rats (21/group) were orally administered doses of 0, 15, 45 or 125 mg/kg (QD) from GD 6 through Lactation Day 20. The NOAEL was at the MD based on a decrease (8-13%) in pup body weight that persisted into adulthood. Toxicokinetic data were not collected. (The data from the rat embryofetal development study were used to estimate plasma exposure at 45 mg/kg.) Plasma exposure (AUC) at the NOAEL for adverse effects on pre- and postnatal development was approximately 5 times that in humans at the MRHD.

L876: Observations and Results
L877: Mortality
L878: Assessments were made twice daily.
L879: There were no unscheduled deaths.
L880: Clinical Signs
L881: Detailed observations were made weekly.
L882: Wet fur on the lower jaw was observed in all AGN-241689 groups.
L883: Body Weights
L884: Recordings were made weekly.
L885: Body weight and overall body weight gain were reduced (1 to 9% and 10%, respectively) in HD males. In females, body weight was reduced at the MD and HD (2 to 6% and 1 to 6%, respectively) and body weight gain was reduced (11%) at both doses. During the recovery period, body weight normalized in males but remained lower (8%) in females compared to controls.
L886: Food Consumption
L887: Recordings were made weekly.
L888: Food consumption was reduced (up to 14%) at the MD and HD in males and females. Recovery was observed in males, and partial recovery was observed in females.

L1109: Mortality and Clinical Signs
L1110: Animals were observed once daily.
L1111: There were no drug-related deaths or clinical signs observed.
L1112: Body Weights
L1113: Body weights were measured during pretest and then weekly during the dosing period (Figure 18 and Figure 19).
L1114: In males, there was a trend for reduced body weight gain, compared to controls, over the 13-week period in the 20 and 100 mg/kg groups, and from Weeks 4 through 13 in the 5 mg/kg/day group. By Week 13, in males, body weight gain decreases at 20 mg/kg were between 8 and 13% (not dose-related) and the decrease at 100 mg/kg was 21% (Table 70).
L1115: There was no change in body weight gain compared to controls in females.

Basis of dose selectionMales: the HD was selected based on a 9% decrease in body weight (relative to control) at 100 mg/kg in the 6-month study and a 16% decrease in body weight gain at 200 mg/kg in the 3-month dose-ranging study. Females: the HD was selected based on the TK data in females from the 3-month study in which plasma exposures (AUC) at 200 mg/kg were approximately 25 times that (8µM*hr) at the maximum anticipated clinical dose.

L1315: 13.2.4.2. Embryonic Fetal Development
L1316: 13.2.4.2.1. Study TT #12-7061: Preliminary Oral Embryo-Fetal Developmental Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study in Rats
L1317: Rats were orally administered MK-8031 (0, 5, 20, 250, or 750 mg/kg, QD, 12/group) from GDs 6 through 20. The animals were assessed for mortality, physical signs, body weights, food consumption, and toxicokinetics. On GD 21, animals were euthanized and assessed for number of corpora lutea and gross placental morphology. The following developmental parameters were assessed: fetal viability, fetal weight, sex ratio, and external and visceral morphology. Maternal body weight between GDs 6 and 18 was decreased up to 36% compared to control in the 250 and 750 mg/kg groups. Fetal weights were decreased 11 to 14% in the same groups. Exposure (AUC0-24 h) increased greater than dose proportionally between 5 and 20 mg/kg and less than dose proportionally between 20 and 750 mg/kg (Table 86).

L1375: 13.2.4.2.3. TT #12-7071: Preliminary Oral Embryo-Fetal Developmental Toxicity and Toxicokinetic Study in Rabbits
L1376: Rabbits (12 females per group) were orally administered MK-8031 (0, 15, 45, 100, or 325 mg/kg/day) from GD 7 through 20. At the HD, from GD 6 to 21, there was a 6% decrease in mean maternal body weight gain, compared to control. AUC0-24 h increased greater than dose proportionally from 15 to 100 mg/kg and decrease from 100 to 325 mg/kg (Table 91).

L1510: 13.2.5. Special Toxicology Studies
L1511: Computational modeling of in vitro experiments assessing bile acid transporter inhibition, mitochondrial dysfunction, and oxidative stress suggested atogepant may have a reduced risk of causing hepatotoxicity in humans compared to the predecessor small molecule CGRP receptor antagonists, telcagepant and MK-3207.
L1512: A 3-day phototoxicity study of atogepant (up to 40 mg/kg/day followed by a single 90-minute ultraviolet ray exposure) in 8 female pigmented rats showed no drug-related skin reactions or ocular findings.
L1513: 14. Clinical Pharmacology: Additional Information and Assessment
L1514: 14.1. In Vitro Studies
L1515: 14.1.1. Plasma Protein Binding
L1516: The binding of atogepant to human plasma proteins was determined by equilibrium dialysis. In Study MK-8031 PK003, atogepant at 0.1, 1, and 10\muM was incubated for 4 hours in human plasma and the unbound fraction was determined. The mean unbound fraction ranged from 4 to 6% across all atogepant concentrations.
L1517: In clinical Study CGP-PK-01, plasma protein binding was determined by equilibrium dialysis using plasma samples collected from subjects with different degrees of hepatic impairment. In participants with no, mild, moderate, or severe hepatic impairment administered a single oral dose of 60 mg atogepant, the percentage of unbound atogepant was found to be 1.8%, 2.6%, 2.9%, and 4.7%, respectively.
L1518: 14.1.2. Metabolism of Atogepant
L1519: In Study MK-8031 PK003, the in vitro metabolism of [14C]-atogepant was assessed using rat, monkey, and human liver microsomes and mouse, rat, monkey, and human hepatocytes to compare species-specific metabolism. Metabolites M1 (N-oxide on the azaoxindole moiety) and M2 (5-hydroxylation on the azaoxindole moiety) were found following liver microsome incubation. All human metabolites were observed in mouse, rat, and/or monkey hepatocytes, the preclinical toxicology species.
L1520: In study MK-8031 PK007, circulating metabolites of atogepant in human plasma were characterized using pooled clinical samples from Study MK-8031 P002. With a semi-quantitative Liquid Chromatography-High Resolution Mass Spectrometry method, results showed that atogepant was the predominant circulating component (97.8% of total exposure) and metabolite M1 was a minor circulating metabolite (2.2% of total exposure) with no apparent accumulation following multiple doses of atogepant. M2 was not found in the pooled clinical samples.
L1521: In the clinical mass balance study of Study CGP-PK-03, the only two radiometric peaks detected in plasma were atogepant and metabolite M23, together representing 15.3% of the circulating radioactivity exposure. Characterization of M23 in Study 3101-N02-050 indicated that M23 is
L1522: not an acyl-glucuronide metabolite. Following a single dose of 50 mg atogepant, the AUC of M23 was found to be 3 times lower than that from monkeys dosed with a single dose of atogepant at 10 mg/kg in Study PK002.
L1523: In study MK-8031 PK003, cytochrome P450 (CYP) reaction phenotyping was conducted using human liver microsomes incubated with inhibitory antibodies for CYP3A4/5, 2C, and 2D6 and with recombinant CYP enzymes including CYP1A2, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, and CYP3A4. The results showed that the oxidative metabolism of atogepant occurred almost predominantly via CYP3A4 with minor involvement of CYP2D6.
L1524: 14.1.3. Inhibition and Induction of CYP Isozymes by Atogepant
L1525: Results from in vitro studies showed that atogepant is not anticipated to inhibit CYP enzymes, monoamine oxidase A, or UGT1A1.

However, atogepant is not expected to have induction potential on CYP3A4 at clinically relevant concentrations.

L1543: 14.2. In Vivo Studies
L1544: Mass Balance of Atogepant Following Administration of [14C]-Atogepant (Study CGP-PK-03)
L1545: Study Design
L1546: Under fasting conditions, healthy male subjects (N=6) were administered 50 mg total (~200 μCi) of a 14C-atogepant oral solution.
L1547: Blood samples were collected at predose through 168 hours, and up to Day 29, to measure total radioactivity, atogepant concentration, and metabolite profiling. Urine and fecal samples were collected to measure the levels of atogepant and metabolites, and total radioactivity from predose through up to Day 29, if discharge criteria were met. If discharge criteria were not met, urine and fecal sampling continued beyond Day 29 postdose.
L1548: Results and Conclusion
L1549: • Based on the AUC ratio of atogepant and total radioactivity in plasma, atogepant contributed approximately 75% of the total radioactivity exposure and metabolite M23 contributed approximately 15%.• The total recovery of radioactivity in urine and feces 336 hours after dosing was approximately 89%, with 8% from urine, and 81% from feces.— In urine, atogepant represented approximately 5% of the radioactive dose and was the only peak detected that was greater than 1% of the radioactive dose— In feces, atogepant represented approximately 42% of the radioactive dose. At least 11 metabolites were detected in feces and each represented less than 10% of the radioactive dose.
L1550: Reviewer's Comments: The reviewer agrees with the Applicant's assessments.
L1551: Food Effect of Atogepant (Study 3101-105-002)
L1552: Study Design
L1553: The study was a randomized, open-label, single-dose, 2-period crossover study conducted in 20 healthy subjects.
L1554: Intervention A: Single dose of 60 mg atogepant, hot melt extrudate oral compressed tablet (HME-OCT) formulation, following a high fat and high calorie meal (with a total of approximately 800 to 1,000 calories; approximately 150 calories derived from protein, 250 from carbohydrates, and 500 to 600 from fat) consumed within 30 minutes before dosing.
L1555: Intervention B: Single dose of 60 mg atogepant, HME-OCT formulation, following a 10-hour overnight fast.
L1556: The pharmacokinetics (PK) blood samples were collected starting on Days 1 (Period 1) and 8 (Period 2); at 0 hour (predose) and 0.5, 1, 1.5, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12, 16, 24, 36, and 48 hours postdose.

L1563: Conclusion
L1564: A high-fat, high-calorie meal reduced the atogepant AUC and maximum concentration (Cmax) by 18% and 22%, respectively, while the median time to maximum concentration was unaffected. These changes in exposures are not expected to be clinically relevant.

L1566: Atogepant PK in Subjects With Hepatic Impairment (Study CGP-PK-01)
L1567: Study Design
L1568: Study CGP-PK-01 was a multicenter, nonrandomized, open-label, parallel-group, single-dose study. Participants with normal hepatic function (healthy controls) and subjects with hepatic impairment (mild, moderate, or severe according to the Child-Pugh classification) received a single, oral, 60 mg tablet of atogepant under fasting conditions. A total of 32 subjects were enrolled with 8 subjects for each group based on the hepatic function.
L1569: PK blood samples were collected at predose through 72 hours postdose. Plasma samples were also collected for evaluation of protein binding at predose and two hours postdose.

L1579: Conclusion
L1580: Subjects with mild, moderate, or severe hepatic impairment generally had Cmax of total atogepant comparable to that of healthy controls. The AUC of total atogepant was 14% to 38% higher in subjects with mild, moderate, or severe hepatic impairment compared with subjects with normal hepatic function.
L1581: Unbound atogepant following a single oral dose of 60 mg was 1.8%, 2.6%, 2.9%, and 4.7%, respectively, in subjects with no, mild, moderate, or severe hepatic impairment.
L1582: Reviewer's Comments: The protein binding data obtained from predose and 2 hours postdose subject samples from study CGP-PK-01 showed minimal difference in atogepant protein binding.
L1583: Based on the unbound atogepant % (calculated as: 1-%bound) at 2 hours postdose and PK parameters (AUC and Cmax) of total atogepant, the reviewer performed the statistical comparison of unbound plasma atogepant PK exposures between subjects with mild, moderate, or severe hepatic impairment and those with normal hepatic function. The results showed that the exposure of unbound atogepant increased by 84%, 91%, and 266% in subjects with mild, moderate, or severe hepatic impairment, respectively as compared with those in subjects with normal hepatic function. However, there are two limitations with the analysis on unbound atogepant PK: 1) the method validation for the equilibrium dialysis assay using the rapid equilibrium dialysis was not validated for protein binding analysis of samples from CGP-PK-01; and 2) unbound atogepant PK was characterized based on the protein binding data obtained from two time points, predose and 2 hours postdose, instead of multiple time points covering the PK profile. Therefore, the unbound atogepant data should be interpreted as supportive information.

L1588: Drug-Drug Interaction: Effect of Itraconazole on Atogepant PK (Study CGP-PK-02)
L1589: Study Design
L1590: Study CGP-PK-02 used a fixed-sequence, open-label, single-sequence design to assess the effect of itraconazole, a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor, on the PK of atogepant in healthy subjects. A 7-day washout period was used between treatments.
L1591: Treatment A: Single oral 60 mg dose of atogepant under fasted conditions on Day 1.
L1592: Treatment B: On Days 8 to 14, 200 mg itraconazole was taken orally once daily under fed conditions; on Day 15, 200 mg itraconazole was co-administered with 60 mg atogepant under fasted conditions; on Days 16 to 17, 200 mg itraconazole was taken once daily under fed conditions.
L1593: PK blood samples were collected at predose through 72 hours postdose on Day 1 and Day 15 to determine atogepant concentration in plasma. Blood samples were also collected on Day 1 at 2, 4, 8, 12, and 24 hours postdose to compare atogepant concentrations determined in Dry Blood Spot analysis. Blood samples were taken at predose on Day 8, 13, 14, and 15 to determine itraconazole plasma concentration.

L1602: Conclusion
L1603: The Cmax and AUC of atogepant increased by 2.15-fold and 5.5-fold, respectively, following co-administration of atogepant with itraconazole, a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor.
L1604: Reviewer's Comments: The reviewer agrees with the Applicant's assessments.
L1605: Drug-Drug Interaction: Effect of Single and Multiple Dose Rifampin on PK of Atogepant (Study CGP-PK-12)
L1606: Study Design
L1607: Study CGP-PK-12 was an open-label, 2-treatment, 2-period, single-sequence, nonrandomized, crossover interaction study conducted in healthy subjects. Rifampin is an inducer of both CYP3A4 and P-glycoprotein (P-gp), and an inhibitor of organic anion transporting polypeptides (OATPs). All subjects received the following treatments with a sequence of A, B1, and B2 as described below:
L1608: Treatment A (atogepant alone): A single dose of 60 mg atogepant was administered orally on Day 1.
L1609: Treatment B1 (Co-administration of atogepant with a single dose of rifampin): A single dose of 60 mg atogepant was co-administered with 600 mg rifampin (2 \times 300 mg capsules) on Day 7, followed by 600 mg once daily, rifampin alone, on Days 8, 9, 10, and 11.
L1610: Treatment B2 (Co-administration of atogepant after multiple doses of rifampin): A single dose of 60 mg of atogepant was co-administered with 600 mg rifampin on Day 12, followed by 600 mg rifampin alone on Day 13.
L1611: On Days 1, 7, and 12, atogepant, either alone or co-administration with rifampin, was administered under fasting conditions. When rifampin was administered alone (on Days 8, 9, 10, 11, and 13), subjects were not allowed to take food for 1 hour before and 1 hour after dosing.
L1612: Blood samples were collected at predose through 48 hours and postdose on Days 1 and 12. On Day 7, blood samples were collected at predose through 24 hours postdose. Blood samples at predose were collected on Days 11 and 12 to determine rifampin concentration.

L1624: Conclusion
L1625: Co-administration of a single dose of atogepant with a single dose of rifampin resulted in increases in atogepant C_{max} and AUC by 2.85-fold and 2.23-fold, respectively.
L1626: Co-administration of a single dose of atogepant after multiple doses of rifampin resulted in decreases in atogepant C_{max} and AUC by 30% and 60%, respectively.
L1627: Reviewer's Comments: The drug-drug interaction (DDI) of rifampin with atogepant is mediated via multiple mechanisms. When rifampin was administered as a single dose, rifampin's effect on atogepant PK is likely to be predominantly mediated by inhibition of OATP. When rifampin was administered at multiple doses, rifampin's effect on atogepant PK is likely a composite interaction mediated by induction of CYP3A4, inhibition of OATPs, induction of OATPs, and induction of P-gp. Refer to Sections 8.2 and 14.4 for detailed discussion. Statistical comparison of atogepant PK exposure between Treatment B1 and Treatment A did not include the statistical analysis for AUC_{0-inf}. Since AUC_{extrapolated} contributed to only a small percentage (less than 5%), the conclusions based on the AUC_{0-24h} values are acceptable.
L1628: 14.3. Pharmacometrics Review
L1629: 14.3.1. Summary of Findings
L1630: 14.3.1.1. Key Review Question: Do Any of the Factors, Including Age, Renal Function (Mild and Moderate Renal Impairment), Sex, Race, and Body Weight, Affect the Pharmacokinetics Profile of Atogepant?
L1631: No. Based on population PK analysis, none of the factors, including age, renal function (mild and moderate renal impairment), sex, race, and body weight, affect the pharmacokinetics profile of atogepant.
L1632: 14.3.2. Label Statements
L1633: 8.5 Geriatric Use
L1634: Population pharmacokinetic modeling suggests no significant pharmacokinetic differences between elderly and younger subjects. Clinical studies of QULIPTA did not include sufficient numbers of patients aged 65 years and over to determine whether they respond differently from younger patients. In general, dose selection for an elderly patient should be cautious, usually starting at the low end of the dosing range reflecting the greater frequency of decreased hepatic, renal, or cardiac function, and of concomitant disease or other drug therapy.
L1635: 12.3 Specific Populations
L1636: Patients with Renal Impairment
L1637: The renal route of elimination plays a minor role in the clearance of atogepant. Based on a population pharmacokinetic analysis, there is no significant difference in the pharmacokinetics of atogepant in patients with mild or moderate renal impairment (CL_{cr} 30-89 mL/min) relative to those with normal renal function (CL_{cr} &gt;90 mL/min). As patients with severe renal impairment
L1638: or end-stage renal disease (ESRD; CLcr &lt;30 mL/min) have not been studied, use of the lowest effective dosage of atogepant (10 mg) is recommended in those patients
L1639: Other Specific Populations
L1640: Based on a population pharmacokinetic analysis, age, sex, race, and body weight did not have a significant effect on the pharmacokinetics (C_{max} and AUC) of atogepant. Therefore, no dose adjustments are warranted based on these factors.
L1641: Reviewer's Comment: We agree with the label statement.
L1642: 14.3.3. Pertinent Regulatory Background
L1643: Atogepant is a potent, selective, orally administered, CGRP receptor antagonist in development for the preventive treatment of migraine in adults with &lt;15 migraine days per month. The proposed dose regimens are:
L1644: • 30 mg or 60 mg once daily• Severe renal impairment: 10 mg (b) (4) once daily• End-stage renal disease: 10 mg once daily
L1645: 14.3.4. Results of Applicant's Analysis
L1646: A dataset composed of 11,763 observations from 1,356 patients was used to develop the final population pharmacokinetic (PopPK) model. The overall PK profile was best described by a three-compartment model with a sequential zero-/first-order lag time absorption model and linear elimination from the central compartment. Formulation, dose, food status, liver function, concomitant medication and body weight were each found to have a statistically significant influence on the atogepant PK.
L1647: Healthy volunteers and patients with migraine shared the same PopPK model parameters, except for apparent clearance, which was 22.9 L/h in healthy volunteers, while it was 23.7% lower in patients with episodic migraines (17.4 L/h). The apparent central volume of distribution was 86.1 L and blood concentrations were predicted to be 57.3% of those in plasma. Median terminal elimination half-lives were comparable between healthy volunteers (8.3 hours) and patients (8.5 hours); the overall median estimate was 8.5 hours. Approximately 69% of the orally bioavailable dose was absorbed through a zero-order process. The derived first-order absorption rate constant estimate was 2.48 1/h. The influence of covariates, including age, renal function, sex, race, and body weight, were not clinically relevant. The tested continuous and categorical covariates over Etas profiles were plotted in Figure 22 and Figure 23, respectively. Continuous covariates, such as creatinine clearance and body weight, have no influence on the PK parameters, including the inter-individual variabilities of bioavailability, apparent clearance), inter-compartmental clearance, duration of zero-order absorption, apparent rapid peripheral volume of distribution, and the inter-occasional variability of bioavailability (Figure 22). The PK parameters after single dose and during steady state were simulated with the final PopPK model (Table 109 and Table 110).

L1665: 14.3.5. Reviewer's Analysis
L1666: 14.3.5.1. Introduction
L1667: Atogepant (also known as AGN-241689 and MK-8031) is a potent, selective, oral calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP) receptor antagonist being developed for preventive treatment of migraine. The proposed dose regimens are: (1) 30 mg or 60 mg once daily for patients with normal renal function, or and mild to moderate renal impairment; (2) 10 mg (b) (4) once daily for patients with severe renal impairment; and (3) 10 mg once daily for patients with end-stage renal disease.
L1668: To support the recommended dose regimen, the Applicant developed a population PK model. The overall PK profile was best described by a three-compartment model with a sequential zero-/first-order lag time absorption model and linear elimination from the central compartment. The factors, including age, renal function (mild and moderate renal impairment), sex, and race, were not significant covariates for PK parameters. Body weight was found to be statistically significant for central volume of distribution, however, with a mild relationship. In this analysis, the reviewers adopted the Applicant's population PK model to estimate the individual atogepant exposure at steady states and confirmed the Applicant's findings.
L1669: The exposure-efficacy relationship was best described by a zero-inflated Poisson model. The treatment effect was comparable and independent of atogepant exposures across the evaluated dose groups ranging from 10 mg QD to 60 mg BID.
L1670: 14.3.5.2. Objectives
L1671: The analysis objective was to evaluate the influence of age, renal function (mild and moderate renal impairment), sex, race, and body weight, on the pharmacokinetics of atogepant
L1672: 14.3.5.3. Methods
L1673: Data Sets
L1674: Data sets used are summarized in Table 111.

L1680: Models

The individual predicted PK parameters were used to estimate the steady state exposure.
L1682: 14.3.5.4. Results
L1683: The goodness-of-fit, dependent variables versus individual predictions, are shown in Figure 24. Part of the observations in the red circle were overestimated by the PopPK model. In individual study goodness-of-fit, this overestimation was identified in a majority of the trials (Figure 25). The reviewers further plotted concentration over time profile of 20 subjects with the top ranked deviations calculated by individual prediction over observation (Figure 26). This plot indicated that the deviation of individual prediction and observation was in the absorption phase. In addition to the PopPK model prediction, the reviewer team considered the observed data along with the clinical pharmacology aspect of atogepant to make conclusion on the acceptability of proposed labeling statements.

L1743: In conclusion, we agree with the Applicant’s conclusion that the influence of age, renal impairment (mild and moderate), sex, race, and body weight on atogepant PK is not clinically relevant.
L1744: Exposure-Response Analysis
L1745: The Applicant conducted exposure-response modeling analysis. A total of 6,471 monthly migraine days observations from 1,668 subjects were used for model building. Individual atogepant steady-state exposures for exposure-response modeling analysis were predicted using the post hoc estimates from the final PopPK model. The change from baseline monthly migraine days over average atogepant concentration plot suggested a flat exposure-response relationship within the subjects exposed to atogepant (Figure 32). The migraine days data were well described by a zero-inflated Poisson model. Typical exposure metrics were simulated and the individual C_{max}, AUC, average concentration, and daily time above specified threshold levels were calculated for each patient in the Phase 2/3 study. These exposure summaries were used to test the exposure-response modeling in the efficacy models. The introduction of a treatment effect generally provided an improvement of the model. However, a model which described the treatment effect as being of equal magnitude, irrespective of atogepant exposure, performed better than any model that took individual exposure into account. The final model showed relatively good predictive capability to the observed efficacy data (Figure 33).

L1759: 14.4. Physiologically Based Pharmacokinetic Modeling Review
L1760: Executive Summary
L1761: The objective of this review is to evaluate the adequacy of the Applicant’s physiologically based pharmacokinetics (PBPK) report, entitled “Development of a mechanistic physiologically based pharmacokinetic (PBPK) model for atogepant and subsequent evaluation of drug-drug interaction potential as a victim of CYP3A4- and transporter-mediated interactions in healthy adult volunteers” for the following intended purposes.
L1762: • To predict the effect of a strong CYP3A inhibitor or a strong CYP3A inducer on the PK of atogepant if only considering CYP-mediated DDI in healthy subjects.• To predict the effect of a moderate CYP3A inhibitor (i.e., fluconazole) and a weak CYP3A inhibitor (i.e., cimetidine) on the PK of atogepant in healthy subjects.• To predict the PK of atogepant with and without complete knockout of P-gp or breast cancer resistance protein (BCRP) transporter function in healthy subjects.
L1763: The Division of Pharmacometrics has reviewed the submitted PBPK reports and supporting modeling files. FDA reviewers conducted additional PBPK analyses and concluded the following:
L1764: • The atogepant PBPK models were able to capture the observed atogepant PK following a single oral dose (50, 60, 100, and 170 mg) and multiple oral dose administration (QD: 50, 100, and 170 mg; BID: 30 and 60 mg) in healthy subjects.• The atogepant PBPK models are adequate to predict the effect of fluconazole and cimetidine on the PK of atogepant in healthy subjects. The model predicted a 69% and 8% increase in atogepant AUC with fluconazole (loading dose of 400 mg followed by 200 mg QD) and cimetidine (400 mg BID), respectively.• Atogepant exposure may be reduced by approximately 50% if co-administered with a strong CYP3A inducer but not an inducer of any transporter.• Atogepant exposure may be increased by approximately 140% if co-administered with a strong CYP3A inhibitor but not an inhibitor of any transporter.• The DDI potential of atogepant with a selective P-gp or BCRP inhibitor was low.
L1765: 14.4.1. Applicant's PBPK Modeling Effort
L1766: PBPK software
L1767: Simcyp V17 (Simcyp Ltd, United Kingdom) was used to develop the atogepant PBPK models and predict the PK of atogepant with and without CYP3A and transporter modulators.
L1768: Model development
L1769: Atogepant
L1770: The Advanced Dissolution Absorption and Metabolism model was used to model atogepant absorption. Atogepant was rapidly absorbed following administration of the tablet formulation with a time to maximum concentration of 1.00 to 1.50 hours under fasting conditions. Single- and multiple-dose PK data indicated that the AUC and C_{max} of atogepant increased in a roughly dose-proportional manner over the dose range of 1 to 170 mg in healthy subjects, suggesting solubility does not limit the absorption in vivo over this dose range. Thus, the Applicant assumed the drug is absorbed in the form of a solution. The atogepant jejunal passive permeability value of 2.85 \times 10^{-4} cm/s was derived from Caco-2 apparent permeability data. The kinetic parameters for intestinal P-gp and BCRP were obtained from transport study data in Caco-2 and MDCKII-BCRP cell lines. Relative activity factor values for intestinal P-gp and BCRP were optimized based on the clinical PK data (Study CGP-PK-12) and excretion data in feces (Study CGP-PK-03).
L1771: The fraction unbound in plasma was estimated to be 0.047 and applied in all simulations assuming albumin as the main binding protein. The blood to plasma ratio was 0.77. A full PBPK model was used to simulate the distribution phase of atogepant PK profile. A steady-state volume of distribution value of 0.306 L/kg with a ratio of total concentration of compound in the tissue to total concentration of compound in the plasma at steady-state scalar of 0.403 was optimized using clinical PK data (Study CGP-PK-12 control arm). Based on the in vitro immunoinhibition, chemical inhibition, and phenotyping study results, all metabolism of atogepant was assigned to CYP3A4 in the PBPK model. The hepatic intrinsic clearance value (22.8 L/hr) was optimized based on clinical PK data (Study CGP-PK-12). Atogepant was minimally excreted in urine, with only 5% of the total radioactivity excreted in urine as the unchanged parent drug. In feces, 42% of the dose was recovered as parent drug due to unabsorbed drug, intestinal secretion and biliary excretion or a combined effect of these processes. Renal clearance (1.11 L/h) was obtained from study CPG-PK-03.
L1772: OATP1B1/1B3, P-gp, and BCRP were incorporated within the “permeability-limited liver” model to simulate hepatic transport processes. The kinetic parameter values for hepatic P-gp and BCRP, and the intrinsic clearance value for OATP1B1/1B3-mediated active transport processes in the liver were defined based on the transport data in Caco-2 cells, MDCKII-BCRP and MDCKII-control cells, and MDCKII-OATP1B3 and MDCKII control cells, respectively. The relative expression factor values for hepatic OATP1B1, OATP1B3, P-gp, and BCRP were obtained from experimental data for transporter abundance in in vitro cell culture systems and liver tissue. Multiple transporter-related processes (gut secretion, hepatic uptake, and biliary secretion) with CYP3A4-mediated metabolism and renal clearance components incorporated in the model allowed recovery of the systemic PK and the amount of drug excreted unchanged in urine (4.84%) and feces (42%).
L1773: Victim Drug Models
L1774: The default PBPK models of itraconazole, rifampin, fluconazole, and cimetidine in Simcyp V17 were used for DDI prediction.
L1775: With respect to the interaction parameter values, the in vitro obtained itraconazole inhibitory constant (K_i) values for P-gp and BCRP (Kishimoto et al. 2014) were scaled down 15-fold and used in the simulations (itraconazole K_i values for P-gp and BCRP are 0.054 and 0.054 \mu\text{M}, respectively). The in vitro obtained hydroxyitraconazole K_i values for P-gp and BCRP (Lempers et al. 2016) were scaled down 37-fold and used in the simulations (hydroxyitraconazole K_i values for P-gp and BCRP are 0.135 and 0.324 \mu\text{M}, respectively). In addition, in vitro obtained hydroxyitraconazole K_i values for OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 (Lempers et al. 2016; Vermeer et al. 2016) were scaled down 15-fold and used in the simulations (hydroxyitraconazole K_i values for OATP1B1 and OATP1B3 are 0.016 and 0.007 \mu\text{M}, respectively).
L1776: 14.4.2. FDA's Assessment
L1777: FDA agreed with the Applicant's assessment and all metabolism of atogepant was assigned to CYP3A4 in the PBPK model. Given the renal clearance of 1.11 L/h, the contribution of CYP3A to the overall atogepant clearance is about 0.75. Using the default itraconazole PBPK model in Simcyp, the model predicted a 140% increase in atogepant exposure (AUC ratio [AUCR] is 2.43) without considering the effect of itraconazole on transporters. As the contribution of CYP3A to the total metabolism of atogepant has been confirmed using in vitro and in vivo data, the observed DDI effect with itraconazole (AUCR of 5.5) is likely partially contributed by nonCYP mediated DDI mechanisms. Since in vitro study showed that atogepant is an OATP1B1/1B3, P-gp, and BCRP substrate, the Applicant conducted additional DDI simulations by incorporating the inhibition potential of itraconazole on OATP1B1/1B3, P-gp, and BCRP as described in the method section. The simulated AUCR with itraconazole is consistent with the observed data as shown in Table 118. The reviewer agreed that the observed atogepant DDI effect with itraconazole might be contributed by both CYP and nonCYP-mediated DDI mechanisms.
L1778: Rifampin may cause DDIs via multiple mechanisms. In the PBPK analysis, the rifampin-mediated CYP3A4 competitive inhibition, P-gp and BCRP inhibition, and OATP1B1/1B3 inhibition effects were incorporated in the model to simulate the effect of a single dose of rifampin on the PK of atogepant. It was reported that the competitive inhibition of CYP3A4 by rifampin has minimal impact on the drug PK in vivo (Varma et al. 2013; Asaumi et al. 2019). The impact of P-gp inhibition by rifampin on atogepant PK was likely low given the clinically insignificant exposure change of atogepant with quinidine (1.25-fold, Study 3101-103-002). Additionally, the Applicant's worst-case scenario analysis showed that the DDI potential of atogepant with BCRP inhibitors was weak. Therefore, the observed exposure change of atogepant (AUCR 2.77) with a single dose of rifampin was most likely caused by the inhibition of hepatic uptake transporters OATP1B1/1B3 by rifampin.
L1779: To simulate the impact of multiple doses of rifampin on the PK of atogepant, the rifampin-mediated CYP3A4 induction, CYP3A4 competitive inhibition, and the P-gp induction effect were incorporated in the default rifampin model. Intestinal P-gp induction by rifampin (600 mg QD) was defined as a 3.5-fold increase in P-gp expression whereas the negligible induction effect of rifampin (600 mg QD) on hepatic P-gp was assumed (Greiner et al. 1999).
L1780: The rifampin-mediated OATP1B1/1B3 inhibition effect was not incorporated in the Applicant’s model. The reviewer simulated the effect of multiple doses of rifampin on the atogepant PK after incorporation of the rifampin-mediated OATP1B1/1B3 inhibition effect (observed in Study CGP-PK-12). The simulation results showed that the reduction in hepatic uptake via inhibition of OATP1B1 caused a significant increase in the atogepant AUC as compared to that without considering the rifampin-mediated OATP1B1/1B3 inhibition effect (AUCR 1.32 versus 0.44). After incorporation of the rifampin-mediated possible BCRP induction effect (assumed to be the same magnitude of induction as intestinal P-gp) in the model, the simulated AUCR (1.06) was still not able to match the observed AUCR (0.41). Thus, the induction potential of rifampin on OATP1B1/1B3 cannot be excluded and could explain, at least partially, the reduction in atogepant exposure. The combined effect of rifampin-mediated OATP1B1/1B3 inhibition and induction would not be expected to cause significant change in the systemic exposure of atogepant as evidenced by the similar simulated AUCR (0.44) in the scenario without considering rifampin-mediated OATP1B1/1B3 inhibition and induction effect to the observed AUCR (0.41) (Table 118).

L1785: 14.4.3. PBPK Model Application
L1786: The developed PBPK models were used to simulate the DDI for atogepant in the following scenarios:
L1787: To predict the effect of a strong CYP3A inhibitor or a strong CYP3A inducer on the PK of atogepant if only considering CYP-mediated DDI in healthy subjects.
L1788: • To predict the effect of a moderate CYP3A inhibitor (i.e., fluconazole) and a weak CYP3A inhibitor (i.e., cimetidine) on the PK of atogepant in healthy subjects.
• To predict the PK of atogepant with and without complete knockout of P-gp or BCRP transporter function in healthy subjects
L1789: Results
L1790: (1) Can atogepant PBPK models describe atogepant PK in healthy subjects?
L1791: Yes. The atogepant models were able to capture the observed atogepant PK following a single oral dose (50, 60, 100, and 170 mg) and multiple oral dose administration (50, 100, and 170 mg QD; 30 and 60 mg BID) of atogepant in healthy subjects (Figure 34 and Table 119). The atogepant AUC was overpredicted following administration of 50 and 100 mg, presumably due to the high variability in intestinal absorption.

L1805: (2) Can atogepant PBPK models predict the effect of a strong CYP3A inhibitor or a strong CYP3A inducer on the atogepant PK if only considering CYP-mediated DDI in healthy subjects?
L1806: Yes. The contribution of enzyme (CYP3A) and transporters (OATP1B1/1B3, P-gp, and BCRP) in the disposition of atogepant were quantified using clinical PK and DDI data and sensitivity analyses. The maximal contribution of CYP3A to the metabolism of atogepant has already been assigned in the model (the contribution of CYP3A to the total metabolism of atogepant is 100% and the contribution of CYP3A to the total clearance of atogepant is 75%). Thus, the possibility of underestimation of atogepant DDI with a strong CYP3A4 inhibitor or a strong CYP3A4 inducer may be lower. Please refer to Section 14.4.2 for details.
L1807: The model predicted an approximately 140% increase (AUC ratio of 2.43) in atogepant exposure after multiple doses of itraconazole if only considering the CYP3A-mediated DDI effect in healthy subjects. The model predicted an approximately 50% reduction (AUCR of 0.63) in atogepant exposure after multiple doses of rifampin if only considering the rifampin-mediated CYP3A4 induction effect in healthy subjects (Table 118).
L1808: (3) Can atogepant PBPK models predict the effect of fluconazole (a moderate CYP3A inhibitor) and cimetidine (a weak CYP3A inhibitor) on the PK of atogepant in healthy subjects?
L1809: Yes. The atogepant models are adequate to predict the effect of fluconazole and cimetidine on the PK of atogepant in healthy subjects. About 69% increase in atogepant AUC was predicted with fluconazole (loading dose of 400 mg followed by 200 mg QD), whereas the increase in atogepant AUC(8.0%) with cimetidine (400 mg BID) was deemed clinically insignificant.
L1810: (4) Can atogepant PBPK models predict the PK of atogepant with or without complete knockout of P-gp or BCRP transporter function in healthy subjects?
L1811: Yes. The model simulated atogepant exposure was increased about 20% if completely knocked out of the intestinal and hepatic P-gp or intestinal and hepatic BCRP-mediated transport processes, indicating low DDI potential of atogepant with a selective P-gp or BCRP inhibitor. The prediction was consistent with the clinical finding that the atogepant AUC increased about 25% if coadministered with quinidine (a P-gp inhibitor) (Study 3101-103-002).

L1820: 16. Efficacy: Additional Information and Assessment
L1821: 16.1. Sensitivity Analyses for Study CGP-MD-01
L1822: A pattern-mixture model based on nonignorable missing (i.e., missing not at random) was conducted to assess the robustness of the primary mixed-effects model for repeated measures analysis to the possible violation of the missing at random assumption. In the pattern-mixture model, the placebo group was used as reference to impute missing values. Each imputed dataset is analyzed using analysis of covariance with treatment group as a factor and baseline value as a covariate and the estimates and p-values were obtained from combining all the results from each individual analysis. The result of the pattern-mixture model is consistent with the primary analysis (Table 121). As shown in Figure 35, the change in the number of migraine days after the first month has similar trends in the placebo and atogepant groups. Therefore, imputing missing data for the atogepant groups based on the pattern of the placebo group yielded a similar result as the mixed-effects model for repeated measures analysis under missing at random assumption.

L1837: In study CGP-MD-01, it was required that a minimum of 12 days’ diary data during each postbaseline 4-week treatment period be evaluable for that particular period. However, the requirement was updated to “14 days” in study 3101-301-002 as requested by FDA during the End of Phase 2 meeting. Therefore, the statistical reviewer conducted an additional analysis using the “14 days” requirement for study CGP-MD-01. The estimated treatment effects were similar to the primary analysis, although the p-value after adjusting for multiplicity is slightly larger than 0.05 for the atogepant 60 mg QD group, and the atogepant 30 mg QD group (Table 123). The analysis using the evaluable requirement of “12 days” is considered as the primary analysis as that was consistently specified in all versions of the Statistical Analysis Plan.

L1852: 16.3. Subgroup Analyses for Study CGP-MD-01
L1853: Atogepant groups showed consistent reductions from baseline in monthly migraine days when compared with placebo across all subgroups of age, sex, and race, except for groups of small sample size. The treatment effect appears to be larger for patients with prior exposure to a migraine preventive medication.

L1894: 22. Postmarketing Requirements and Commitments
L1895: (1) A juvenile animal toxicology study of atogepant in rats.(2) A randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled efficacy and safety study under the Pediatric Research Equity Act for the preventive treatment of episodic migraine in children 6 through 17 years of age. This efficacy study must be designed to show superiority of atogepant over placebo and is to be submitted as a special protocol assessment (SPA). A pharmacokinetics evaluation needs to be conducted in children 6 through 11 years of age to determine the appropriate dosing in the efficacy portion of the study in this age group.(3) A study under the Pediatric Research Equity Act to evaluate the long-term safety of preventive treatment of episodic migraine in patients 6 through 17 years of age. This study should be a minimum of one year in length.(4) A prospective pregnancy exposure registry cohort analyses in the United States that compare the maternal, fetal, and infant outcomes of women with migraine exposed to atogepant during pregnancy with two unexposed control populations: one consisting of women with migraine who have not been exposed to atogepant before and during pregnancy and the other consisting of women without migraine. The registry will identify and record pregnancy complications, major and minor congenital malformations, spontaneous abortions, stillbirths, elective terminations, preterm births, small-for-gestational-age births, and any other adverse outcomes, including postnatal growth and development. Outcomes will be assessed throughout pregnancy. Infant outcomes, including effects on postnatal growth and development, will be assessed through at least the first year of life.(5) A pregnancy outcomes study using a different study design than provided for in the pregnancy registry postmarketing requirement above (for example, a retrospective cohort study using claims or electronic medical record data with outcome validation or a case control study) to assess major congenital malformations, spontaneous abortions, stillbirths, preterm births, and small-for-gestational-age births in women exposed to atogepant during pregnancy compared to an unexposed control population.(6) A dose range-finding study of orally administered atogepant in female CD-1 mouse.(7) A 2-year carcinogenicity study of orally administered Qulipta in female mouse.
L1896: Enhanced pharmacovigilance is recommended for liver toxicity, cardiovascular (including hypertension), cerebrovascular, and serious gastrointestinal events.

L1900: There were three investigators in CGP-MD-01 who did not provide financial disclosure forms. One site subsequently submitted the required forms. Two other sites did not submit forms, but only two subjects were enrolled in the study during the time these investigators were active in the study. The number of patients enrolled by these sites was minimal compared with the overall number of patients in the study. Removing these sites did not affect the p-value on the primary endpoint.

L1903: There were 54 sites noted to not have financial disclosure documents for some of the staff (associate investigators and/or site coordinators). L1971: Division of Hepatology and NutritionDILI Team Consultation
L1972: NDA215206

Consultation IssueDrug-induced liver injury (DILI)

DrugAtogepant (calcitonin gene related peptide receptor antagonist)

IndicationMigraine headache prevention

ApplicantAbbVie, Inc

Requesting DivisionDivision of Neurology 2Daniel Ngembus, RPMViveca Livezey, MDHeather Fitter, Clinical Team Lead

Primary ReviewerPaul H. Hayashi, MD, MPHDILI Team Lead, OND/DHN

Office of PharmacoepidemiologyAssociate Director, OPE/OSE

Signatory AuthorityJoseph Toerner, MD, MPHDirector, OND/DHN

Assessment DateAug 25, 2021
L1973: Context:
L1974: Atogepant is an oral small molecule antagonist of the calcitonin gene related peptide (CGRP) receptor. It is under NDA review for the prevention of migraine headaches in adults with more than 15 migraine days per month. Other agents in this class have had problems with drug-induced liver injury (DILI). We are asked by the Division of Neurology 2 (DN2) to review “patients with abnormal LFTs for potential liver toxicity” associated with Atogepant with particular attention to a severe adverse event that resulted in fatality. We are asked to give opinion on whether atogepant “carries a risk of potential liver toxicity and, if so, how this may inform labeling.”
L1975: Executive Summary:
L1976: We do not see significant liver toxicity that threatens approval of this drug. There were no cases that met Hy’s Law criteria in the registration trials in which over 1300 patients were given Atogepant. Patients were treated for 12 weeks in the placebo-controlled trials and up to 52 weeks in the long-term open label studies. There was no significant imbalance in transaminase elevations between placebo and treatment arms. The fatal case of particular concern did not involve DILI (see Section 4.6.2). Nevertheless, other agents in this class have had significant liver injury issues in development, and there were 5 cases of probable DILI without jaundice overall. All cases did well with holding the Atogepant. We do not recommend monitoring for liver injury with blood tests, but we suggest discussing potential for liver injury in the label because rare cases of severe liver injury could arise in the larger post-market population. Avoidance of use in patients with severe hepatic impairment should be considered. We have full recommendations including comments for labeling in Section 5.0

L1995: 1.0 Rationale:
L1996: 1.1 Disease: Migraine headaches affect over 1 billion people worldwide with substantial morbidity and disability. Persons aged 25 to 35 carry the highest incidence rates with more women than men by a 2:1 ratio (23 versus 10 per 1000 person-years).1,2 Theories on pathophysiology have moved away from the idea that vascular constriction leads to migraines. Nowadays, theory on migraine pathophysiology is based on cortical spreading by a propagating wave of neuronal and glial depolarization (cortical spreading depression of Leao).3 This wave helps to explain the migraine aura. This wave of depolarization reaches the trigeminal vascular system which is felt to be central to the subsequent pain and other manifestations of a migraine attack.
L1997: Stimulation of the trigeminal system leads to release of several neuropeptides including calcitonin gene related peptide (CGRP). These peptides then lead to neurogenic inflammation by vasodilation and protein extravasation. CGRP and its receptor are widely expressed in the peripheral and central nervous systems. CGRP may also play a role in pain transmission. It is increased in cerebral spinal fluid and cranial circulation during migraine headaches and can trigger migraine attacks.
L1998: 1 Ashina M, et al. Lancet 397:1485-95 (2021)
L1999: 2 Lyngberg AC, et al. Am J Epidemiol 161:1066-73 (2005)
L2000: 3 Cutrer, FM. Pathophysiology, clinical manifestations, and diagnosis of migraine in adults. UpToDate.com (accessed May 14, 2021)
L2001: 1.2 Mechanism of action: Because of CGRP's significant role, it became a primary therapeutic target, and blocking of the CGRP receptor has been shown to prevent migraine headaches. Both small molecules and monoclonal antibodies aimed at blocking the CGRP receptor are approved or in development. Atogepant (AGPT) is a small molecule antagonist similar to two agents approved for migraine prevention.1.3 CGRP receptor antagonists and DILI risk: Telcagepant is in the same class as AGPT. Telcagepant development was stopped by another sponsor due to liver injury signals in Phase III studies, primarily when given daily for more than 7 days.4 Elevated transaminases occurred at a rate too high and severe to warrant further development. Similarly, development of another agent, MK-3207 was also stopped due to liver injury concerns. The mechanism of liver injury for these agents is postulated to be a combination of bile acid accumulation (transport inhibition) and oxidative stress (reactive metabolite formation), and less so from mitochondrial toxicity.5 In vitro and in silico testing have led to development of other CGRP receptor antagonists with less hepatotoxicity. Since the abandonment of telcagepant and MK-3207, several agents in the same class have successfully made it to market without significant liver injury concerns.
L2002: Telcagepant structure (Figure 1):
L2003: Figure 1The chemical structure of Telcagepant is shown. It features a central piperazine ring connected via amide linkages to a 1,2,3,4-tetrahydroisoquinoline-3-carboxamide group on the left and a 4,4-difluorobutyl group on the right. The 1,2,3,4-tetrahydroisoquinoline-3-carboxamide group is further substituted with a 2-fluorophenyl ring.Chemical structure of Telcagepant
L2004: 1.3.1 FDA approved oral, small molecule CGRP antagonists (year of approval): rimegepant (2020) and ubrogepant (2019)
1.3.2 FDA approved parenteral, monoclonal antibody CGRP antagonists: Eptinezumab (IV), Galcanezumab (SC), Fremanezumab (SC), Erenumab (SC)

L2006: 2.1 Chemical structure of MK-8031 (now Atogepant) and metabolites (Figure 2):

L2008: 5 Smith, B, et al. Toxicological Sciences, 177:84-93 (2020)
L2009: Figure 2
L2010: Proposed Metabolites of MK-8031
L2011: The diagram illustrates the metabolic pathways of MK-8031. The parent compound, MK-8031, is shown at the top center. Metabolite M1 (L-005039327) is formed by the loss of a fluorine atom. Metabolite M2 (L-005041565) is formed by the addition of an oxygen atom. Other metabolites are grouped by their formation from specific precursors: M3, M4, and M5 from M1 via +3O; M6, M7, and M9 from M1 via +2O; M8 from M1 via +O; M10 from M1 via +2O+2H; M11 and M18 from M1 via +O; M12 and M19 from M1 via +O+gluc; and M13 from M1 via +2O+CH2+gluc.Chemical structures of MK-8031 and its proposed metabolites M1 through M13. The diagram shows the parent compound MK-8031 and its various metabolic pathways, including oxidation, glucuronidation, and hydrolysis to form different metabolite groups.
L2012: 2.2 AGPT is absorbed quickly (median T_{max} = 1-2 hrs). Volume of distribution is estimated at 73 to 86.1 L. It is metabolized via CYP3A4 primarily. It is predominantly (81%) hepatically eliminated via metabolites in bile and feces. Renal clearance is minor (&lt;10%). AGPT is a substrate for P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1.
L2013: 2.3 Hepatic impairment studies: C_{max} is +9, -12% and -4% for mild, moderate and severe impairment compared to normal function (Table 1, Figure 3). However, AUCs were higher in “subjects with hepatic impairment” (14-38% increase). While AUCs were similar between mild and moderate impairment, the highest AUCs at 0-4 hours and overall were for severe impairment (38% increase compared to normal) (Table 1).

L2020: 3.0 Non-clinical data related to DILI:
L2021: 3.1 Animal studies: The sponsor did rat, mouse and monkey studies for 3 months (100 mg/kg/d), 6 months (200 mg/kg/d) and 9 months (300 mg/kg/d), respectively. No liver injury was found. Three-month and 9-month necropsy studies in Rhesus monkeys did not show any liver abnormalities.
3.2 In vitro testing: The sponsor did bile acid transporter inhibition, oxidative stress and mitochondrial dysfunction studies for AGPT, telcagepant and MK-3207. AGPT is an inhibitor of OCT1 and MATE1 but not BCRP. There is no mention of BSEP inhibition. AGPT is oxidized predominantly by CY3A4 and inhibits it as well. There is no mention of mitochondrial toxicity.
3.3 Quantitative systems toxicology (DILISym®) studies suggest a “reduced risk of hepatotoxicity” for AGPT compared to telcagepant based on in vitro studies outlined. (Seq 001, 4.2.3.7.3). No ALT elevations were predicted at the 60 mg BID dosing. Mild elevations were predicted at 120 mg BID and “more significant” elevations at 180 mg BID.

4.1.3 Dosing of AGPT ranged from 10mg daily to 60 mg twice daily.


4.2 Total Exposed numbers across all trials:

L2041: 4.6 Case level analyses: There were 29 patients on AGPT (13 from Group 1; 16 from Group 2) who had transaminases (ALT or AST) greater than 3x ULN
L2042: during the study. All 29 had narratives and were assessed. No cases met transaminase, ALP and bilirubin criteria for potential Hy's Law. Twenty-three cases were assessed as unlikely DILI due to AGPT. The most common alternative diagnosis amongst these 23 was myopathy or myositis (9 cases). Three cases had elevations in transaminases related to infection (e.g. EBV, enteritis). Four had elevations more likely due to another drug or herbal product. Autoimmune hepatitis was assessed more likely in 2 patients. No identifiable alternative was found for 5, but DILI due to AGPT was still felt unlikely for other reasons (e.g. latency, dechallenge while still on AGPT). Therefore, only 6 of the 29 were considered at least possible DILI due to AGPT.

L2046: We describe 1 unlikely, 2 probable and 1 possible case in detail.
L2047: 4.6.2 Case (b) (6) (Study 3101-302-002): Unlikely DILI due to AGPT. We discuss this case because the patient died, and the consulting Division specifically requested that we assess this case in detail.
L2048: Summary: This is a 26-year-old black woman who developed elevated transaminases without jaundice 4 weeks after drug start (Atogepant 60mg QD)
L2049: Her only baseline medication was ibuprofen. There is no mention of underlying liver disease. Liver tests were normal at baseline but CPK was mildly up at 213 U/L. She started study drug on (b) (6) and started sertraline 50 mg/d one day later. She had a flu-like illness (b) (6) taking 2 over the counter medications that contained acetaminophen (APAP). She stayed on study drug.
L2050: Elevated liver tests were noted on routine follow-up (b) (6) (ALT 141, AST 491, normal ALP and bilirubin), but her CPK was also elevated at 28,210 U/L. Study drug was stopped. She had been exercising with a
L2051: trainer but was asymptomatic. On (b) (6), her CPK and liver tests were higher (ALT 205, AST 677, CPK = 38,607), but ALP and bilirubin remained normal. She was advised to go to the ER, but she did not go until (b) (6) at which time the CPK was down to the 400s. ALT and AST had fallen by 50%. These are the last set of labs available.
L2052: She still felt well through (b) (6), but in the early morning of (b) (6) she had chest pain and SOB. She was being taken by ambulance to the ER, when she had a cardiac arrest. CPR was in progress on arrival. ACLS measures recovered vital signs, but she had another cardiac arrest the same morning from which she did not recover. No labs available after (b) (6). No mention of fever or elevated WBC.
L2053: Autopsy diagnosed Group A beta hemolytic strep in the liver, blood and spleen. No primary source. Toxicology showed only doxylamine (antihistamine) and dextromethorphan. No study drug detected. No illicit drugs or alcohol mentioned.
L2054: Assessment: This is unlikely DILI. Even before the cardiac arrest, myositis or myopathy is a better explanation for the elevated AST and ALT. CPK was over 25,000. Myopathy may have been related to sertraline which has been reported with this drug. It was started at 50 mg/d, one day after study drug start. There is no mention of sertraline thereafter, but gradual dose increase is typical. An increasing dose may have played a role in her myopathy. The shock and death are unrelated. Her CPK and liver enzymes had fallen by &gt;50% before the onset of shock (Figure 6).

TBL (solid line with crosses) remains low, around 0.2. Vertical lines mark 'First Dose' at day 0 and 'Last Dose' at day 23.
L2056: 4.6.3 Case (b) (6) (Study 3101-301-002): Probable DILI due to AGPT
L2057: Summary: This is a 39-year-old white woman who had elevated transaminases without jaundice 3 weeks after starting Atogepant 30mg/d.
L2058: At baseline, she was on an acetaminophen (APAP) medication, but AST, ALT and CPK were normal at baseline. She was asymptomatic when elevated liver enzymes were noted on routine follow-up (ALT 249, AST 107, ALP 169, Bilirubin 0.2). Although, she did not meet interruption criteria, study drug was held from (b) (6) (day after onset) to (b) (6). While study drug was held, her liver tests returned to normal within 9 days.
L2059: However, on (b) (6) 18 days after restarting study drug, her ALT and AST rose again (ALT 96, AST 55). ALP remained normal on this second rise. She remained asymptomatic throughout. Study drug was stopped on that day (b) (6). She had one more follow-up set of labs 15 days later and ALT &amp; AST were normal again at 15 and 14. She was last lost to follow-up thereafter.
L2060: HAV "antibody", HBV surface antibody and HCV antibody negative. EBV antibody also negative. No other evaluation noted. No mention of new medications or herbal/dietary supplements.
L2061: Assessment: This appears to be a DILI due to the study drug. Though evaluation testing was sparse, this appears to be a positive re-challenge (Figure 7).

L2063: 4.6.4 Case (b) (6) (Study CGP-MD-01): Probable DILI due to AGPT
L2064: Summary: This is a 39 year-old white woman who developed elevated transaminases w/o jaundice 12 days after starting Atogepant 60mg QD.
L2065: No mention of pre-existing liver disease. She drinks one alcoholic beverage per week. Liver tests were normal at Day 0, but they had climbed during screening. She had no symptoms when ALT and AST rose on Day 12. No ALP or bilirubin rise. Drug was stopped 5 days after DILI onset. Viral hepatitis serology testing was "negative". Autoimmune hepatitis (AIH) markers negative. Ultrasound (US) unrevealing.
L2066: Patient was taking Excedrin Migraine "frequently" (every other day) but precise dosing was not provided. This over the counter was continued through the washout after stopping study drug.
L2067: Assessment: This case is a weak probable DILI due to the study drug. APAP (acetaminophen) use competes some, but use continued through the washout. It was also part of her baseline medications. Unclear if APAP dosing changed. The sponsor suggests NAFLD, but that disease would not present with such an acute rise and fall of ALT (peak of &gt;9x ULN). Sponsor notes inadvertent double dosing of IP, but this occurred the day of DILI onset when ALT was already &gt;300. The main argument against DILI is that her ALT and AST had increased by 2.1x and 1.67x her baseline even before drug start, so something else may have been brewing (Figure 8). No other prior baseline liver enzyme data provided.

L2070: 4.6.5 Case (b) (6) (Study 3101-302-002): Possible DILI due to AGPT
L2071: Summary: This is a 56-year-old white woman who developed elevated transaminases without jaundice 24 weeks after drug start (Atogepant 60mg QD)
L2072: On acetaminophen (APAP) at baseline. ALP mildly elevated at baseline. Other LFTs and CPK normal. She developed asymptomatic elevation in transaminases 24 weeks after study drug start, which peaked a week later (ALT 177, AST 83). Evaluation showed a (+) CMV IgM. She had a flu-like illness but this came on after injury onset. Estrogen and rosuvastatin was started just prior to injury but these were held and washout did not occur. Study drug continued and her liver enzymes did decline by 50% from peak. However, they stayed elevated over baseline for 22-27 weeks while on drug. They were gradually declining but then returned to normal 9 weeks after stopping study drug.
L2073: Other viral serologies negative. ANA was (+) at 1:160. Malar rash and chelosis mentioned. No immunosuppression treatments mentioned. No liver biopsy. Imaging negative.
L2074: Assessment: This case is of some interest because it may be a case of chronic DILI while on AGPT with partial or slow adaptation (Figure 9). Autoimmune hepatitis (AIH) competes but injury did resolve without immunosuppression and after drug stop. Latency is long for DILI at 24 weeks compared to the 2 probable DILI cases outlined above.
